**An embedding is a cache, not primary data.** The cross-cutting requirement states that the system "must be able to regenerate them", and wishing does not make it so: a vector, in isolation, cannot be recomputed in another space. The rule that makes it mechanically true is one of persistence, and it governs **every** embedding the network keeps beyond a single event:

* **The triple `{source_text, model_id, vector}` is persisted,** where the vector is a pure function of the source text and the `model_id`. It applies to a blueprint's capability embedding (`capability_text`, §3.13.3) and to that of a Topic Registry topic (§3.7.6). Regenerating under another model stops being a data migration and becomes recomputing a cache from an input that was never lost.
* **The source text cannot be re-derived from the other fields, which is why it is stored explicitly instead of being reconstructed.** It is not always a description of what the vector represents: that of a topic is the text of the task that originated it (§3.13.2), which no other field preserves. Regenerating from a newly chosen text, instead of the original, would move the vector without anything recording it.
* **The triple stays consistent or it means nothing.** Editing the text without recomputing the vector leaves the node competing with a semantics that is no longer the one it declares, and nothing external would detect it: the `model_id` would match. Text and vector are written together, in the same operation that provisions or updates the blueprint.

**And a rule of use, not only of persistence: no vector is ever compared against another unless both `model_id` values match and are the current one.** The cross-cutting requirement says the `model_id` "is what makes the mix detectable", but detectable is not detected: stating the property obliges no one to exercise it. The Task Broker exercises it—it discards the proposal whose `model_id` does not match before recomputing anything (§3.7.5, step 4)—and that check is not a special case of the award path but the most visible instance of a rule that governs everywhere. Where a comparison between different spaces is not rejected, it returns a plausible number nobody validates, and the damage is proportional to what that comparison decides. §3.13.3 instruments the rule for the other place where a comparison decides something: the neighborhood searches with which the Spawner decides coverage.

This rule is also what lets a blueprint cross a network's boundary without dragging along the vector space of its network of origin (§3.13.6).

#### 3.7.4. The task embedding

The task embedding is a self-selection tool: it lets each node, without knowing the rest of the network, decide whether it should propose for a piece of work. It is not a global ranking mechanism ("am I the best candidate?") but a local filter ("is this within my competence?"). Its generation rules derive from that purpose:

* **Who generates it:** it is not the exclusive attribution of any role (§2.1)—*the node that publishes the `task.announced`* generates it, whatever that role is. That includes the Entry Node (User-Proxy) publishing the root request, or any worker that decomposes its work into subtasks and announces them as new `task.announced` events.
  * **Which half of the node:** the **Integration Sidecar**, not the Logic Container (§3.6.1). The Logic Container decides *which* task to announce and with what text; the Integration Sidecar derives the vector from that text when publishing. That is what lets a deterministic node (§3.6.3) announce subtasks without a client for any model, and what keeps any blueprint from carrying a dependency on the embedding model.
  * **Under which space:** the **current** `embedding_model` (§2.3), read by the Integration Sidecar at publishing time—never the one pinned by `config_version` in the envelope. The distinction is deliberate, and §2.3 argues it when classifying that parameter. The Integration Sidecar prepends to the task text the prefix that the convention of that space reserves for the querying side (§3.7.3), and the `model_id` of the space it generated under travels in `data` next to the vector (§3.8.1), which is what lets the Task Broker detect the mix (§3.7.5, step 4).
* **Which text:** the announcement's text decides who competes, because from it comes the vector against which each node measures its floor (§3.7.5). It describes the work requested, at the granularity at which the emitter wants competition, and the material being worked on goes to the Context Store by Claim-Check (§3.4). An emitter that announces a closed set of jobs on a topic can use a fixed text per job and declare them in its blueprint (§3.13.6): the vector stops depending on the material, a text written by a third party does not shift who claims the task, and a bundle's closure can check that the claimant wins the announcement.
* **When it applies:** the embedding only makes sense in open broadcast events (`task.announced`, the CNP `cfp`), because they are the only ones that require multiple nodes unknown to each other to select themselves. An event addressed to a specific node—`task.assigned` and `task.rejected` toward whoever proposed, `task.completed` and `task.failed` toward the requester that opened the task—needs no `task_embedding`: the recipient is already resolved, by the proposal in one case and by the announcement's `requester_identity` in the other (§3.8.1), and there is nothing to match.
* **Regenerating vs. reusing:** the rule is whether the semantic content of the task changed, not whether the event changed.
  * **It is regenerated** when the re-publication implies a respecification: for example, a `task.failed` that leads to reopening the task with a more precise description (including the traceback or the cause of the failure)—whoever emits that new `task.announced` generates a new embedding, because the text describing the task is no longer the same.
  * **It is reused** when the re-publication does not change the task's description, only its declarative metadata: the typical case is a re-announcement that only increments `current_attempts` (§3.3) because the request itself is identical and the only thing that changed is how many times it was attempted. It is still a `task.announced` like any other—what decides is whether the semantic content changed, not the event type—and it is already covered by "when it applies". Reusing the original embedding avoids an unnecessary regeneration, in the same spirit as reviving nodes to reuse their embedding (§3.7.1). An embedding whose `model_id` is not the current `embedding_model` is never reused: it is regenerated even if the text is the same (§3.7.3).
* The embedding travels in the event payload (a lightweight vector), together with the `TraceID` and the Claim-Check reference to the heavy context.

#### 3.7.5. Decision flow
1. The node listens to its topic constantly, waiting for `task.announced` (the CNP `cfp`, see §3.8).
2. It computes `cosine_similarity(agent_embedding, task_embedding)`.
3. **Similarity floor (local filter), and only if the node is free:** the Integration Sidecar publishes `task.proposed` only when the node has no task in progress (§3.6.1, capacity rule, which does not govern a `hitl_contact_*` channel) **and** the similarity exceeds its credential's `similarity_floor` (§3.13.3, §3.14.2). The Integration Sidecar evaluates this itself, since it already holds that credential, the signed embedding it carries inside and the node's busy state (§3.6.1). For either of those causes the node does not propose—and there lies the distinction this step upholds: "no one is an appropriate candidate" versus **"no one decided to compete"**, which covers both the node that does not reach the floor and the one that is busy. Both leave the task legitimately vacant in step 5, and the one that separates them afterwards is the Spawner looking at the active fleet (§3.13.2), not the Task Broker. When the announcement passes, the Integration Sidecar publishes the proposal with the similarity it computed, without consulting the Logic Container (§3.6.1); the computation is a traffic filter, not the award criterion.
4. **Recomputation and award (authority):** the Task Broker does not award by the number the node declares. For each `task.proposed` it recomputes `s_calc = cosine_similarity(signed_embedding, task_embedding)`—the embedding comes from the credential signed by the Spawner (§3.14.2), the `task_embedding` from the task state the Task Broker already holds (§3.1)—and awards by `s_effective = min(s_reported, s_calc)`. The signature thus acts as a ceiling: the similarity a proposal declares can lower the one its credential allows, never raise it. The Task Broker applies that same credential's `similarity_floor` to `s_effective` (the node's check is an optimization, not the guarantee) and grants the task to the highest through `task.assigned` (atomic operation); it answers the rest with `task.rejected`.
   * **Each proposal is judged against its own floor, and the floor decides admissibility and never order.** Nodes with different floors thus compete in the same auction without the Task Broker resolving any floor against the configuration or checking one against another, and a wide floor beats no one: it only admits its node to tasks where one with a narrow floor would have stayed silent. Hence a broad-coverage node loses against any specialist in its region and wins where there is none, with no dedicated rule.
   * For both ends to give the same number, embeddings are stored and signed **normalized** (the cosine reduces to a dot product) and the comparison against the floor is done with rounding to `similarity_precision_decimals` (§2.3). Without that, a last-bit difference decides arbitrarily at the edge of the floor.
   * **"The highest" is evaluated over the rounded `s_effective`, and so there can be more than one.** Rounding is not only for the floor: if the network declares that beyond a certain decimal it does not distinguish two similarities, it cannot afterwards assert that at higher resolution one beats the other—it would be exercising a precision it declared itself not to have. The set of proposals that tie at that rounded value is then a legitimate equivalence class, and **the Task Broker chooses within it by draw**. Arrival order does not work: it is the variable that has least to do with the competition and correlates most with deployment details.
     * **The draw is deterministic, seeded by `TaskID`:** the highest `hash(TaskID, node_id)` of the tied set wins. It is the only way to have these properties at once—over many tasks the work is spread uniformly among the tied nodes, and yet the award **remains recomputable from the trace**, which is what §3.1 asks of the Task Broker and what a `random()` would break. It adds no round trip or state: it is a pure function of the set of proposals the Task Broker already has at hand when it awards, so the operation remains atomic.
     * **Rounding is applied after the `min`,** on `s_effective` and not on `s_calc`. The other way round, a proposal would enter the tie by a declared value higher than the recomputed one, and the ceiling would stop being one.
     * **This is what keeps a tie from always going to the same node,** and the blue/green of §3.13.4 depends on it: a candidate provisioned on the niche of a node that already covers it might never receive a task, never reach `min_observations` and leave the replacement without converging. The Task Broker still does not know a replacement is in progress (there is no dedicated rule and no exception by node type); what there is, is a general award rule that, incidentally, stops being biased. The most frequent live case is that of a revived blueprint, whose triple comes back intact from the Catalog (§3.13.3) and therefore ties exactly with its neighbor's.
   * The Task Broker discards the proposal if the credential's `model_id` does not match that of the `task_embedding`: they are vectors from different spaces and their cosine means nothing. Detecting it is its responsibility, because it is the one that recomputes.
   * The Task Broker also verifies that the credential's subject is the platform identity the deployment attributes to the event (§3.14.1). Since the credential is static and travels in every proposal, without that check a node could replay another node's better-matched credential. For the same reason it can cache the credential by identity and not re-verify the signature on every proposal.
   * **Exception: structural components do not go through this step.** The rule, in its general form:

     > Given a `task.proposed` published by a **structural component** (§2.1), the Task Broker verifies the signature and the correspondence with the platform identity that published the event; **it does not recompute similarity, does not apply a floor and does not discard for a mismatched `model_id`**, and it ranks the proposal by the `s_reported` it declares.

     The rule applies when the candidates are interchangeable (there is nothing the similarity recomputation could rank among them) and, at the same time, the proposer's credential does not depend on any internal component of the network, so a change of the embedding model (§2.3) cannot invalidate it. These paths meet both conditions:

     * **The `hitl_contact_*` family** (§3.5.3): the topic names the audience, and every channel subscribed to it serves it by definition, so there is no competition to rank—all candidates are equally competent, and the blueprint's `attempt_window` (§3.5.2) and the atomic award already settle the choice among them. On these topics the Task Broker verifies the signature, the correspondence with the platform identity that published the event and, in an emergent channel, the operator's proof against the deployment root (§3.14.2): that is the real admission barrier. It admits only channel credentials of that audience—the deployment's that serves it, or an emergent one with a proof—so the Spawner's sentinel proposal does not get in here: the Spawner closes a vacant query without taking it (§3.13.2). The `task_embedding` still travels in the announcement (§3.7.4), but it takes no part in admission. The `hitl_contact_operator` credential in particular comes provisioned with the deployment and nothing inside the network can re-issue it (§3.14.2), so if the Task Broker recomputed similarity here, a model change would leave discarded for a mismatched `model_id` precisely the proposals of the channel that authorizes that very change.
     * **The fixed-convention topics with a structural claimant** (§3.7.7): `config_change`, `trace_query`, `node_review`, `node_query`, `node_catalog_query`, `node_catalog_publish`, `node_catalog_import`, `node_provision`, `node_create`, `node_retire` and `topic_catalog_query`. In none of them is there competition to rank—either the owner is unique by deterministic routing, or the candidates are interchangeable because they answer by reading a shared store—and in all of them the credential comes provisioned with the deployment instead of being signed by the Spawner. Without this rule, a change of `embedding_model` (§2.3) would leave the Task Broker discarding the proposals of the Config Store, the Tracing Collector and the Spawner precisely in the window where administering the network, querying its history and repairing the vector space (§3.13.3) is the only thing that has to keep working.
     * **The Spawner's sentinel proposal on a vacant emergent topic** (§3.13.2): it is the only structural path that does not go through a fixed-convention topic, and that is why a rule stated per topic would not cover it. Here the effect would be permanent, not transient: the Spawner's credential, like that of every structural component, comes provisioned with the deployment and nobody inside can re-issue it (§3.14.2), so its `model_id` stays fixed forever. Once the model changed a single time, the Task Broker would discard all its proposals and the Spawner would never win a vacancy again—bringing down with it node creation, the cold population of `intake` (§5.1) and the very repair the model change requires.

     **How the Task Broker knows the proposer is structural.** The distinction is cryptographic: both credentials verify against the same root of trust, and what separates them is the depth of the chain (§3.14.2). A structural component's credential comes provisioned with the deployment and verifies directly against the root; an emergent node's does so through an intermediate link—the `spawner` role certificate of whoever signed it—which is precisely what marks it as emergent. The Task Broker does not choose which key to verify against or consult any table: it verifies a chain and reads its shape. An emergent node trying to pass itself off as structural would need a credential signed by a root that nothing inside the network can issue—the same property that ensures a compromised Spawner cannot mint operator authority for itself. The envelope's `source` takes no part in this decision.

     **The predicate evaluates the class of the proposer, not a specific identity, meeting the constraint of §3.13.1 and §3.13.2 against rules dedicated to the Spawner.** It applies equally to all the paths above, and the Spawner's sentinel is covered as one more case, with no logic of its own: it proposes declaring the lowest possible similarity, and with `s_calc` absent `s_effective = min(s_reported, s_calc)` is still that minimum, so the usual rule—the highest wins—still automatically prefers any legitimate candidate that shows up in the same round. A structural component declaring itself high buys nothing—its credential is of verified provenance, and overbidding would only make it win tasks it then fails—and the `model_id` on this path never decided anything: it could only break it.

     **The dividing line is the claimant, not the topic.** `intake` is also fixed-convention, but an emergent role claims it (§3.7.7): there similarity does decide among candidates that are not interchangeable, and step 4 runs in full. A fixed topic is not exempt for being fixed; it is exempt for having nothing to rank—and, symmetrically, an emergent topic does not require ranking when the proposer is the structural component that exists precisely to cover what nobody covered.
5. **Vacancy:** if no proposal exceeds its floor within the auction timeout (`auction_timeout`, Global Configuration, §2.3), the task returns to `UNASSIGNED` and the Task Broker re-announces it with backoff (§3.1). The Spawner, which observes this situation passively, decides when and how to intervene—see §3.13.2 for the full mechanism.
   * On `hitl_contact_*`, where there is no floor to exceed, vacancy simply means that **nobody proposed** within the timeout—and since a channel does not stop proposing because it has pending queries (§3.6.1), it means that no live channel of the audience is eligible in that round. The state transition and the re-announcement are the same; both the `attempt_window` of §3.5.2 (a round with no enabled channels expires through here, cheaply) and the Spawner's vacancy branch (§3.13.2) depend on that.

#### 3.7.6. Topic governance: registration, deduplication and query

The `topic` space is open by design (§3.8): any node that decomposes its work can mint a new topic when publishing a child `task.announced` (§3.7.4), without coordinating with the rest of the network. Left ungoverned, this risks semantic fragmentation—`coding`, `programming` and `develop` coexisting as distinct topics, each with its own node, when in reality they are the same work artificially split. That risk is closed by reusing patterns that already govern elsewhere in the network, without adding new structural components.

* **Two populations of topics:** the **fixed-convention** ones (`intake`, §2.1, is the only one the architecture requires as a component in its own right) are established when the network is deployed and go through no check—their full inventory and their contracts are in §3.7.7; the **emergent** ones are all the others, minted dynamically as the network decomposes work, and they are the ones this mechanism governs.
* **Optimistic emission, centralized authority:** a node that decides to publish on a new topic needs no permission and consults nothing before doing so—an invented topic nobody claims is as cheap as any other topic without candidates (§3.7.5, vacancy). The only authority that actually *mints* a topic is the Spawner, and only at the moment it is about to pay the real cost of creating a node for it (§3.13.2, missing-coverage branch, step 2). Nodes propose topics; the Spawner decides which ones actually come to exist.
* **Topic Registry:** an extension of the Specialty Catalog (§3.13.3)—the same store shared among all of the network's Spawner partitions, not a new component. Each entry is `{topic, embedding, model_id, description}`—the `description`, a short description, is the **exact source text** from which that embedding was derived (§3.7.3), not a separate gloss written for human consumption. It is written only once, in step 2 of the missing-coverage branch of §3.13.2, when the Spawner confirms that a topic is genuinely new and creates the first node for it.
* **`topic_catalog_query`: optional query on the emitting node's side.** A node that is about to decide which topic to dispatch a subtask to—typically when decomposing a request into more specific subtasks (§3.7.4)—can, if its own blueprint implements it, publish `task.announced` on the dedicated topic `topic_catalog_query` (§3.8) to ask for the current listing. Any Spawner instance can answer—unlike `trace_query` (§3.10, scoped by `TraceID`) or `node_review` (§3.12.3, scoped by `target_topic`), the Topic Registry is already shared among partitions (§3.13.3), so no deterministic routing is needed: it is resolved by **auction without similarity** (admission by structural credential, §3.7.5 step 4), because any instance is equally competent to answer. Ranking by neighborhood would not choose better among interchangeable candidates and would set the Spawner competing against the same vector space it repairs (§3.13.3), breaking this query precisely when the model is changing. The listing (names + descriptions) is lightweight and travels directly in the payload of the `task.completed`, with no need for Claim-Check.
* **Coordination cost:** the general criterion of §3.10 applies—it feeds a node decision, not the critical path. And the marginal cost is even lower than that of `trace_query`: a semantic node that is going to invoke its LLM anyway to decide how to decompose the work only needs to add the listing to the context of that same call—it does not trigger an additional model call; it only enriches it.
* **Heuristic, not authority:** querying `topic_catalog_query` reduces how often a node ends up proposing a redundant topic, but does not guarantee it—the listing may be out of date, or the node's criterion may fail anyway. The Spawner's neighborhood check (§3.13.2, missing-coverage branch, step 1) is still the final authority and always runs, whether or not the emitting node queried. A network can operate without any node that implements this query—more vacancies, more coordination cost, but without compromising the system's correctness (the same principle of emergent composition as §2.1).

#### 3.7.7. Fixed-convention topics

§3.7.6 divides the topic space into two populations and governs the emergent one. This section closes the other: the topics that exist **by convention, not by minting**—they are established when the network is deployed, do not go through the neighborhood check, do not enter the Topic Registry, and no node can rename or deduplicate them. Each is born where its mechanism is born; here is the full inventory and what each one guarantees.

| Topic | Who claims it | Mechanism | If nobody claims it |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `intake` (entry topic) | the first domain node that proposes | auction (§3.7.5) | vacancy → the Spawner provisions (§5.1) |
| `config_change` | Config Store (§3.14.4) | deterministic routing | does not happen: it is structural (§2.1) |
| `trace_query` | Tracing Collector of the `TraceID`'s partition (§3.10) | deterministic routing, scoped by `TraceID` | timeout visible to the requester (§3.10) |
| `node_review` | Spawner of the `topic`'s partition (§3.12.3) | deterministic routing, scoped by `target_topic` | does not happen: it is structural (§2.1) |
| `node_query` | Network Monitor—the one of the `topic`'s partition in the point form, any instance in the active-fleet census form (§3.12.3) | deterministic by `data.target_topic`; without it, auction without similarity | timeout visible to the requester (§3.12.1) |
| `node_catalog_query` | any Spawner instance (§3.13.3) | auction without similarity—the Catalog is already shared | timeout visible to the operator: they cannot choose what to promote or what to delete, and nothing else is affected (§3.13.6) |
| `node_catalog_publish` | Spawner of the partition of the blueprint's `topic`; any instance if the request names a bundle (§3.13.6) | deterministic by `data.target_topic`; without it, auction without similarity | timeout visible to the operator: the promotion does not happen and nothing else is affected (§2.1) |
| `node_provision` | Spawner of the partition of the blueprint's `topic` (§3.13.8) | deterministic routing, scoped by `target_topic` | timeout visible to the operator: the instantiation does not happen and nothing else is affected (§2.1) |
| `node_create` | Spawner of the partition of the `topic` the node is going to claim (§3.13.8) | same | timeout visible to the operator: the entry is not registered and nothing else is affected |
| `node_catalog_import` | Spawner of the partition of the blueprint's `topic`; any instance if the request names a bundle (§3.13.8) | deterministic by `data.target_topic`; without it, auction without similarity | timeout visible to the operator: the entry is not registered and nothing else is affected |
| `node_retire` | Spawner of the partition of the `topic` of the node or the entry (§3.13.8) | deterministic routing, scoped by `target_topic` | timeout visible to the operator: the node keeps running or the entry stays unmarked, and nothing else is affected |
| `topic_catalog_query` | any Spawner instance (§3.7.6) | auction without similarity—the Registry is already shared | optional query: more redundant topics, without compromising correctness |
| `trace_risk` | nobody: it is a broadcast, not a task—the Tracing Collector emits it (§3.15.2) | `notice` (§3.8.2), fan-out per node (§3.6.5) | does not apply: there is nothing to award |
| `network_risk` | same: the Network Monitor and the Spawner emit it (§3.15.7) | same | same |
| `hitl_contact_<audience>` | the channel that serves that audience (§3.5.3) | auction *within* the audience, without similarity: admission by credential and membership (§3.7.5, step 4) | vacancy → the Spawner fails it without taking it, and the requester counts it as a round without an answer (§3.5.2, §3.13.2) |

What the table does not say:

* **Fixed does not mean exempt from vacancy.** A topic existing by convention only takes it out of the minting cycle (§3.7.6); auction and vacancy apply equally to those claimed by auction. That is why `intake` in a freshly deployed network triggers the Spawner like any other topic without candidates (§5.1, step 3). And there is no need to exempt the broadcast rows: no `task.announced` is ever emitted on them, so there are no rounds to count and no vacancy to declare (§3.8.2)—the rule holds with no list of exceptions to maintain.
* **`hitl_contact_<audience>` is a family, not a topic.** The prefix is fixed-convention; the suffix is one of the audiences §3.5.3 fixes and is **not** free: no node mints it, so no topic of the family enters the Topic Registry (§3.7.6). It is the only case where the suffix of a topic name carries meaning (§3.6.5).
* **The topic a request is about travels in `data.target_topic`, and `data.topic` is always that of the announcement.** The requests that name a node or an entry—`node_review`, the point form of `node_query`, `node_catalog_publish` and the operator's requests on the fleet (§3.13.8)—are announced on their topic from the table and carry in `target_topic` the topic of the specialty they name, which is the key against which their claimant selects itself. They are two fields because `data.topic` is read by consumers that do not know the event's topic—the Task Broker when choosing a partition (§3.1), the Spawner when counting vacancy rounds (§3.13.2), the Network Monitor when counting a topic's awards (§3.12.3)—and a request that put the specialty there would count as traffic of that specialty: the review of a node for `no_demand` would add an award to the topic whose lack of demand it reports. It is the rule §3.8.1 fixes for `subject`: a task's argument travels in a field of its own. A request without `target_topic`—publishing or importing a bundle (§3.13.6)—is claimed by any instance.
* **Deterministic routing is what makes an empty network administrable.** The topics claimed by routing instead of by auction (`config_change`, `trace_query`, `node_review`, `node_query` in its point form) have a structural owner from startup, before a single emergent node exists. That is what upholds the statement of §2.1 and §5.1: administering the network, querying its history and inspecting what is running cannot depend on the network being populated.
* **The claimant column splits the table, and that split decides admission.** The split is not binary. Where the claimant is a structural component, the award does not go through similarity: admission is by credential and membership (§3.7.5, step 4). Where there is no claimant—`trace_risk` and `network_risk`, which are broadcasts—there is no admission at all: a `notice` is neither admitted nor rejected because it never goes through the Task Broker (§3.8.2). And **a single topic remains where step 4 runs in full**: `intake`, the only one in the table whose candidates are not interchangeable and where neighborhood actually chooses. The practical consequence is what matters: **administration, observability and human contact do not depend on the current vector space**, so they survive a change of `embedding_model` (§2.3) just as they survive a pause, with no exceptions. The mechanisms by which an admitted fixed topic sidesteps similarity—deterministic routing and admission exemption—are not different rules but implementations of the same one; broadcast does not even need to sidestep it, because there is nothing to award.

  **And the dividing line goes beyond this table.** The criterion being the claimant and not the topic has a consequence that cannot be seen by looking only at this table's rows: a structural component is exempt **also when it proposes outside of here**. The concrete case is the Spawner with its sentinel proposal on a vacant emergent topic (§3.13.2)—structural, with nothing to rank, and with a credential that comes with the deployment and nobody can re-issue. §3.7.5 treats it in the same rule and by the same argument. Reading the exemption as a property of this table would leave that path being discarded for `model_id` permanently after the first migration.
* **The table is also the list of exemptions from the network pause.** With admission paused (§3.18), what the Task Broker keeps admitting is a subset of this table: **everything that is a task, except `intake`**. It is not a list invented for that section: the line falls where it already was, because everything else in this table is administration, observability, human contact or fleet preparation, and `intake` is the only domain work that appears in it. The operator's requests on the fleet (§3.13.8) being on that side is deliberate: instantiating coverage the network will need when it reopens is work on the fleet and not domain work, and the node brought up during the window is born signed under the current `embedding_model` (§3.13.8), so it enters the roster already migrated instead of adding to what is pending (§3.13.7). The broadcast rows are left off the list for a different and stronger reason than an exemption: a `notice` is neither admitted nor rejected, because it never goes through the Task Broker—the pause does not reach it, and not because it was exempted. A concrete consequence of `hitl_contact_<audience>` being exempt: a human query opened before the pause can close during it, instead of expiring by `hitl_response_window` (§3.5.2) while the network is stopped.

**`intake` in detail.** It is the only one in the table that §2.1 lists as a structural component in its own right—the others are topics a mechanism uses, not infrastructure that gets deployed. Its contract deserves to be made explicit:

* **Why it exists by definition.** The Entry Node is deterministic (§3.6.3) and classifying a request into a domain topic is a semantic decision that is not its to make (§3.5.1). If there were no entry topic agreed in advance, the edge would have to choose one—and that choice is exactly what the architecture forbids it. `intake` is the way out of that problem: a destination that requires no classification.
* **Who claims it.** Nobody by contract. Whoever proposes and the Task Broker chooses wins it (§3.7.5), and that node is, in practice, the first to decide what to do with the request—typically decompose it toward more specific topics. There is no reserved "Planner": the role is emergent and replaceable like any other (§2.1, §3.13.4).
* **What happens if nobody does.** Normal vacancy: backoff, re-announcement, and on crossing the rounds threshold the Spawner that owns `intake` in its ring (§3.19) provisions a node for it (§3.13.2). That Spawner exists from the first second, even though `intake` has no candidate yet, because partitioning covers the full space of possible topics from startup (§2.1).
* **Relation to deduplication.** None: `intake` does not enter the Topic Registry (§3.7.6) and takes no part in the neighborhood check. The practical consequence is that a node that mints an emergent topic semantically close to "receiving generic requests" **will not** collapse against `intake`—the Spawner will treat it as a new niche. It is acceptable because `intake` does not compete for work: it is an entry destination, not a specialty.
* **Its name is contract, not configuration.** `intake` is fixed like the rest of the table: no Global Configuration parameter redefines it (§2.3), and nothing about this topic is provisioned with the deployment beyond the convention itself (§5.1). It is the only one in the table that *could* have been configurable—the others are claimed by a structural component through deterministic routing, or emitted by a mechanism that does not consult configuration to name them—but the name takes part in no decision of the network: it carries no weight in deduplication (*relation to deduplication*, above), and the award is resolved by `task_embedding` and never by the topic's name (§3.7.5). A deployment that needs to isolate its topics from those of another network on the same substrate has that problem over the whole table and not over this row: the Bus's namespace solves it (§3.6.5).

### 3.8. Event schemas: envelope and types

So that the network can grow dynamically (new nodes, new topics, new capabilities) without each addition requiring changes to the bus or coordination with the rest of the nodes, Zobik separates two layers:

* **Envelope (fixed, closed):** the structure every event follows and every node knows how to interpret without needing to know the task's domain. It follows the **CloudEvents (CNCF)** standard.
* **`type` (fixed, closed):** a small set of lifecycle types, taken from FIPA's **Contract Net Protocol (CNP)**—the classic multi-agent systems standard for announcing, proposing and awarding tasks. This set does not grow when new nodes are added.
* **`data.topic` / `data.task_embedding` (open, dynamic):** this is where all domain variability lives. A new node does not require a new event `type`—it subscribes to a topic (which may be brand new, created by the Spawner) and competes by semantic similarity (see §3.7). The meaning of a task is not encoded in the event's name but in its description/embedding.

#### 3.8.1. Envelope (CloudEvents)

| Field | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `id` | Unique UUID of the event. |
| `type` | One of the closed types of the table in §3.8.2: those of the `task.*` namespace, plus `notice`. |
| `source` | Identifier of the emitting node, declared by whoever publishes. It is read to attribute observations—the Tracing Collector when reconstructing the trace (§3.2), the Network Monitor when checking against its roster (§3.12.2)—never to decide an admission: the identity against which the Task Broker verifies a proposal is the one the deployment attributes to the event (§3.14.1), not this field. |
| `subject` | Identifier of the task this event belongs to. For a trace's root that identifier is the `TraceID`, which is its `TaskID` (§3.5.1, §3.9.1). It always means that and only that: every identifier that is an *argument* of the task—a queried trace (§3.10), an evaluated node (§3.12.3), a partition or a niche (§3.15.7)—travels in `data`, never in `subject`. That is what lets a generic consumer (the Tracing Collector when deriving span parentage, §3.2; whoever observes a trace's close, §3.9.1) interpret the field without knowing the event's `topic`. **Sole exception, and it is one of type, not of topic:** in a `notice` (§3.8.2) there is no task to belong to, so `subject` is **the resource the notice talks about**—the frozen `TraceID`, the partition without coverage, the niche. That is why the generic consumers above have to look at `type` before `subject`: a `notice` with `subject = TraceID` is **not** the close of the root request, and reading it as such would be exactly the error the general rule exists to avoid. |
| `time` | Emission timestamp. |
| `traceparent` | `TraceID`/`SpanID` in W3C Trace Context format (the basis of the OTel observability of §3.2). Present in every `task.*` event and in **trace-scope** `notice` events; absent in **network-scope** ones, which belong to no trace—a partition without enforcement coverage is not a fact of any particular trace. It is the absence that keeps the Tracing Collector from observing them, with no need for a new rule: its subscription is already defined by carrying `traceparent` (§3.2). |
| `config_version` | Version of the Global Configuration snapshot under which this trace is judged. Whoever opens the trace stamps it (§3.5.1) and every child event inherits it unchanged, just like `traceparent`. It carries no configuration values, only the version identifier: it is what lets a node and the Task Broker evaluate the same fact against the same snapshot when the configuration changes at runtime (§2.3). It also follows `traceparent` in its absence: a network-scope `notice` does not carry it, because there is no trace to judge. |
| `data` | Type-specific payload: `topic` (the topic on which the task is announced, and only that: the topic a request is about travels in `target_topic`, §3.7.7), `task_embedding`, `workspace` (project/repo identifier, optional, chosen by whoever opens the trace (§3.5.1) and inherited unchanged by every child event of the same `TraceID`, just like `traceparent`: the Integration Sidecar stamps it from the task it acts on (§3.6.1)—see §2.4), `origin_audience` (the audience of the channel that opened the trace, to which `requester` resolves throughout it; whoever opens the trace stamps it (§3.5.1) and it is inherited like `workspace`—see §3.5.3), the Claim-Check reference to the Context Store, `bodies` (the references to the bodies of an ingress message, in the announcement of the entry subtask that comes from it or in the `task.completed` of the query it answers; the channel's Integration Sidecar stamps them, §3.16.7), `current_attempts` and `previous_attempt` (the `TaskID` of the attempt a `task.announced` reopens; the requester's Integration Sidecar stamps both, §3.3), `final_round` (the mark of the attempt that opens the final round of a human query; the same Integration Sidecar stamps it, §3.5.2), declared similarity (optional in `task.proposed`; a ceiling on the value the Task Broker recomputes, §3.7.5), the similarity with which the Task Broker awarded (in `task.assigned`, the winner's; in `task.rejected`, that of the candidate that lost—the same value it already recomputed in step 4 of §3.7.5, published as a **scalar** and not as a vector: it does not reintroduce geometry into an addressed event, and knowing it gives no leverage because the node cannot move the embedding it competes with, which is signed into its capability credential, §3.14.2), the `model_id` of the vector space under which the `task_embedding` was generated (§3.7.3), `requester_identity` (the platform identity—§3.14.1—of the node that opened the task: its Integration Sidecar stamps it in the `task.announced` and the Task Broker copies it into the `task.assigned`, and it is to whom the terminal event of that task is addressed, §3.6.1. It is declared in each announcement and is not inherited down the trace tree, unlike `traceparent`, `config_version`, `workspace` and `origin_audience`: each node that announces a subtask stamps its own), the Context Store scope token (only in `task.assigned`, where the Task Broker mints it for the winner—it is not inherited down the trace tree, §3.14.3), `failure_reason` (mandatory in every `task.failed`, see §3.3), `failure_code` (in the `task.failed` whose cause the architecture fixes, see §3.3), `suggested_topic` (optional in `task.failed`, when the reason is topic redundancy instead of insufficient specification—see §3.13.2), `metrics` (mandatory in terminal events—`tokens_in`, `tokens_out`, `cost` as monetary cost of any origin, `duration_ms`; see §3.10), etc. In a `notice` the payload is the condition being broadcast and nothing more: it carries no `metrics`—there was no work to measure—and no `task_embedding`, because there is nothing to award (§3.8.2). |

#### 3.8.2. Event types (Contract Net Protocol)

| `type` | Equivalent CNP performative | Typical emitter |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `task.announced` | `cfp` (call-for-proposal) | The channel that opens a trace, in its entry subtask (§3.5.1), or any worker when decomposing/re-specifying a subtask (§2.1, §3.7.4) |
| `task.proposed` | `propose` | Any candidate worker (§3.1, §3.7) |
| `task.assigned` | `accept-proposal` | Task Broker, when atomically granting the claim (§3.1) |
| `task.rejected` | `reject-proposal` | Task Broker |
| `task.completed` | `inform-done` | Worker that executed the task, or the channel that opened a trace, on its root (§3.5.4) |
| `task.failed` | `failure` | Worker that executed the task—at its Logic Container's request or, if that goes down, by its Integration Sidecar (§3.6.1)—the channel that opened a trace, on its root (§3.5.4), or the Spawner on a vacant task—badly specified, redundant with an existing topic, or with its embedding in an obsolete space (§3.13.2)—on a human query no channel claimed, which it closes without having it assigned (§3.13.2), or on an operator request that does not verify (§3.13.6, §3.13.8) |
| `task.aborted` | — (own extension) | Tracing Collector, when closing a trace's entry subtask after the human decision to abandon or accept a partial result, or after its absence (§3.15.6) |
| `notice` | — (outside CNP: it is not a speech act) | Structural components (§2.1) only |

A closed set of types. **Principle: new topic, not new `type`.** Adding a new node, a new topic or a new capability never requires adding a `type`: it is resolved entirely in `data.topic` and `data.task_embedding`. Minting a topic, moreover, costs nothing: there is no schema to version, no registry to update and no node to notify, and a topic nobody claims is simply a topic without candidates (§3.7.5). Every new channel opens as a topic under this principle.

**Asking for human intervention does not add a `type` either.** It is the case that most tempts one to add one—a pause feels like a protocol state—and it is where the principle is put to the test: the request is a `task.announced` on a `hitl_contact_*` topic and the answer its `task.completed`, with the human's disposition in the payload (§3.5.2) and the audience in the topic (§3.5.3). A pair of types dedicated to "waiting for a human" / "human answered" would describe nothing the `task.*` types do not already describe.

**Nor does reopening for another reason.** It is the same test applied to the other tempting case: a requester that reopens because the previous approach fell short emits nothing different from a requester that reopens because the specification changed. The reason for the reopen is the node's policy (§3.3) and not a moment in the task's lifecycle; what the network needs to know—that the task is in play again and how many times it was attempted—travels whole in a `task.announced` with its `previous_attempt` and its `current_attempts`. A dedicated type would split the `cfp` in two and force every generic consumer to match two where today it matches one, starting with the Spawner's count of vacancy rounds (§3.13.2).

##### `notice`: the type that stays outside the cycle, and why the "new topic, not new `type`" principle does not reach it

The `task.*` types are a task's lifecycle: someone announces it, someone proposes, an arbiter awards, whoever executed it closes it. **`notice` takes no part in that cycle.** It is a broadcast event that reports a condition and **obliges nothing and no one**.

The principle above **does not reach it, and it is worth seeing why the distinction is not an escape hatch**. "New topic, not new `type`" is a principle about **domain variability**: a task's meaning lives in its embedding and not in the event's name, so a new node, a new capability or a new channel is resolved in `data.topic`—and that remains true without exception. A `notice` is not new domain. It is a **form of interaction** the CNP cycle does not model, and no number of new topics produces it: the difference is not in *what* the event talks about but in that there is no counterpart, no award and no cycle to close. The contrast with a task's lifecycle is exact and deliberate: asking for human intervention **does** fit in the task cycle—there is a requester, there is someone who claims it, there is a close—and that is why it adds no type.

**The performative column carries `—` because there is none to borrow.** The rest of the table maps to CNP because each of those events is a speech act addressed between nodes, and FIPA-ACL is entirely messaging with a sender and a receiver: it has no unaddressed act. **A `notice` is not a speech act at all**: it is addressed to no one and does not aim to induce belief or action. It is the echo of a state.

**The invariant that keeps it harmless, and that is contract, not advice:**

> A `notice` never creates an obligation, and every condition it broadcasts must be backed by **authoritative state that a structural component reads on its own**. No network rule can depend on a `notice` being delivered.

Without the backing of authoritative state, a broadcast channel becomes a shadow control plane—a way to influence nodes outside the single enforcement point. With it written down, it cannot be: **the `notice` is never the source of truth; it is always the echo.** It is the same reason §3.18 refuses to notify a pause, resolved here by construction instead of by abstention.

From that invariant and from the absence of a lifecycle the rules follow, none of which has to be invented case by case:

* **It is never awarded.** There is no `task.proposed` or `task.assigned` on a `notice`. It does not enter the Topic Registry, is not deduplicated by neighborhood and similarity does not touch it (§3.7).
* **It does not trigger vacancy, and it does not need to be exempted.** The Spawner counts rounds over re-announcements of `task.announced` (§3.13.2); on a topic that only carries notices none is ever emitted, so there is nothing to count. That is what lets the vacancy rule on fixed topics (§3.7.7) stay intact instead of needing an exception and a list of topics to maintain.
* **Only on fixed-convention topics (§3.7.7).** Broadcasting toward an emergent niche that may not exist means nothing, and it would let a domain topic accumulate control semantics.
* **No `metrics`.** There was no work to measure, and it is not a terminal event (§3.10).
* **Its delivery is fan-out per node**, like that of any Bus event—see §3.6.5. Every subscriber receiving its copy is what makes a notice reach **the node doing the affected work** and not just any one of the others.
* **`notice` describes the shape of the event, not its severity.** It is worth saying because in operations vocabulary (RFC 5424) `notice` is a severity level below `warning`, and here it is not: the severity of the condition lives in the topic and the payload, not in the type.

**Only structural components emit it (§2.1), and that is a contract rule, not a mechanism.** The Bus does not validate `source`, and no component is positioned to verify it on this path, because a `notice` does not go through the Task Broker. It is not that the distinction is unverifiable—§3.7.5 resolves it cryptographically for proposals, by credential against the deployment root and without looking at the envelope's `source`—it is that here it does not need verifying: **the invariant makes security independent of who emitted.** Even if an emergent node published a `notice`, it would condition nothing. It is the same criterion as §2.2 and as `metrics` in §3.6.4—nothing prevents a node from lying, and the architecture is built so that lying buys nothing. The restriction holds anyway, as hygiene: it keeps clear what is authoritative and keeps the Bus from filling up with unbacked broadcast.

**Detecting the systematic pattern, though not preventing it on the path, is the Network Monitor's responsibility (§3.12.2, §3.12.3).** It needs to ask nobody anything: it already reads the Active Node Registry for the usage-axis roster (§3.12.1) and already observes the Bus broadly, so checking a `notice`'s `source` against that same roster is not a new capability, only a comparison over data it already has.

**Its mechanism comes in §3.15**, where `trace_risk` and `network_risk` broadcast the condition that freezes a trace or leaves a partition without coverage. It is the same split that `task.aborted` follows: the type is declared here, next to the lifecycle it belongs to, and the mechanism that produces it lives where the condition that triggers it lives (§3.15.6).

### 3.9. Recursive subtask composition and local trust

When a node decomposes the task it was assigned into subtasks of its own (new `task.announced` events, §3.7.4), it becomes the *requester* of those subtasks and is subject to the rules that derive directly from §2.2 and hold at any level of the chain, with no need for a special coordinator role.

#### 3.9.1. Closing rule: nobody completes before its children
A node that emitted subtasks cannot publish its own `task.completed` until it has resolved (received and evaluated, by its own criterion) the result of all the subtasks it depends on to accept its task as done. A node that opens a single verification subtask before declaring itself finished (§5.2) is a particular case, with a single child, of this general rule; a node that splits its task into N subtasks does nothing structurally different—it just has more children to wait for. There is no special event or component that "aggregates" or "synchronizes" the close: each node keeps its own count of which subtasks it emitted and which results it is waiting for. That count is of the subtasks the node actually emitted, never of those it should have emitted—the network has nothing to check the latter against (§2.2). A node that closes without having emitted any subtask passes this rule without problem, whether because the task was actually atomic or because it should have announced something and did not: zero expected subtasks is as valid a count as any other, and the rule does not distinguish the two cases (§2.2).

**The root request closes in two steps.** Whoever resolves the entry subtask closes it like any other, addressed to the channel that opened the trace, which is its requester (§3.5.1): that way the channel knows when to answer whoever originated it, without knowing the internal structure of the plan that resolved it. The channel delivers that outcome and only then closes the root (§3.5.4). The terminal event with `subject = TraceID` (§3.8.1), which every generic consumer reads as the trace's close, is always emitted by the channel that opened it. The close waiting for the delivery is what makes visible an answer that did not arrive: the trace stays open while delivery is attempted, and closes with `task.failed` if it does not succeed.

#### 3.9.2. Local trust, not a global contract
From the fact that a node's internals are neither inspectable nor enforceable (§2.2) it follows that **there is not, and cannot be, a global contract that tells a requester what degree of verification was applied by whoever returned a resolved task to it**. Each requester decides, by its own criterion (fixed by whoever created it), how much assurance it needs before accepting a result—and if what it received is not enough, it has the same tool as any other node: emitting its own additional verification subtasks, with no need to know whether the previous node already did something equivalent internally.

This is recursive and has no cut-off point defined a priori: a node can verify itself before declaring `task.completed`; the requester that summoned it can still ask for additional verification beyond the scope of that self-verification; and a human, at the top of the chain, can refuse to approve the whole plan—queried as `requester` by some node of the chain (§3.5.2)—even if all the intermediate nodes reported success. No level of this chain is mandatory or guaranteed by the system—it exists only if there is a node in the network willing to demand it.

**Important consequence:** the level of assurance of any resulting flow (how much a change is verified before it is considered finished) is entirely **emergent from which nodes exist in the network at the time of the request and what trust policy each one implemented**—it is not a guarantee the architecture can promise. A network without any node dedicated to verifying simply verifies nothing; it is still a valid operation of the system, only with less certainty about the result. This is intentional: it is the same principle as §2.1 (the network is agnostic to which nodes make it up) applied to the quality of the result, not only to its composition. What this section fixes is how much a result is verified, not whether the work got done: a request being able to close without producing the expected effect is a separate property, and it lives in §2.2.

**Local trust is not just a limitation: it is the incentive engine of the quality model.** Nobody guaranteeing assurance from outside is what forces each requester to demand it on its own, because **a node that accepts bad work from a child takes the blame itself**: its own requester will reopen *it*, and that reopen counts against its `reopen_rate`, not the child's (§3.12.2). The demand thus propagates from the root downward—each node is demanding because it is judged—and the root is the human via the User-Proxy. The corollary worth accepting instead of fighting is that **the consumer defines quality**: if the human accepts a mediocre result, the bar of the whole trace legitimately drops, and that is the system working, not failing.

One case remains that this chain does not punish on the culprit: a requester that accepts a child's result and then **redoes the work on its own** instead of reopening leaves no negative signal on that child. But it is not invisible either—it pays for those calls in **its own `cost_own`** (§3.12.2), which is emitted in every terminal event whether anyone reopens or not. The mediocre child goes unpunished on the quality axis; the parent becomes the expensive node and it is *the parent* that ends up reviewed. The waste is recorded, and it is recorded where action can be taken. That is why reopening is the cheap path and redoing the expensive one, and no rule is needed to force it.

**Reopening when a result is not trusted:** when a requester decides it needs more verification, it does not call the previous node directly—it publishes a `task.announced` (or reopens one, §3.3) like any other request, subject to the same open auction and with no private channel between nodes (§3.6.1). Two different requesters could, in theory, ask for verification of the same result in parallel without coordinating with each other; and a chain of "I don't trust it, I ask for more" could, in the worst case, grow without limit.

**These cases are bounded by aggregate cost, not by point detection.** Neither has a dedicated detection mechanism, and neither needs one: the budget per `TraceID` (§3.15) adds up both the `cost` and the reopens of the *whole* tree of a trace, so a duplicated verification or a growing chain of distrust consume the same budget as any other work under that `TraceID` and end up freezing the trace if they become abusive.

### 3.10. Trace history query and mandatory metrics

The Tracing Collector (§3.2) solves observability for humans, but a node of the network may also need to reconstruct "what happened" in a `TraceID` to make a decision of its own (e.g. evaluating whether an equivalent subtask was already attempted and failed). That need is solved with the same pattern that governs everything that crosses a system boundary: no node calls the external resource directly; it does so through a boundary node.

* **The Tracing Collector as the observability boundary node:** the same role the User-Proxy (§3.5) plays toward the human user, applied here to the tracing backend. It is the only path to the backend open to every node. What goes beyond one's own trace—other traces, a node's history, identities—is reachable only through egress (§3.16): a class the operator registers and that the node whose `egress_grants` associate it reaches, which is how the Forge reads a network's evidence (§6.6).
* **The query is one more task, not a direct call:** a node that needs its trace's history publishes a `task.announced` on the dedicated topic `trace_query`. The Tracing Collector (or any instance of that role, partitionable just like the Task Broker, §3.1) claims it, queries the backend, persists the result in the Context Store under the section of the query task (Claim-Check, §3.4)—it is purged with the trace's close, like the rest of its content—and answers `task.completed` with the reference—with `subject` = the query task, like any other close (§3.8.1), so that the requester can correlate the answer even if it has several queries open, and so that the close is not confused with that of the root request (§3.9.1).
* **A requester queries only its own trace.** The queried trace is the one of the announcement's `traceparent`, which the Integration Sidecar stamps over the task assigned to the node (§3.6.1): it is a trace the requester already reads with its scope token (§3.14.3), and the announcement carries no field with which to name another.
* **The reconstruction arrives without node identities, to every requester.** It receives the trace's structure, its topics, its outcomes and its `metrics`—what it needs to know whether something equivalent was already attempted and how it ended—but not who published each event or against whom each auction was won: a requester does not learn this way who resolved its subtask (§3.3). Neither this rule nor the rule of querying only one's own trace depends on the requester's class, so the Tracing Collector does not distinguish it.
* **Partitioned by `TraceID`, not by `topic`:** unlike the Task Broker—which partitions by `topic` because the tasks of the same topic compete with each other (§3.1)—the Tracing Collector partitions by `TraceID`. Reason: a single trace crosses several topics under a single `TraceID` (§5.2); partitioning by `topic` would scatter a trace's spans across several partitions and force a fan-out to reconstruct it. Partitioning by `TraceID`, each partition is self-sufficient to reconstruct and answer any trace that falls to it.
  * **Ingestion:** each Tracing Collector instance, on observing a Bus event, evaluates its `TraceID` against its own range—the same consistent-hashing scheme as the Task Broker (§3.1), with a ring of its own whose size only the Tracing Collector instances agree on (§3.19)—and persists the event only if it falls in that range; otherwise, it ignores it.
  * **Routing of `trace_query`:** the Tracing Collector does not compete by semantic similarity for this task—it selects itself by evaluating the announcement's `TraceID` against its own range: the same criterion with which it already ingested that trace's events. Only the owner of the partition publishes `task.proposed`; the rest do not compete. There is no race and no double claim: it is deterministic routing, not negotiation—the Task Broker still mediates the atomic assignment, but in practice it receives a single proposal.
* **Availability of the query function:** if there is no active instance for a given partition, that partition's events are not aggregated into any tracing backend and the corresponding `trace_query` queries expire by timeout without an answer, treated by the requesting node like any other timeout of the system (e.g. absence of proposals in §3.7.5)—it proceeds without the benefit of that history. No task-assignment flow depends on the Tracing Collector *answering* a query, and the loss is observable: whoever needed it sees the timeout.
  * **This does not apply to the Tracing Collector's other function.** The same component enforces the anti-abuse budget (§3.15.2), and there the loss is observable by nobody: without a Tracing Collector no cost is accumulated, no threshold is crossed and the network keeps working without a ceiling, indistinguishable from a healthy network. That is why the Tracing Collector is structural (§2.1) and why §3.15.7 defines an explicit coverage mechanism instead of letting it degrade silently.
* **The query goes through the boundary node, and that concentrates in one point what would otherwise be scattered:** it stays on the Bus and in the trace, the credentials and the client of the chosen backend live in a single place, and so do access control and rate-limiting. The cost is the latency of the `announced → proposed → assigned → completed` cycle compared with a synchronous query. **General criterion: a query that feeds a node decision is not on the critical path.** What waits for it is the internal reasoning of whoever emitted it (§2.2), not the task the network is solving.
* **Mandatory metrics in the terminal event:** every `task.completed`/`task.failed` includes `data.metrics` (§3.8.1)—`tokens_in`, `tokens_out`, `cost`, `duration_ms`. It is part of the global contract because it describes the node's external interaction (§2.2), not its internal reasoning, and it is cheap to produce. **The Integration Sidecar produces it entirely**, over what crossed its own boundary: it executes the external calls itself (§3.6.1, §3.16) and it handles the endpoints of the interval. No field is a value the Logic Container declares. The Tracing Collector picks these values up from the event and attaches them as span attributes in the backend, where they are available both for human APM and for `trace_query` queries.
  * **The fields of `metrics` have different scopes, and the difference matters** because both the node quality cycle (§3.12.2) and the anti-abuse budget (§3.15.1) hang on it:
    * **`cost` is universal:** every node can incur monetary cost, with an LLM or without one.
    * **`tokens_in`/`tokens_out` are LLM-specific:** a deterministic node (§3.6.3) carries them as `0` or omits them. **That does not imply its `cost` is zero**—a deterministic node that calls a paid API spends real money.
    * **`duration_ms` is always present**, in every node without exception, because it is the only one that does not depend on the node calling anyone: two events the Integration Sidecar handles anyway delimit it.
  * **Format of each field:**
    * `tokens_in` / `tokens_out`: integers, raw token count as returned by the model provider's API (not normalized across providers). The Integration Sidecar reads it from the response it received itself, through the extraction rule the registry declares (§2.3).
    * `cost`: floating-point number in the monetary unit the Global Configuration fixes for the whole network, `cost_unit` (§2.3)—the field carries no currency code of its own; the unit is the same for every node of the same deployment—with enough precision not to lose magnitude on calls costing fractions of that unit (minimum 6 decimals, e.g. `0.000345`). It is **the monetary cost the node incurred to solve the task, whatever its origin**: LLM tokens, calls to paid third-party APIs, metered infrastructure. The Integration Sidecar derives it from each call it executed, crossing the billing data extracted from the response with the current rate of the provider registry (§2.3, §3.16) and converting it to `cost_unit` if the provider bills in another currency. **When billing does not travel in the response**—providers that settle out of band, protocols that do not allow inline extraction—the registry entry declares a maximum fee per call that is deducted anyway: it overestimates, and in exchange the ceiling of §3.15.1 is never left unfed. The envelope never carries that rate, nor the model used, nor which providers were called: only the aggregated result. **Being a single total and not a breakdown is what makes it compatible with §2.2**—the network knows how much it cost, never what it was spent on.
    * `duration_ms`: integer, milliseconds elapsed between the instant the Integration Sidecar receives the node's `task.assigned` (start of the execution the network awarded it) and the instant the Integration Sidecar emits the corresponding `task.completed`/`task.failed`. It does not measure from `task.announced` (that would include waiting time in the auction, unrelated to the node's work) or from a hypothetical "start of internal processing" of the Logic Container (that boundary is not observable by the network, §2.2, and would be at the discretion of each blueprint). It is, by construction, the same interval the Tracing Collector already uses to open and close the task's span (§3.2), so `duration_ms` is redundant with the trace but is included in the payload anyway so that the data is available without depending on the Tracing Collector being active (§3.10, availability). The root is not assigned: its interval runs from the `task.announced` of the entry subtask to the terminal event with which the channel closes it (§3.5.4).
    * **Nodes with multiple internal calls:** the Integration Sidecar accumulates `tokens_in`, `tokens_out` and `cost` by adding up each call it executed on behalf of the Logic Container—local retries, self-evaluation, its own tools, third-party APIs (§2.2)—and attaches the total to the terminal event; the envelope reports a single aggregated total per task, never a breakdown per individual call—that breakdown, if the node wants it for its own debugging, is its private telemetry (§3.2). If the node uses more than one model or more than one paid provider internally, `cost` is still a single sum in `cost_unit` (the registry's rates already normalize the difference between them); the envelope does not expose which ones were used. **The aggregate being kept by whoever executed each call is what makes it complete without observing anything inside:** the network does not know what the node did internally, but nothing it spent could go out without crossing its boundary (§3.6.1).
* **Node quality: derived by the network, not self-reported:** following §3.9.2 (`task.completed` is an assertion by the emitter, not a verifiable proof), no node evaluates itself as part of the contract—a self-declared "confidence" field would be gameable. The quality signal the Spawner uses to decide on a node's lifecycle is derived externally, over patterns already observable in the trace—never self-reporting by the evaluated node. Who computes it and how it is aggregated belongs to the Network Monitor (§3.12); how it translates into an action on the node, to the Spawner (§3.13).

  * **`metrics` is not an exception to that either**, although unlike quality it measures a fact the node itself produces: it is **measured** at the integration boundary instead of being derived from the Bus (§3.6.1), but it is no more a self-report than the reopen rate is. Nothing the network uses to decide about a node—neither how well it works nor how much it costs—comes from an assertion of its own, and that is why neither signal requires observing its internal calls, which is exactly what §2.2 forbids.

### 3.11. The Active Node Registry

The source of truth for what is running *right now, in this network*. It is not a component the network installs: it is an implicit responsibility of the orchestration substrate the Spawner operates on (§4), which by definition already knows what is deployed, and that is why it counts as an **external dependency** and not as structural (§2.1). It is local by nature: it would make no sense for the activity of a particular instance of this network to travel to a potentially external catalog.

**It is not conflated with the Specialty Catalog, and the separation is what scales.** The Registry says what runs; the Catalog (§3.13.3) says what can be built. They are purposes that do not mix: the fleet changes with every provisioning and every decommission, and does not outlive the network that carries it; the blueprint library persists beyond the lifecycle of any instance and can be promoted to other networks (§3.13.6). The Spawner writes the Registry entirely and alone; of the Catalog it writes its qualified row, and not the artifact (§2.1, §3.13.3). Reading them is not exclusive—the Network Monitor reads the Registry, and never the Catalog (§3.13.3).

**It carries the `topic`, the `model_id`, the `signer_id` and the `similarity_floor` of the issued credential, plus the node's `egress_grants`, `ingress_grants` and memory reservation.** When provisioning, the Spawner already passes the orchestrator the `artifact_ref` to instantiate; next to it, as deployment metadata (a label or annotation, depending on the substrate), it stamps the `topic` of the niche that node covers—only one, §2.1—the `model_id` under which it signed the credential it handed to the Integration Sidecar, the identifier of the key it signed it with, the floor that node competes with (§3.14.2, §3.13.3), the egress registry entries that node reaches (§3.16.3), in a channel, the ingress registry entries it receives through (§3.16.7), and the memory reservation it brought it up with (§3.13.10). It adds no store or component: they are extra fields in an act that already happens. What they buy is **making re-provisioning observable**, which it otherwise would not be—re-signing a node keeps the same `artifact_ref` (§3.13.7), so without these data the registry looks identical before and after migrating it.

**The `similarity_floor` is also what tells apart two instances of the same entry.** The Catalog keeps a single entry per artifact and each instance carries the floor (§3.13.3), so while a widening lasts, or while a narrowing waits for its instance to be ready, two deployments run the same `artifact_ref` with different floors (§3.13.4). The registry's floor says which is which, and it is what the Spawner reads as the entry's active floor.

**The `topic` label is what makes the registry readable per partition.** It is from here that the Spawner and the Network Monitor get what to evaluate their own range against (§3.12.1, §3.13.1), without either having to infer the scoping key. And it leaves structural nodes out by construction, with no need for a list of exceptions: a row without that label is a structural node, which the Spawner did not provision (§2.1), and it belongs to no partition.

**`signer_id` is a selection field, never a validity field, and the distinction is what makes it cheap.** The Task Broker does not read this registry: its verification is against the deployment root and it does not care which key signed (§3.14.2). What it enables is answering *"what is running with a credential issued by this key"* over a registry that is already queried, which is the question needed when a signing key stops being trustworthy—and answering it this way avoids the only alternative, a revocation list the Task Broker would have to consult on the critical path of every proposal. The repair is then to take down that subset and let vacancy repopulate it, not to invalidate a piece of paper (§3.14.2, §3.13.2). The `model_id` travels with the same economy and answers another question—under which vector space that credential was signed—and it is from here, and not from the Catalog, that the census gets it (§3.12.3).

**Absence from the registry is what it means for a node not to run.** There is no decommission mark anyone stamps: a node stops existing for the network when its deployment disappears, and that same fact **extinguishes its credential**, because the platform identity that just disappeared is what sustains it (§3.14.1, §3.14.2). It is half of the "archived" predicate, whose other half—the metrics snapshot persisted alongside the blueprint—lives in the Catalog (§3.13.3): neither store is enough on its own to answer it.

### 3.12. The Network Monitor: node measurement and network state

No node evaluates itself (§3.9.2, §3.10): the network derives from outside how well and at what cost each one works. The Network Monitor is the component that does it—it observes the Bus, aggregates per node the signals of §3.12.2 and delivers them, by evidence or by exploration, to whoever can act on them. It measures and does not enforce: what is decided with those signals belongs to the Spawner (§3.13). From the same observation post it also watches which partitions keep budget enforcement coverage (§3.15.7). This section is its complete home: §3.12.1 fixes its contract, §3.12.2 what it measures, and §3.12.3 the topics through which that result goes out to the Spawner and is queried from outside.

#### 3.12.1. Network Monitor contract

A structural, passive **protocol service** (§2.1), deliberately separated from the Tracing Collector so as not to load the latter with a purpose different from its own (observability for humans, trace queries and per-trace budget, §3.2/§3.10/§3.15). It covers the following objects of surveillance:

* **Node performance**—the object the rest of §3.12 develops: the quality, cost, radius, usefulness, provenance and resource signals of §3.12.2 per node, which lead to `node_review` toward the Spawner (§3.12.3).
* **Network state**—which partitions have budget enforcement coverage and which do not, by reading the leases of the shared registry; when one is missing, the Network Monitor itself opens the query to the operator on `hitl_contact_operator` and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` (§3.15.7).

It is not one more aggregation axis over the Bus: network state does not aggregate events; it reads keys. That is why it lives here instead of justifying one more component.

On network state the Network Monitor **also writes a key**, `risk:coverage` of the Contention Registry (§3.15.2), which is the one that holds the open query and at the same time chooses which instance asks. It is a declared key of a registry it already read, and not part of its working aggregate (below).

On those objects it **also answers queries**: it is the claimant of `node_query` (§3.12.3), the only topic where the Network Monitor answers instead of publishing. That does not take it out of passivity (§2.1), and it is the only exception: in everything else the Network Monitor only emits and reads.

**Partitioned by `topic`, and the subject is the node, not the task.** The Network Monitor comes up partitioned in its own ring (§3.19) and the membership criterion is the `topic` of the **observed node**—the specialty that node covers, not the topic of the event that brought it into the stream. The two always coincide when there is an assignment, and where they could diverge the rule of §2.1 resolves it beforehand: a node claims a single topic, so its partition is one and does not depend on what is being looked at. It is the same axis the Spawner uses (§3.13.1), and that is why `node_review` travels from one partition to the other without either computing the other ring's partition (§3.19)—the two rings can have different sizes and neither needs to know the other's.

**The aggregate lives where a successor can read it, and keeps what its windows ask for.** It is the requirement of §3.19, met the way any partitioned component meets it. For each node of the roster it keeps the evidence of its windows—up to `evidence_staleness` back—its deferred classifications in flight and its snooze, together with the point in the stream up to which it observed. An instance that restarts, or that inherits a range, reads it and resumes the stream from that point: from the Bus it rereads only what happened while the range had no owner, which is the recovery the Bus's retention covers (§3.6.5), and a reread event is not counted twice (§3.6.4). A node's aggregate leaves with it when it leaves the roster; what is kept of its measurement is the snapshot the Spawner writes into the Catalog (§3.13.3).

**An observation gap does not count against any node.** If a range was without an owner longer than the Bus retains, the events of that interval were lost, and the Network Monitor knows it because the stream no longer reaches the point where it stopped observing. It is the requirement further down—no interval in which the network could not generate evidence counts against a node—applied to evidence that existed and was not observed: **every window that spans the gap is left incomplete**, its signals are reported as absent and no trigger that depends on them goes out until the window fills up again with later observations. That includes the no-demand period of `no_demand` (§3.12.3), which counts again from the end of the gap. What the loss produces is abstention, like the staleness of evidence, and never a review by evidence. Occurrence triggers—`notice_provenance`, `memory_exhausted`—still hold: a gap can hide an occurrence, never fabricate one.

**Deliberate limit: the Network Monitor notifies, it never enforces.** It does not act on a node's lifecycle (that belongs to the Spawner, §3.13.1) or freeze anything (that is produced by the lease absence the Task Broker already reads, §3.15.7). If the Network Monitor is down, the notice and the inspection are lost, never the containment—and that is what makes it acceptable for it to be off the critical path. The deployment platform watches its own liveness, just like transport authentication (§3.14.1); no node of the network watches the Network Monitor.
