* It subscribes broadly to the Bus, with the same passive pattern as the Tracing Collector (§3.2), but with a different goal: computing the signals of §3.12.2 per node.
* It keeps a working aggregate: the minimum needed to classify outcomes and compute the running signals of §3.12.2. How it organizes it internally (storage, structures) is an implementation detail outside the contract, just like the Context Store (§3.4) or the Tracing Collector itself: the network does not need to know how it does it, only what it exposes outward and that a successor can read it (above). **Exposing percentiles and not just ratios does not make it larger**: a percentile over a sliding window is kept in bounded space with the usual structures (reservoir sampling, t-digest-style sketches), without storing the series of observations—so the aggregate stays of the same order. **Partitioning, on the other hand, is not internal to it**, and the dividing line is not binary: the *axis* is contract—it is what guarantees a single competent responder for the point form of `node_query` and that the census needs no fan-out (§3.12.3)—while the *size of the ring* stays private, through the self-selection of §3.19: whoever emits never computes the partition of whoever answers.
* **Deferred classification:** on observing a `task.completed`, it waits a `grace_period` (Global Configuration, §2.3) before deciding whether it was clean or reopened—a reopen can arrive with latency, while the requester evaluates the result.
* **Roster of subjects for the usage axis:** to evaluate `usage_share` what the Bus brings is not enough, because the idle node does not appear in the stream (§3.12.2). The Network Monitor enumerates the live nodes by reading the Active Node Registry (§3.11), scoped to its partition by the `topic` label that registry carries, and checks the assignments observed in `usage_window` against that roster. The label is also what leaves structural nodes out with no list of exceptions (§3.11). It is the same pattern it already uses for network state—reading keys of a registry, not adding a new axis over the Bus—and it does not take it off the non-critical path: if the Network Monitor is down, the network simply does not collect garbage for a while.
* **Score:** a value in `[0, 1]` that is a decreasing function of `reopen_rate` (§3.12.2) and of nothing else, computed over the observation window (`quality_window`) and actionable from a minimum of observations (`min_observations`), Global Configuration parameters (§2.3). The quality constraint is `score ≥ score_floor` (§3.12.2), and it is read by the Network Monitor, for `low_score`, and by the Spawner, to compare (§3.13.4). The formula belongs to the trigger logic; the `score` and the `margin` travel already computed in the vector (§3.12.3), so the Spawner never evaluates it.

  **`quality_window` is counted in the node's observations, not in time:** it is the last `quality_window` tasks the node closed, among those that occurred within `evidence_staleness` (§2.3). The window being a count is what makes `n_observations` and the score be computed over the same set **by construction**, with no need to declare it separately: there is no state in which a node reads as mature and actionable with a score computed over an already empty window, because an empty window has no observations to count.

  **`evidence_staleness` does not slide the window: it trims it.** A node that worked a lot months ago and is idle today keeps its observations until they age, and when they do the count falls below `min_observations` and the node becomes **not evaluable**—not badly scored. The distinction is what sets the direction of the error: what the staleness of evidence produces is abstention by the Spawner (§3.13.4), never an archiving. The same bound limits how much evidence the aggregate keeps for each node (above).

  **`avg_attempts` does not take part in the score**: it lives on the cost axis (§3.12.2). It measures how much work it took to reach the result, not whether the result is good; inside the score it would be a magnitude of the *objective* placed inside the *constraint*, and duplicated besides, because that work is already counted in `cost_subtree`.

  **`usage_share` is deliberately left out of the score.** Quality and usefulness are orthogonal axes—does what the node produces serve? vs. does anyone need it?—and mixing them breaks both readings: an excellent, rarely summoned node would come out badly scored, and a mediocre, often summoned one would come out well scored. Usage is evaluated separately, with its own signals over `usage_window` (§3.12.2) and its own trigger, `low_usage` (§3.12.3). It carries no declared startup grace because it does not need one—every freshly provisioned node has zero usage by construction, including the blue/green candidate of §3.13.5, and what keeps it from being archived before it has had the chance to win its first task is that its opportunity window is still incomplete and the metric therefore undefined. Once the window is complete, what sustains it while the trial stays open is the exception in the preamble of §3.13.4, and the ceiling becomes the maturation expiry of §3.13.5.

**Exploitation and exploration: why triggering on evidence is not enough.** Everything above is local descent over an observed gradient—the Network Monitor moves when a node's signal shows something is wrong. That leaves an entire class of improvements structurally blind:

> **The cost-quality frontier moves for causes exogenous to the network**—a better model comes out, prices change, the runtime improves—**and no observation of a node can reveal it, because the node did not change.** Its signals are identical before and after the world moved under it.

A node that was flawless six months ago may today be the most expensive in the network measured against what *would be possible*, and its `score`, its `reopen_rate` and its `cost_own_p50` will all say it is perfect. The only way to find out is to try without prior evidence. That is why the Network Monitor also emits **routine reviews** (`routine`, §3.12.3) on nodes that triggered nothing: it is not a luxury; it is the half of the cycle that would otherwise be missing entirely.

**And that is why it is better to trigger too much than too little.** The asymmetry is strong and worth stating, because it is what authorizes the rest of this section: an unnecessary `node_review` costs **one Spawner cycle and one neighborhood query, without provisioning anything** (§3.13.4), and it closes with `no_action` (§3.13.5); a node that is never reviewed costs **the improvement, forever**. Discrimination then does not lie in being conservative when triggering, but in the payload being enough for the Spawner to discard cheaply (§3.12.3).

**A single emission queue, with a bounded rate.** What does need bounding is not the sensitivity but the flow, and for a concrete reason: **the cost trigger has correlated failures**. If a model's price goes up, the cost of every node implemented with it goes up at once, and the Network Monitor would emit a burst over half the fleet that would drown the Spawner—which is also handling vacancy (§3.13.2). It is the widest case and not the only one: a latency drift caused by an egress destination reaches every node that uses it, and a niche with too many nodes fires `low_usage` on several at once. The answer is a single piece, which bounds the flow whatever trigger produces it:

> **Every `node_review` emission of a Network Monitor instance goes through a queue with a rate cap `review_emission_rate`** (Global Configuration, §2.3), local to its partition, and a fraction `exploration_share` of that flow is **reserved** for exploration (`routine`, §3.12.3). The order in which what is waiting goes out belongs to the trigger logic.

* **The reserve is not a calibration detail.** Without it, a burst of evidence triggers would consume the entire flow for its whole duration and exploration would drop to zero right when the world has just moved—that is, exactly when it is most useful.
* **The Network Monitor's output rate is bounded by construction**, whatever happens outside. That is what keeps standing the limit of this section—it notifies, it never enforces—also in the worst case.

**Eligibility comes from the snooze expiring, not from a separate schedule.** A node returns to the queue when the `snooze_until` the Spawner stamped on it when closing its last review (§3.13.5) is used up—in new observations of the node or in admission time, depending on what that snooze is waiting for (below). The Network Monitor keeps no new record: that deadline is already the contract between the two and already travels in the `task.completed` that closes each `node_review`, from which the aggregate takes it like any other observation. If that event falls in an observation gap, the damage is a redundant review, which is what the asymmetry above already declared cheap.

**The Network Monitor's windows do not measure time.** The requirement that governs them:

> **No interval in which the network could not generate evidence about a node counts against that node.**

A paused network (§3.18) and an `open` network nobody uses compromise it equally: in both the entire fleet stops working without any node having changed, and a window denominated in time slides just the same. The rule that satisfies it:

> **Each node-lifecycle magnitude is denominated in the same currency as the cause that generates it.** Evidence about a node is counted in **observations of the node**; the occasion to generate it, in **assignments granted on its topic**; and what depends on the outside world or on the cost of having a node up, in **wall-clock time gated by admission** (§2.3).

Under that denomination a pause is the particular case of zero demand: no window moves during it **by construction** and not because a mechanism freezes it, and there is no resumption logic because there is nothing to resume.

**What is denominated in time is gated against admission:**

> **`admission_clock`**—a monotonic clock that advances along with wall-clock time **only while `network_admission = open`**, and stays stopped in the other states. The deferred-classification `grace_period`, the no-demand period of `no_demand` (§3.12.3) and the exploration backoff the Spawner stamps, `no_action_snooze` with its ceiling `max_exploration_backoff`, are evaluated against it.

Each one is there for its own reason. The `grace_period` waits for a reopen, which is latency of the requester but cannot arrive if the network does not admit. The `no_demand` period measures holding cost, which accrues just the same during a pause; it is gated because archiving the fleet for a lack of demand the pause itself produces is the expensive error, and whoever paused already accepted that cost. And the exploration backoff waits for the world to move—new models, prices—which is exogenous to the network and therefore not denominated in its traffic; what sustains the gate is that the Spawner must not provision candidates during the vector space migration (§3.13.7).

**`evidence_staleness` is the exception and runs in wall-clock time.** A long pause ages the accumulated evidence, and that is correct: the age of a datum is a fact about the datum and not about the node's merit. Its only effect is to make it **not evaluable**—abstention, never an archiving.

**It adds no state or channel.** Properties already written sustain it: `network_admission` belongs to the current class (§2.3), so the Network Monitor reads it from the latest snapshot in its own cycle, without subscribing to anything; pausing and unpausing is a `config_change` (§3.18) over immutable snapshots, numbered and stamped with their publication time (§2.3), so **the history of pauses is already recorded in the only component that is authoritative over it**; and the Network Monitor is the only consumer of its own windows, so the clock does not need to be shared, or consistent with anyone, or travel in the envelope. It materializes it as a local accumulator and rebuilds it entirely from the Config Store's sequence of versions if it restarts, so it does not store it with the aggregate.

**The Network Monitor being partitioned adds nothing to this, and in fact reinforces it.** The N instances do not have to agree on the clock: each derives it from the same immutable sequence, numbered and stamped with publication time, so they coincide **by construction** and not by coordination. It is the same property that makes embedding regeneration idempotent (§3.13.3)—a pure function of a shared input needs no owner—and it is also what makes an instance that inherits a range start with the right clock without receiving it from anyone. The counts of observations and of assignments are local to the topic and therefore to the partition, so they have no one to disagree with either.

**`drain` needs no separate rule.** The network is emptying on purpose and its traffic decays toward zero by design; since the usage axis is a ratio whose denominator is the assignments granted, numerator and denominator decay together and the indicator stays where it was. The observations that do occur during the draining—and those of the topics exempt under §3.18—are ingested normally, because they are real observations.

#### 3.12.2. Observable signals

**The network's objective function defines the shape of the measurement, and it is worth writing it down before the signals:**

> **The network minimizes cost subject to `score ≥ score_floor`.** Quality is a **constraint**, not something to maximize; cost is what is optimized. `score_floor` is a Global Configuration parameter (§2.3).

It follows that the unit of evaluation of a node is not a number but **a point on a (quality, cost) plane with a constraint line**, and that a node can be wrong in two ways that look nothing alike: violating the constraint (insufficient) or satisfying it at excessive cost (extravagant). A cycle that only detects the former corrects insufficiency and **never corrects extravagance**—an expensive, excellent node gets a perfect score and stays forever. Measuring both is what turns the node lifecycle (§3.13) into an optimization instead of a failure detector.

**Aggregation invariant.** Before listing them, the rule that governs them all:

> Every signal is **a ratio or a position statistic**, always over a window denominated in the currency of its cause (§3.12.1). **No lifetime accumulator, and no rate against time.**

The ban on rates is the flip side of the denomination of §3.12.1: a rate measures a numerator against a denominator that runs on its own, whereas in a ratio, when nothing happens, neither side moves.

The point about accumulators is not tidiness: a metric of either of those shapes **is bounded by construction and empties itself**, so it needs no reset mechanism or declared cap. The alternative—monotonic counters—has a failure mode of its own, and a silent one: *any* sufficiently used node eventually crosses a threshold on an accumulated total, so the trigger would end up measuring popularity instead of improvability. Where something has to accumulate anyway, it follows the shape of `max_successive_candidates` (§3.13.5): a declared cap and an explicit reset event.

None requires adding a field to the envelope (§3.8.1) or that whoever observes it understand the content of the task: they are structural patterns over events that already cross the Bus, plus the `data.metrics` fields that §3.10 already makes mandatory.

**The axes are orthogonal and are never averaged.** `score` is the **constraint**, cost is the **objective**, usage is the **relevance filter**. It is the same argument by which §3.12.1 leaves `usage_share` out of the score formula, extended to the missing axis: a single "value" index would tie a terrible, very cheap node with an excellent, expensive one, which is precisely the comparison the objective function above declares invalid.

**Quality axis—the constraint:**

* **External reopen rate (`reopen_rate`):** of the tasks a node closed with `task.completed`, what fraction its requester later reopened—a `task.announced` whose `previous_attempt` names that task, §3.3—within `quality_window`. It is the most direct signal that the requester did not trust the result (§3.9.2). It counts only the reopen published by the same platform identity that published the reopened attempt (§3.14.1): the requester's Integration Sidecar already validates it (§3.6.1), and the Network Monitor checks it over events it already observes, without trusting the announcement.
* **`score`**, derived from the previous signal (§3.12.1), and **`margin` = `score` − `score_floor`**: the distance to the constraint. The margin is not decoration on the score—it is what says **how much quality is available to trade for cost**, and without it the "healthy with slack" quadrant cannot be told apart from "healthy and at the limit".

**Cost axis—the objective.** It is measured over the `cost` of `data.metrics` (§3.10), which the network collects mandatorily and which no other decision consumes:

* **`cost_own_p50` and `cost_own_p90`:** median and 90th percentile of the node's own cost in the window. **Median and not mean**, because cost distributions are heavy-tailed and a mean lets a single runaway task dominate the signal of an otherwise cheap node.
* **`cost_subtree_p50` and `cost_subtree_p90`:** median and 90th percentile of the cost of the subtree the node opened under the same `TraceID`, with the same median-not-mean criterion. **They are metrics distinct from the previous ones and are never added to them.** `cost_own` decides about the node's internals; `cost_subtree` decides about **its planning**, which is a question no other signal answers: an orchestrator that costs fractions of a cent and hands out fifty dollars of subtasks looks impeccable on every other signal. Keeping them separate is what avoids the symmetric error—if cost were the subtree's and nothing else, every decomposing node would look very expensive and every decision would blame the wrong node. It is derivable with no new fields: the Network Monitor sees the whole Bus and parentage is reconstructed by `TraceID` plus the emitter of the `task.announced`. **And §3.9.1 is what makes it computable**: since nobody closes before its children, the subtree is complete at the instant of the parent's terminal event.
* **`cost_per_resolution_own` and `cost_per_resolution_subtree`:** the cost of the node's attempts in `quality_window` divided by those that closed clean—its `task.completed` events that nobody reopened. **Each attempt bears its own cost**, whichever node won the other attempts of its lineage (§3.3): the numerator adds up all the node's attempts, including its `task.failed` events, and the denominator counts only the clean ones. Whoever fails pays with its own cost and with its `reopen_rate`, never with the cost of the attempt that fixed it. The two variants differ only in which cost the numerator adds up—`cost_own` or `cost_subtree`—and they are kept separate for the same reason as those two. Numerator and denominator count the same set of attempts, with each `task.completed` already classified after the `grace_period` (§3.12.1): an unclassified attempt in the numerator would count against the node with more recent work. It is a ratio of sums, and therefore a mean: the heavy tail the median leaves out of `cost_own_p50` comes in here whole. These are the metrics that put quality and cost in the same currency without collapsing them—a cheap node that gets reopened three times **is not cheap**. When the denominator is less than `min_observations` they are **undefined, not infinite**, and they are reported as absent; the Spawner reads the absence as "not evaluable", just as it already does with an empty neighborhood (§3.13.4, step 2).
* **Self-correction cost (`avg_attempts`):** when the node acted as requester of its own subtasks—typically verification ones (§3.9.2)—the mean final `current_attempts` it took to close them before it could emit its own `task.completed`. It tells apart a node that gets it right the first time from one that "comes out well" but after several internal retries. **It lives on this axis and not on the quality one**: it measures how much work it took to reach the result, not whether the result is good—and that work is already paid for and counted in `cost_subtree`.
* **Dispersion (`cost_dispersion` = `cost_own_p90` / `cost_own_p50`):** a node whose cost is bimodal is a node whose **region is heterogeneous**—some tasks it solves in one go, others it grinds through. **Heterogeneous is not broad, and the two properties are orthogonal:** breadth is geometric—the radius `similarity_floor` governs (§3.13.4)—and heterogeneity is economic—the variance of cost *within* the region, whether wide or narrow. A narrow niche can be heterogeneous and a wide one can be homogeneous; keeping them separate is what keeps splitting a niche from swallowing the floor movement, or the reverse (§3.13.4, §3.13.9). It is a cheap proxy for where it would pay to split a niche, and the reading it enables differs from all the others: high dispersion with a healthy median does not ask to make the node cheaper; it asks to **bound it**. It is always measured and always travels; when it is actionable is decided by the Spawner's strategy (§3.13.4), and what governs every split lives in §3.13.9. How the `capability_text` of each half is derived when splitting a niche is node-creation procedure (§6).
* **`spend_window`:** the node's accumulated spend in the window. **It is not a performance measure but a measure of the size of the prize**—how much there is to gain if it is improved—and that is why its role is one of **priority and never of trigger** (§3.12.1). If it were a threshold, any sufficiently used node would end up reviewed for being popular instead of improvable, which is exactly the failure mode the aggregation invariant exists to avoid.

**The trigger always measures cost and always duration; the objective, in contrast, is exclusive—and the two things should not be conflated.**

Triggering is *"something changed, look at this node"*: it requires no trade-off, only each metric's drift against the node's own history (§3.12.3). There is no reason to turn off that trigger on latency just because the node also has a monetary cost—a deterministic node (§3.6.3) with zero `cost` can be perfectly improvable, one that takes forty seconds where it could take two wastes a real resource, and the same holds for a paid node that also became slow. That is why the cost axis always carries both metrics, with no predicate choosing between them:

* **`duration_p50` and `duration_p90`:** median and 90th percentile of the node's duration in the window, with the same median-not-mean criterion as `cost_own_p50/p90` and for the same reason (heavy tail). They are always measured, whether or not the node has a monetary cost.
* **`duration_dispersion`** (`duration_p90` / `duration_p50`): it plays on the time axis the same role `cost_dispersion` plays on the money axis, with the same reading—heterogeneity of the region, not breadth—and the same destination (§3.13.4, §3.13.9).

Always measuring both also makes **the predicate disappear instead of getting more complicated**: the Network Monitor never needs to know whether it is looking at a semantic or a deterministic node—which would mean looking inside the node (§2.2)—and hybrids, mostly deterministic with some paid call, fall on the right side on their own, because the Integration Sidecar measured that call and it does not depend on the blueprint having declared it (§3.10). `duration_ms` is available for every node without exception—including one that calls no one—because it is the only `metrics` field that needs no external call to exist; `cost`, on the other hand, can legitimately be zero, and zero is a complete measurement, not an absence.

**The objective is still a single one.** The objective function above—minimize cost subject to `score ≥ score_floor`—does not gain a second term: adding `duration` as an objective would require a weight—how many units of `cost_unit` a second is worth—that nobody can calibrate without inventing it, and that is exactly the collapse of axes the previous paragraph already forbids. **Which of the two metrics is a node's objective is decided by the Spawner (§3.13.4)**: the vector always carries cost and duration, and the Network Monitor does not choose between them.

**Why latency matters even when there is a monetary cost:** `duration` is capacity consumption, not a convenience. A node busy for ten minutes occupies a slot for ten minutes, and the capacity rule of §3.6.1 makes a busy node not propose—latency produces vacancy (§3.13.2), and vacancy produces provisioning of more instances. A slow node costs resources even if it costs no tokens; it simply charges them in infrastructure instead of in `cost_unit`. Truly unifying that would require putting a price on infrastructure, and this architecture deliberately does not model it (transport identity and everything that hangs on it is delegated to the deployment platform, §3.14.1)—that is why the trigger always measures both metrics, but the objective stays exclusive.

**Diagnostic axis—it triggers nothing; it says what to change.** The triggers live on the axes above; these signals exist so that the `node_review` arrives with the direction inside and not just with the alarm:

* **`tokens_in_p50` and `tokens_out_p50`.** `cost` answers *how much it costs the network* and **moves with the market, which is correct**: if the price of the model a node is implemented with goes down, the node *is* cheaper and the network really spends less; if it goes up, the node became expensive without having changed, and it is worth trying an equivalent one on another model. Tokens answer the other half—*how much work is being done internally*—which is the only one of the two things that is fixed by rewriting the blueprint. Crossing them disambiguates:

  | `cost` | tokens | Reading | Direction |
  | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
  | up | flat | the price changed | same node, cheaper model or provider |
  | up | up | the node works more inside | simplify: fewer cycles, shorter context |
  | flat | up | the price dropped and swallowed the improvement | real degradation, masked by the market |

  And within "works more inside", the `tokens_out`/`tokens_in` ratio separates those causes, because §3.10 accumulates all internal calls into a single total: **high `tokens_in` with low `tokens_out`** is one large call—long prompt, inflated context—; **both high** is many internal cycles, where each round resends the context and produces output.
* **`duration_p50` / `duration_p90`**, which also triangulate the above—many cycles take time, a large prompt not so much—besides living on the cost axis in their own right (above): always as part of the trigger, and as the objective when the Spawner chooses it (§3.13.4).
* **`requester_queries_per_task` and `egress_gates_per_task`:** human queries the node opened per closed task in `quality_window`, separated by their origin (§3.5.2). The first counts those its Logic Container asked of `requester`; the second, the egress gates it triggered, toward any audience. The `kind = gate_approval` the Integration Sidecar stamps on the gate separates them. The node waits for each query, so it already weighs on its duration; these signals say where that duration comes from. They are not enough to blame the node: many queries to `requester` can be due to ambiguous requests, and many gates to an operator rule (§3.16.2).

**Radial axis—where, within its radius, the node performs.** The quality and cost signals aggregate all of the node's tasks, whether they fell near or far from its `capability_text`. These separate them by distance:

* **`reopen_rate_inner` and `reopen_rate_outer`, `cost_own_p50_inner` and `cost_own_p50_outer`, `duration_p50_inner` and `duration_p50_outer`:** `reopen_rate`, `cost_own_p50` and `duration_p50` computed separately over two groups of the tasks of `quality_window`. The outer group is the fraction `outer_share` (§2.3) with the lowest similarity between the task's `task_embedding` and the node's embedding; the inner one, the rest. It is derivable with no new fields: the `task_embedding` travels in the announcement (§3.7.4) and the node's embedding in the credential of its proposal (§3.7.5, step 4). When the outer group has fewer than `min_observations` tasks, they are all undefined.
* **The cut is a fraction of the tasks.** That way both groups have observations whatever the node's floor, and what is compared is the direction in which its performance changes as it moves away from the center, which is what moves the floor (§3.13.4).

**It separates breadth from heterogeneity** (`cost_dispersion`, above): a dispersion that grows with distance from the center is one of breadth, and narrowing the floor corrects it (§3.13.4); one that does not follow it belongs to the region, and splitting the niche corrects it (§3.13.9).

**Usefulness axis—the relevance filter:**

* **Share in its niche (`usage_share`):** of the last `usage_window` assignments granted on the node's specialty `topic` (Global Configuration, §2.3), what fraction it won. It measures usefulness, not quality: it tells apart the node the network needs from the one that stayed resident without anyone summoning it. Measuring assignments won and not proposals covers two cases with a single signal: the hyper-specific node created for a one-off task that outlives its purpose, and the duplicate that always proposes but always loses against a better-matched neighbor (§3.7.5)—both are redundant coverage consuming resources. **"Always proposes" is part of the description and not a detail:** proposing requires being free (§3.6.1), so the redundant duplicate is by construction one that is idle, and that is what separates it from the node of a replicated niche with demand, which also wins a fraction but because it is busy and not because it loses. The fraction alone does not tell them apart; `idle_share` does.

  **The denominator is assignments granted and not tasks announced**, because during a pause nodes keep announcing and nothing is awarded (§3.18): counting awards, the denominator stops along with the network and the ratio stays where it was, with no need for any clock.

  **The scope is the specialty topic and not the embedding neighborhood**, even though the neighborhood is the more precise reference class. The division of capabilities rules: the Network Monitor measures each node against itself and the Spawner compares it against its neighbors (§3.12.3), so a per-neighborhood denominator would give the Monitor a new capability that §3.13.1 denies it. The topic, moreover, is what its partition already observes.

  **An incomplete window is undefined, not zero.** As long as `usage_window` assignments have not been granted on the topic since the node was provisioned, the metric **is reported as absent** and the Spawner reads it as "not evaluable", just as with `cost_per_resolution_own` and `cost_per_resolution_subtree` or with an empty neighborhood (§3.13.4, step 2). It is also what covers the startup of every freshly provisioned node without needing a declared grace: not yet having had the chance to win anything and having an incomplete opportunity window are the same proposition.
* **Idleness in its niche (`idle_share`):** of the same `usage_window` assignments, what fraction was granted to another node while N was free. Faced with each assignment of its topic, N is in one of three states—it won it, it was busy, or it was free and another won it—so `usage_share`, `idle_share` and the fraction in which it was busy add up to one. It is the signal that separates the node that is surplus from the one that cannot keep up: the redundant duplicate is free when the network awards, the node of a saturated niche is busy.

  **N being free is observable without looking inside the node**, because a node works one task at a time (§3.6.1): it is busy from the `task.assigned` that names it until the terminal event of that task, or until the task returns to `UNASSIGNED` (§3.1). It carries the same window and the same incomplete-window treatment as `usage_share`.
* **`win_rate`:** proposals emitted against assignments won. The Network Monitor already observes both events, so it costs no new subscription. It does not replace `usage_share` but decomposes it: since the quantities nest—topic assignments ≥ node proposals ≥ assignments won—**`usage_share` = (proposals ÷ topic assignments) × `win_rate`** holds, and each factor names a different diagnosis. Low usage with low `win_rate` is a **badly sized** node, which proposes where it does not belong. Low usage with high `win_rate` is a node that barely gets to compete, and the possible causes are: the network rarely needs it, its `similarity_floor` is silencing it before the auction (§3.7.5, step 3)—the floor with which an archived entry is revived hangs on that reading (§3.13.2)—or **it is saturated**: busy almost always, it proposes little and wins almost everything it proposes (§3.6.1). They are opposite diagnoses that look identical without this signal, and `idle_share` separates saturation from the other causes: the saturated node is busy when the network awards, and under the other causes it is free.

  **A replicated niche is read with `idle_share`, without a saturation axis or a census of replicas.** Several identical nodes on the same niche split the assignments, so each one's `usage_share` is worth a fraction of what a single one would have—but the fraction comes from two opposite causes. If the niche is saturated, each node spends most of the assignments busy and its `idle_share` stays low: there are too many tasks, not too many nodes. If instead there are more nodes than work, almost all of them are free when the network awards and each one's `idle_share` rises: there, what is surplus is nodes. The capacity rule of §3.6.1 is what makes the difference observable.

**This axis is the only one that needs a roster of subjects.** The other signals are ratios or statistics over events the node produced; low usage is an *absence* of events: the node that receives no task emits nothing and does not enter the aggregate—the case that matters most to clean up is the one the stream does not show. That is why the subjects are enumerated against the Active Node Registry (§3.11) and not against what is observed. The denominator, on the other hand, does come from the stream like everything else: it is the topic's `task.assigned` events, which the instance that owns that partition already sees.

**Provenance axis—a contract violation, not a rate:**

* **`notice_provenance`:** of the `notice` events observed within `evidence_staleness`—the quality window is counted in closed tasks and a `notice` is not one—whether the `source` corresponds to an entry of the Active Node Registry (§3.11) instead of to a structural component. No new data is needed: it is the same registry the usage axis already reads for its roster of subjects, against the same broad subscription to the Bus the Network Monitor already holds (§3.12.1). By contract (§3.7.7, §3.8.2) no emergent node is licensed to emit a `notice`, regardless of the topic or the content—so the signal does not need to tell a false notice from a merely improper one: the emission itself is already the violation.
* **It does not wait for `min_observations` or for a complete opportunity window.** It is a deliberate departure from the rest of this axis, and it is worth marking as such: `reopen_rate` is a ratio that needs volume not to be confused with noise, but here there is no statistical reading to make—a single occurrence is already fully diagnostic, because the rule it violates is binary and admits no grey areas. It fires on the first one.

**Resource axis—a platform fact, not a rate:**

* **`memory_kills`:** the times the platform recorded, within `evidence_staleness`, the death of N's Logic Container for exceeding its memory reservation (§3.14.1, §3.13.10). It comes from the platform's record and not from the Bus, by the same path through which the Network Monitor already reads the Active Node Registry for its roster (§3.12.1); no node reports it.
* **It fires on the first one**, like `notice_provenance`: a death by memory repeats on every task that asks for the same thing, and waiting for volume means losing those tasks.

#### 3.12.3. `node_review` and `node_query`: the trigger and the query

When any of the signals of §3.12.2 indicates an opportunity for improvement, the Network Monitor does not act directly on the node's lifecycle—that is still the exclusive responsibility of the Spawner (§3.13.1)—; it publishes one more task, following the same pattern already established for `trace_query` (§3.10):

* `task.announced` on a dedicated topic, `node_review` (§3.8), with `data.node_id` (the node in question), `target_topic`—the topic of its specialty (§3.7.7)—`n_observations`, `trigger_reason` and **the full vector of signals of §3.12.2**, plus the node's reopens in the window grouped by requester. That grouping is diagnostic, with no weight and no trigger: a requester that contradicts the others about the same nodes is the fingerprint of the concerted reopening that §3.3 leaves as a residual risk. The evaluated node travels in `data` and not in `subject` because a node identifier is neither a `TaskID` nor a `TraceID` (§3.8.1); `subject` identifies the review task itself, which is what later lets it be closed and correlated.

  **The `trigger_reason` values.** Each one is an assertion about node N, and the assertion decides which rule the Spawner applies to it (§3.13.4). **That meaning, plus the full vector, is the contract between the two components.** Which signals and thresholds detect each one is the Network Monitor's trigger logic, which belongs to the implementation; its parameters live in the Global Configuration, declared by that logic (§2.3).

  | `trigger_reason` | Axis | What it asserts about N | Spawner rule (§3.13.4) |
  | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
  | `low_score` | quality | **It violates the quality constraint**: its results are reopened more than the network admits. | disqualification |
  | `notice_provenance` | provenance | **It violated its contract**: it emitted a `notice`, which no emergent node can emit (§3.7.7). | archiving |
  | `memory_exhausted` | resource | **It died for exceeding its memory reservation** (§3.14.1). | raising the reservation (§3.13.10) |
  | `high_cost` | cost | **Its cost rose** relative to its own history. | tie-break by confidence |
  | `high_latency` | cost | **Its duration rose** relative to its own history. | tie-break by confidence |
  | `outer_gradient` | radius | **It performs worse at the edge of its radius than near the center**, in quality, in cost or in duration. | narrowing the floor |
  | `low_usage` | usage | **It is little used**: its topic has demand, and N spends most of it free while other nodes serve it. | archiving or widening the floor, depending on `win_rate` |
  | `no_demand` | usage | **Its niche has no demand**: the network awards no work on its topic. | archiving |
  | `routine` | — | **Nothing**: it is budgeted exploration on a node with no indications (§3.12.1). | tie-break by confidence |

  **Every assertion is measured in the currency of its cause (§3.12.1).** "Most" in `low_usage` is counted over its topic's assignments, and "its own history" in `high_cost` and `high_latency`, like the edge and center of `outer_gradient`, over the node's observations; none is counted in wall-clock time. `no_demand` is the only one denominated in time, gated by admission, because it is motivated by the cost of having a node up, which accrues in time and not in traffic (§3.12.1).

  **A saturated node does not fire `low_usage`, and neither does the only node of a topic.** The saturated one is busy when the network awards, and the only one receives everything that is awarded while it is free. That is what upholds the Spawner archiving on `low_usage`: what the trigger points at is surplus, and other nodes already serve its topic's demand.

  **A `node_review` carries a single `trigger_reason`.** If N meets several assertions at once, the trigger logic decides which one travels, and the full vector carries the evidence of the others anyway. With one condition: an evaluable node that violates the constraint does not travel as `high_cost`, `high_latency`, `outer_gradient` or `routine`, because the Spawner reads from the trigger's word which rule to compare it by (§3.13.4).

  **`high_cost` and `high_latency` compare N against its own history and not against an abstractly high cost or duration**, and the choice is not one of convenience: "expensive" or "slow" mean nothing without a reference class, and the only legitimate reference—the embedding neighborhood—is computed by the Spawner and not by the Network Monitor, which is the division of capabilities §3.13.1 already fixes between the two. The division is then clean: **the Network Monitor measures each node against itself, the Spawner compares it against its neighbors** when processing the review, with the search it already does in §3.13.4. Neither gains a new capability. A node that was always expensive, or always slow, and never drifted, is reached anyway through `routine`.

  **`high_latency` is a trigger of its own and not a variant of `high_cost`.** They are two distinct facts—cost and duration are axes §3.12.2 always measures separately, never averaged—so they carry two names. `high_latency` fires even if cost is the node's current objective—it does not wait for cost to drop to zero—because the trigger always measures both metrics and never depends on which of them is the objective.

  **Many latency drifts have a cause outside the node**—a slow egress destination, for example—and no Spawner candidate fixes them, just as with any other trigger whose cause is not in the node (§3.13.4, §3.13.5): the trigger happens anyway, the review closes `no_action` or the candidate does not beat N, and the backoff spaces out the next exploration (§3.13.5). If the cause ends up making N violate the constraint, it arrives through `low_score` and follows the replacement path, which does escalate as `niche_uncoverable` and carries the cause to the human.

  **`outer_gradient` compares N against itself in space**, as `high_cost` and `high_latency` do in time: the outer group of its radial axis against the inner one (§3.12.2). Each axis is compared against itself, so the trigger does not mix quality with cost and does not need the objective function to assert anything. What it asserts is that the edge is worse than the center, not that it is bad: how much worse is enough to let it go is fixed by the trigger logic's threshold, and that threshold is what decides how specialized the network's nodes end up.

  **The payload carries the whole vector whatever the trigger, not just the signals that lit up.** The `trigger_reason` says what lit up; to decide *in which direction* to move—a more sophisticated node, a cheaper one, a more bounded one—the Spawner needs the complete plane, including the axes that are healthy. It is also what lets it close `no_action` with no extra work, which is the counterpart of triggering generously (§3.12.1).

  **`no_demand` is the flip side of `low_usage`, and the usage axis needs both because they assert different facts:** `low_usage` is *there was demand and others served it*, `no_demand` is *there was no demand*. The distinction has to be explicit because `low_usage` does not see that a niche went dark: without assignments its window does not advance—the right protection against idleness—and what remains in it describes a demand that no longer exists.

  **`notice_provenance` and `memory_exhausted` fire on the first occurrence**, without waiting for `min_observations` or a complete opportunity window (§3.12.2).

  **No `node_review` is emitted on a `hitl_contact_*` channel.** A User-Proxy answers queries on behalf of people and its performance is not compared against neighbors, so there is no replacement or split to decide; and archiving it cannot be undone, because archiving rests on a vacancy reviving the blueprint (§3.13.4) and on this family the Spawner does not revive (§3.13.2). It also holds for `no_demand`: a channel without demand keeps running until the operator removes it (§3.13.8). The channel is still measured, and the point form of `node_query` reaches it.
* **Deterministic routing, not an open auction:** just as the Tracing Collector selects itself for `trace_query` by evaluating the announcement's trace against its range (§3.10), the Spawner selects itself for `node_review` by evaluating `data.target_topic` against the same range it already uses for vacancy (§3.13.2). Only the instance that owns that key proposes; the Task Broker assigns trivially.
* The Spawner closes the cycle with `task.completed` on the review task, describing what action it took (§3.13.4)—normal Bus traffic, so it is traced by the general Tracing Collector like any other flow.

**`node_query`: the reverse path.** `node_review` is a push from the Network Monitor toward the Spawner; the pull is missing. The Spawner uses it, on a specific node (§3.13.4), and so does the operator, on the fleet (§3.17.1). It is one more fixed-convention topic (§3.7.7), with the same request/response pattern as `trace_query` (§3.10) and **different forms depending on the payload**, because partitioning already distributes the data:

* **Point**—`data.node_id` plus the topic of its specialty in `data.target_topic`. **It applies only to nodes provisioned by the Spawner**, which are the only ones that have a specialty: a structural node does not claim a topic by similarity (§2.1), is not in the usage-axis roster (§3.12.1) and has no signals to return, so the point form on one is not defined and the Network Monitor rejects it. It is not a limitation: nothing this query informs (§3.13.4) is ever decided about a structural node, whose lifecycle belongs to the deployment platform and not to the Spawner. Deterministic routing by `data.target_topic`, just like `node_review`: only the instance that owns that topic in its ring proposes, because the Network Monitor's aggregate belongs to its partition and the signals exist nowhere else. **It returns the same vector as the `node_review` payload**, and for the same reason: whoever queries a point is about to make one of the decisions of §3.13.4 and needs the full axes, not one. It is still a lightweight payload and travels directly in the `task.completed`.
* **Census**—without `node_id`. It is answered by reading only the Active Node Registry (§3.11)—the same store from which the usage axis gets its roster of subjects (§3.12.1)—so any Network Monitor instance is equally competent and it is resolved by auction without similarity, the same argument §3.7.6 already uses for `topic_catalog_query`. It returns the fleet with the fields that registry carries (§3.11). There is no fan-out and the requester does not need to know `N`. The listing is heavy, so it goes by Claim-Check to the Context Store (§3.4) and the `task.completed` carries the reference, like `trace_query`.

  **The census lists what is deployed**, which is what is in the store it reads: a blueprint that exists but nobody runs has no row in the Registry. `node_catalog_query` enumerates the whole library (§3.13.3).

  **`model_id`, `signer_id` and `similarity_floor` are those of the running node's credential**—never those of the blueprint's entry in the Catalog, which carries a `model_id` of its own and measures something else (§3.13.3). They travel because they are selection fields and not validity fields, so that a person decides about the fleet (§3.11); from the `model_id`, in particular, comes the tracking of a vector space migration's progress (§3.13.7).

**The census carries no score.** The signals of §3.12.2 are aggregates per partition (§3.12.1), so putting them in the census would force the answering instance to query the others—the fan-out this form avoids. Whoever sees something odd in the fleet drops down to the point form for that node.

There remains, then, a division in which each question has its store and its responder: **`node_catalog_query` says what exists, the census says what of that runs, and the point query says how what runs is doing.**

**No form of `node_query` asks a node anything.** It is the same rule as §3.10 and §3.9.2 taken all the way: the network admits no self-reporting, not even for inspection. The Network Monitor answers with what it observed and with what the substrate's registry says, neither of which depends on the cooperation of the evaluated node—a degraded or compromised node appears in the census whether it wants to or not, and has no way to improve its own entry. Nor is there any need to open a side channel to the Integration Sidecars.

The census's `model_id` and `signer_id` are not an exception to this, and that is what makes them trustworthy: the **Spawner** wrote them when provisioning (§3.11), not the node at startup. A node left behind in the migration cannot declare itself migrated, one that did not restart cannot appear as if it had, and none can hide which key it was signed with.

**It works with the network paused and with the vector space broken**, which is when it is needed most. With the network paused it works because it is in the table of §3.7.7, exempt from the pause (§3.18); with the vector space broken, because its claimant is structural and therefore does not compete by similarity (§3.7.5, step 4). None of those forms generates or compares embeddings.

**If there is no Network Monitor, the query expires.** The same treatment §3.10 gives the Tracing Collector: a timeout visible to the requester, which proceeds without that benefit. The distinction matters to whoever operates the network—an unanswered census means "there is no Network Monitor", never "there are no nodes"—and it does not change the limit of §3.12.1: if the Network Monitor is down, the notice and the inspection are lost, never the containment.

### 3.13. The Spawner: vacancy and node lifecycle

The Spawner is the only component that decides which nodes exist. This section is its complete home: §3.13.1 fixes its contract, §3.13.2 the path by which it creates, §3.13.3–§3.13.6 the path by which it improves the fleet—archiving, replacing and trying cheaper alternatives on the signals the Network Monitor delivers to it (§3.12)—and §3.13.7 the only work of its that decides nothing: migrating the active fleet when the operator changes the embedding model. When another section says "the Spawner decides X", the mechanism of that decision lives here.

#### 3.13.1. Spawner contract

**What it is.** A structural component (§2.1), partitioned by `topic` under the same consistent hashing scheme the Task Broker (§3.1) and the Tracing Collector (§3.10) use—its ring is its own, and its size is an agreement among Spawner instances that no other component needs to know (§3.19). It comes up with the network, before any domain topic exists, and from the first second there is an authoritative instance for any topic that comes to exist—that is why no special bootstrap step is needed (§5.1). A single instance owns each topic, so two Spawners never compete to create the same specialty. The only thing it creates on demand is *workers*: never itself, never the rest of the infrastructure.

**What it observes.** None of its inputs is privileged—the Spawner is not a special subscriber of the Bus and receives reports from no one:

* **Re-announcements of vacant tasks in its partition**, counted by passive observation of the stream, just as the Tracing Collector reconstructs spans without anyone reporting them to it (§3.2). The trigger comes from the Task Broker's own re-announcement backoff (§3.1), not from a notice addressed to it. Mechanism in §3.13.2.
* **`node_review` from the Network Monitor**, by deterministic routing of `data.target_topic` against that same range. Mechanism in §3.12.3.
* **The mismatch between the current `embedding_model` and the `model_id` of its active fleet's credentials.** The Spawner reads the parameter from the latest published version in its own cycle (§2.3) and checks it against the metadata it stamped itself when provisioning (§3.11). It is **passive detection, with no event**: nobody needs to notify it of a `config_change`, so "nobody subscribes to configuration changes" still holds—the same criterion as "the absence is the signal" of §3.15.7, with the difference that here the signal is an inequality and not an absence. Mechanism in §3.13.7.
* **Its own fleet, on each renewal of the presence mark** (§3.13.3): it lists in the Active Node Registry the instances of its partition, as in the cross-check of §3.13.4. It is a cycle of its own with the same criterion as the coverage lease of §3.15.7: if it stops, the mark expires on its own.
* **`node_catalog_publish` and the Operator Channel's requests on the fleet**—`node_provision`, `node_create`, `node_catalog_import` and `node_retire`—through the same deterministic routing of `data.target_topic` as `node_review`: the instance that owns the blueprint's topic is the only one that proposes. A request that publishes or brings in a bundle carries no `data.target_topic`, and any instance claims it (§3.13.6, §3.13.8). These are the inputs that originate in a person and not in the network's own operation, and that is why they are also the ones that arrive with an operator proof embedded (§3.14.2). Mechanisms in §3.13.6 and §3.13.8.

**What it decides.** The following decisions, and no others:

1. **Create or revive a node** on a genuine vacancy, or **fail the vacant task** when the problem is the specification and not the coverage, or when it is a human query no channel claims (§3.13.2).
2. **Archive a node**, or **replace it by blue/green** when there is no healthy neighbor to cover its niche (§3.13.4, §3.13.5).
3. **Mint a topic**, and only at the moment it is about to pay the real cost of creating a node for it (§3.7.6). Nodes propose topics; the Spawner decides which ones really exist.

The same limits cut across all of them: **it never edits a node in place**—it provisions a candidate with the same external contract and lets the auction decide (§3.13.4)—and **it never deletes on its own a Catalog entry or an artifact's image**, so that archiving is always reversible (§3.13.3). The image is deleted only at the operator's request, and the entry never (§3.13.8).

**What it produces.** Deciding to create a node and creating it are distinct acts. A provisioning produces, all at once and for a single node:

* **The deployment of the `artifact_ref`** against the orchestration substrate, with the **confinement spec** that declares the channels the node has and the **memory reservation** within which it runs (§3.14.1, §3.13.10).
* **A volume shared between the two halves**, which hosts the transport of the Node Runtime Interface (§3.6.1), and **a private volume of the Integration Sidecar**, which hosts the outbox's durable buffer and the deduplication by `id` on receipt (§3.6.4). The outbox stores envelopes with the capability credential and the scope token inside (§3.14.2, §3.14.3), and the Logic Container handles neither of them (§3.6.1): **the confinement the spec above declares only holds if the outbox stays outside what that container mounts.**
* **The node's platform identity** and the **signed capability credential**, both delivered to the Integration Sidecar in the same act (§3.14.1, §3.14.2).
* **The deployment metadata** that §3.11 enumerates, stamped next to the `artifact_ref`.
* **The row qualified by this network** in the Catalog entry (§3.13.3).

And it evaluates the preconditions **in that act and against the state its network has at that moment**: the Node Runtime Interface version within `runtime_interface_range`, each slot of the `egress_required` associated with an entry of the local `egress_registry` (§3.16.3), the artifact's `scan_result` at `pass` and issued under the current ruleset (§2.1), the `artifact_ref` with no operator veto in its network and no deletion mark (§3.13.8), and the embedding regenerated under its network's `embedding_model`. §3.13.3 argues why here and not when the artifact is registered.

**Enumerating the product does not add a fourth decision, and registering an artifact is not a Spawner decision at all.** The former for the same reason that re-signing, promoting and instantiating on request do not add one either: producing is executing what the list above already decided, not choosing anything. The latter is stronger—the act of registration does not choose which specialty exists, does not alter the active fleet and can happen in another network of the tenant without any Spawner of this one intervening (§3.13.3). The Spawner is the authority over which nodes **run**, never over which artifacts **are stored**.

**What it signs.** It is the only authority that generates a node's capability embedding (§3.7.3) and the only one that signs it, at provisioning time, in the credential the Integration Sidecar attaches to each `task.proposed` (§3.14.2). That signature is what lets the Task Broker award on a recomputed value instead of a self-declared one (§3.7.5, step 4). It signs with a key of its own, certified by the deployment, and what it signs **verifies against the root and not against it** (§3.14.2): a credential issued by this instance stays valid after the instance is redeployed or stops owning that topic. That is what decouples its lifecycle—and that of the ring—from the validity of the fleet it provisioned.

Because it is the only one that generates them, it is also **the only one that can regenerate and re-sign them** when the network's model changes (§2.3): that is where the repair of the vector space and the migration of the active fleet come from, both in §3.13.7. Neither adds a decision to the list above, and for the same reason. Regenerating an embedding recomputes a cache from the text that originated it. Re-signing is re-provisioning **the same `artifact_ref` with a new credential**—it does not choose which specialty exists, does not change the node's external contract and competes with nothing. Deciding which nodes exist is the list above, and nothing else.

**Promoting a blueprint to the Shared Catalog does not add a fourth either**, for the same reason taken one step further: here the Spawner does not even evaluate whether it is worth it. The decision is the operator's (§3.13.6, §3.17.1) and arrives already made, with its proof; what the Spawner does is verify the admissibility floors, assemble the payload from the Catalog and push it. It does not choose which specialty exists, does not change any node's external contract, does not alter the active fleet and competes with nothing. It is the same shape §3.14.2 gives to the credential of an audience channel—**it carries a proof it cannot fabricate**—applied to an act that goes out of the network instead of into it.

**The operator's requests on the fleet do not add one either**—instantiating, creating, bringing in, removing and vetoing (§3.13.8)—by the same argument: the decision arrives made and with its proof, and the Spawner verifies preconditions and executes. **The reach of the effect, on the other hand, is different:** those requests alter the active fleet or what can come to run, so they stay on the side of the authorities that authorize something inside the network (§3.17.1). Creating and bringing in end in the act of registration, which is not a Spawner decision (above); instantiating chooses which of the already stored entries comes up; removing executes an archiving the operator decided; and vetoing takes an entry out of what the network can bring up on its own.

**Raising a node's memory reservation does not add one either**, by the argument of re-signing: it re-provisions the same `artifact_ref` with more memory, without changing its external contract or competing with anything (§3.13.10).

Its signature **is not enough**, on the other hand, to enable a `hitl_contact_*` channel (§3.14.2): there the credential is only valid if it carries an embedded operator proof, which the Spawner can carry but not fabricate. The credential of the deployment's Operator Channel goes one step further and is not signed by the Spawner at all—it is the root, and it comes provisioned with the deployment. The network cannot mint for itself the authority that controls it.

**What it does not do.** It is the counterpart of the limit §3.12.1 sets for itself:

* **It does not arbitrate assignments.** When it takes part in an auction it does so as one more candidate, with sentinel similarity and with no dedicated rule in the Task Broker (§3.13.2).
* **It does not interpret text: it embeds it or carries it.** It is deterministic and invokes no generative model—the embedding model is a model, but one that does not follow instructions. What imposes this is being on the hot path of vacancy for every topic, partitioned (above), where an LLM would have a cost nobody bounded; and being the only authority that signs capability credentials ("What it signs", above), which turns any reader of text that can be influenced, with that key in hand, into an injection target with a very high prize. It works on embeddings and on structural patterns of the stream, never on what the task means.
* **It does not measure.** It consumes from the Network Monitor (§3.12.1) the quality, cost and usefulness signals already aggregated; the separation is deliberate, because whoever measures and whoever acts are distinct components. What it does do, and the Network Monitor does not, is **compare**: the embedding neighborhood against which it contrasts a node with its alternatives is its own (§3.13.4), and it is the half of the judgment no local measurement can give.
* **It does not decide what is published.** It does not sweep the Catalog looking for candidates to promote, or evaluate promotion when processing a `node_review`. Without a `node_catalog_publish` from the operator there is no promotion, even if a blueprint clears the floors comfortably (§3.13.6).
* **It does not watch itself.** Its liveness belongs to the deployment platform (§3.14.1), just like the Network Monitor's.

**What a topic changing owner costs it.** The ring resizes with load (§3.19), so the full inventory of what moves when a partition is split is worth having—and it is short, because §3.13.3 already distributed the Spawner's state in the only way that makes it indifferent to this:

* **Credentials, nothing.** They verify against the root and not against the signer (§3.14.2), so the inherited fleet keeps competing without anyone re-provisioning it. It is the condition without which everything else on this list would not matter.
* **State about a niche travels on its own**, because it is not in the instance: the `capped` mark and the `TaskID` of the query it holds, the key of the irreparable vector space, the keys of audiences without a channel and the blueprints themselves live in the shared Specialty Catalog (§3.13.3). The successor reads them with no handover.
* **Vacancy counts restart, and it costs one round.** They are derived by passive observation of the stream and are not a field that travels in the payload, so the successor re-derives them by watching the re-announcements already taking place. Losing the accumulated count delays the intervention until `vacancy_rounds_threshold` is crossed again, and nothing more: it is the same asymmetry that §3.13.3 and §3.13.4 explicitly accept in their own fallbacks.
* **A `node_review` in flight is lost or duplicated, and neither matters.** Both writes of archiving are idempotent and the trigger does not live in memory: the Network Monitor keeps observing, so its next `node_review` reopens exactly the same decision (§3.13.3, §3.13.5).
* **The successor resumes a fleet migration left halfway with no bookkeeping**, because the registry is the progress mark: each deployment carries stamped the `model_id` under which it was signed, and the predicate that selects what to re-provision is evaluated over the fleet the instance has now (§3.13.7).
* **Two transient owners are arbitrated where it matters.** The only expensive decision they could duplicate is provisioning on the same niche; a write-if-absent in the Catalog settles it, the same primitive with which §3.15.7 chooses which instance opens a query to the operator. The rest—regenerating a vector, re-signing an already migrated node—is pure by construction and writes the same value (§3.13.7).

**How it creates.** The decisions above fix *which* nodes exist; *how* the blueprint of one that does not exist is derived—its `capability_text`, its `artifact_ref` and the egress entries it will need (§3.16.3)—is the node-creation procedure, and it lives in §6. Every branch of this section that ends in "provisions a node" leads there.

#### 3.13.2. Vacancy: when and how the Spawner intervenes

When a task is re-announced several times without anyone proposing (§3.7.5), the possible causes are: a node with the necessary specialty is missing, **the nodes that cover it are all busy** (§3.6.1), the task is badly framed, or its embedding stayed in a vector space that is no longer the current one, and therefore no proposal can be admissible (§2.3). Resolving that ambiguity is the Spawner's responsibility, following the same CNP protocol as any other node—with no new events and no direct coupling with the Task Broker. The branches further down are those causes, and the stale-space one is evaluated first because the others cannot be decided while it holds.

**Saturation has no branch of its own, and that is deliberate.** From outside it is indistinguishable from missing coverage—in both cases the topic is re-announced and nobody proposes—and the Task Broker could not tell them apart without checking availability, which is exactly what it does not do (§3.7.5, step 4). Nor is there any need to separate them: the missing-coverage branch is the same for both, and the only thing that changes is whether what it chooses already has nodes running. That is what lets the network's capacity be regulated by the trigger that already exists, without a saturation axis, without a new metric and without anyone declaring how many instances a niche deserves.

**The path**, in the order in which the Spawner evaluates it. Each step is developed by the branch it names:

1. **Trigger:** the task has gone `vacancy_rounds_threshold` rounds without a valid proposal; on the `hitl_contact_*` family, a single one.
2. **Is it a `hitl_contact_*`?** It closes the query with `task.failed` without taking it, and leaves the fact in view if what is missing is the channel. End.
3. **It takes the task** with sentinel similarity. If another node proposes in that round, that node wins and the path ends.
4. **Stale space?** `task.failed` with `stale_vector_space`, which the requester's Integration Sidecar reopens. End.
5. **Is the topic redundant?** Only if `T` never had nodes: `task.failed` with `suggested_topic`. End.
6. **Local Catalog:** among the entries that would have proposed for the task, it provisions the best one, whether it runs or not.
7. **Shared Catalog**, if the network participates in one and the Local Catalog gave no candidate: one suitable candidate or none (§3.13.6).
8. **Forge**, if neither gave a candidate: it requests the derivation. If rejected, it is the specification branch.
9. **It returns the task** by letting its lease expire, and the re-announcement puts it within reach of what was provisioned.

The searches over the Topic Registry and the Local Catalog abstain if the vector-space discard emptied them, everything that is provisioned first passes the same preconditions, and the niche's successive-candidates counter counts every intervention that leaves it vacant.

* **Passive observation and intervention threshold:** each Spawner instance listens to the re-announcements of its own partition (§3.13.1) and counts how many successive rounds of the same task passed without any valid proposal—a count derived by observation, not a field that travels in the payload. On crossing `vacancy_rounds_threshold` (Global Configuration, §2.3), the Spawner acts; on the `hitl_contact_*` family, on the first vacancy (below).
* **The Spawner takes the task through the normal flow**—except on the `hitl_contact_*` family, below: it proposes (`task.proposed`) with a sentinel similarity (the lowest possible), so that if a legitimate proposal appears in that same round, the Task Broker's existing rule ("the highest similarity wins", §3.7.5) automatically prefers it and the Spawner is discarded with no special logic in the Task Broker. The sentinel stays effective under the Task Broker's recomputation precisely because the attached value works as a downward cap (`min(s_reported, s_calc)`, §3.7.5, step 4): declaring itself below what the credential allows is always authorized, and it is all the Spawner needs to come last in the auction. If nobody else proposed, the Task Broker assigns the task to it just as to any worker (`task.assigned`), under the same Lease/TTL of §3.1.
* **Before any branch—is the vacancy on a `hitl_contact_*`?** A channel holds at once all the queries of its audience that fall to it (§3.6.1), so a vacancy there means that no live channel of the audience is eligible in that round. **On this family the Spawner has no provisioning outcome: it does not derive, does not revive and does not instantiate again.** A User-Proxy blueprint is defined by which people it reaches, through which external system and how it identifies them (§3.5.3); none of that travels in the vacant task, and a channel's `task.completed` counts as a decision of its audience (§3.14.2): every channel is brought up by the operator (§3.13.8). Reviving an entry with its stored proof would bring back up what the operator took down, and revoking a channel is taking it down (§3.14.2). The predicate is over identifiers—whether the topic belongs to the family—of the same class as those the Task Broker already evaluates, and it does not ask the Spawner to interpret anything (§3.13.1).

  **It closes the query without taking it.** The Task Broker assigns a task of the family only to a channel credential of that audience (§3.7.5, step 4), so the Spawner does not propose. On the first vacancy it publishes `task.failed` on the vacant task without having it assigned, with `failure_code` = `undelivered` (§3.5.2), and the Task Broker closes the task with that event only if a Spawner published it (§3.14.1, §3.14.2) and only on this family. The requester treats it as a round with no answer (§3.5.2). Failing a human query produces the conservative way out and completing it does not, so the Spawner is left with the only way out that decides nothing: it cannot complete a query it does not have assigned.

  **It does not wait for the threshold.** The threshold gives a busy node time to free up, and a channel is never busy: it holds all the queries that fall to it (§3.6.1). Within an attempt the eligible channels are always the same, because the lineage fixes the round and not the re-announcement (§3.5.2), so re-announcing it does not change the outcome and only delays the next round.

  **And it leaves the fact in view when what is missing is the channel.** A vacant round with live channels that were not enabled in it—the owner absent in round 1 of the origin audience, or no `attempt_window` covering it (§3.5.2)—is closed just the same and nobody is warned. The audience is without a channel when no row of the Active Node Registry carries its topic (§3.11); in `operator` and `user`, whose deployment channel declares no `attempt_window` and competes in every round, when the vacant round is not round 1 of the origin audience. It then stamps the key of the audience without a channel in the Catalog, with the moment the condition started (§3.13.3), and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` with `kind` = `audience_unreachable` (§3.15.7). It does not open a query: what resolves the condition is bringing up a channel, which is an act of the operator and not a disposition that can be asked of them (§3.13.8). The key is deleted when a query of that audience stops being left vacant. If the audience without a channel is `operator`, the key stays as queryable state for when the person comes back; the deployment's Operator Channel is structural, and bringing it up when it goes down belongs to the platform (§3.14.1).
* **Stale-space branch—the task was left outside the current vector space.** It is the one evaluated first, and the order is not one of preference but of validity. If the vacant task's `data.model_id` is not the current `embedding_model` (§2.3), the Spawner publishes `task.failed` with `failure_code` = `stale_vector_space`, and that is the end: the requester's Integration Sidecar reopens the attempt with an embedding of the current space, without going through its Logic Container (§3.3).

  * **Why the task arrives in that state.** The Task Broker discards the *proposal*, not the task (§3.7.5, step 4). The task returns to `UNASSIGNED` and the Task Broker re-announces it **with the same `task_embedding`** (§3.1, explicit), so the cycle repeats identically: no admissible proposal, vacancy again, and the round count crossing the threshold. The Task Broker cannot get out of there on its own—it does not call the model, does not read content and deliberately does not emit events (§3.15.7)—so the unblocking has to come from whoever is already watching the vacancy.
  * **Why it goes before the missing-coverage branch.** That branch's topic redundancy check uses the vacant task's `task_embedding` as a proxy for the topic's purpose to search for neighbors in the Topic Registry. With a `task_embedding` from another space, that search is exactly the comparison between different spaces that §3.7.3 flatly forbids: it would return a plausible number that would decide whether or not a topic is minted. The stale-space branch is not an alternative to the others; it **gates** them.
  * **The Spawner detects it without interpreting text.** Comparing `data.model_id` against the current one is a predicate over identifiers, of the same class as the one for the `hitl_contact_*` family further up and as those the Task Broker already evaluates. It does not break "it does not interpret text: it embeds it or carries it" (§3.13.1).
  * **It is a window branch, not a regime.** The only tasks that can fall here are those that were in flight at the moment of the model change; their draining and their effect on budgets are part of the migration procedure (§3.13.7).

* **Missing-coverage branch—a node is missing:** before creating anything, the Spawner resolves two distinct redundancy questions, in this order. Both are neighborhood searches over **stored** entries—the Topic Registry and the Specialty Catalog—and that is why §3.13.3 governs them: they discard the entries whose `model_id` is not the current one, trigger the repair with that discard, and if the discard left the search without candidates the Spawner **abstains** instead of reading the emptiness as absence of coverage. It is a different check from that of the previous branch, and over a different population: there the `model_id` is the vacant task's and decides whether the question can be formulated; here it is that of each consulted entry and decides whether the answer is complete. **The abstention also reaches the specification branch**, because not knowing whether there is coverage does not authorize failing the task as badly framed.
  1. **Is the topic itself redundant?** It only applies when `T` never had any active node—that is, when whoever emitted the original `task.announced` (§3.7.4) may have minted a new topic without knowing an equivalent one already existed. The Spawner searches the **Topic Registry** (§3.7.6) for neighbors by embedding similarity ≥ `topic_similarity_floor` (Global Configuration, §2.3), using the vacant task's `task_embedding` as a proxy for the purpose of `T`—there is no embedding of `T` itself yet, because it was never registered. If it finds an existing topic with active coverage above the floor, it creates nothing: it publishes `task.failed` on the vacant task with `data.suggested_topic` pointing to the existing topic. The requester decides, by its own criterion, whether to reopen on the suggested topic (§3.3)—the Spawner never rewrites the original event; it only fails it with a suggestion (§3.13.1).
  2. **Does the needed node already exist?** If the topic passes the previous check (or already had nodes before and is vacant only for another cause), the Spawner searches the **Local Catalog** (§3.13.3) for the entries that would have proposed for the vacant task: those that would admit the `task_embedding` with the floor they would be provisioned with (§3.7.5, step 3; §3.13.3). It is simulating the auction over what could be running. The question is one of a task against a node, so the floor is each entry's radius and not `agent_similarity_floor`, which measures the distance between two nodes (§3.13.4). An entry that does not meet the provisioning preconditions (further down) is not a candidate, and neither is one whose floor history records an archiving for `notice_provenance` or for `memory_exceeded`: its Logic Container violated the contract (§3.13.4) or does not fit in the memory the network gives it (§3.13.10), and bringing it up again is an operator's `node_provision` (§3.13.8).

     > **With candidates, the Spawner provisions the best one**, chosen with the same comparison by which §3.13.4 decides which neighbor wins, and with the age of each piece of evidence discounted (§3.13.3). If the chosen one already has nodes running, it brings up another instance of that entry; if it is archived, it revives it. It is the same act in both cases, and the resulting node competes through the normal mechanism of §3.7.5 and the lifecycle evaluates it like any other.

     **Saturation is resolved with this same choice.** With running candidates, the vacancy is one of capacity: those that cover the niche would have proposed and did not because they were busy (§3.6.1). The Spawner provisions without waiting again, because the wait for a node to free up is already `vacancy_rounds_threshold`. If the demand was transient, the surplus node loses auctions and the usage axis archives it (§3.13.4). Whether what is provisioned is another instance of what already runs or a different entry is decided by the comparison, not by the cause of the vacancy: choosing with the same rule that judges afterward is what keeps the lifecycle from undoing what the vacancy provisioned.

     **Without local candidates**, it queries the Shared Catalog if the network participates in one. What comes back is a suitable candidate, already registered in the Local Catalog, or none (§3.13.6), and a suitable candidate is provisioned like any local entry.

     **Without a candidate in either**, the Spawner **requests** a new blueprint instead of provisioning it on the spot: the procedure by which it is derived—to whom the request is addressed and how it comes back—belongs to §6, and this section fixes only the contract with which the Spawner consumes it.
