The request is asynchronous: the Spawner stores a handle next to the niche's other counters (§3.13.3) and checks it on each re-announcement round it already performs, without blocking any lease longer than the vacancy itself already blocks it. A network without a Forge has no one to request it from, and the niche stays vacant with its count running (§6.1). The check comes back in one of three ways:

     | Comes back | What the Spawner does |
     | :--- | :--- |
     | **Delivered**—an artifact (or several, §3.13.9) | Validates the preconditions below and provisions |
     | **Blocked**—a question pending a decision the Spawner cannot make on its own (on egress, §3.16.5, or from another authority the request needs) | Relays it without formulating it and keeps the request alive; meanwhile the niche stays vacant with its count running |
     | **Rejected**—a reason, with no artifact: what was left vacant does not describe a derivable specialty | **Specification branch**, below |

     Provisioning—whether a Local Catalog entry, an import or a delivered artifact—first requires the same preconditions, all of them predicates over identifiers and none an inspection of the artifact; and if `T` was a new topic (step 1 found no neighbor), it is registered in the Topic Registry (§3.7.6) with the `task_embedding` that originated it—and with that task's text as the entry's source text, so that the vector stays recomputable (§3.7.3)—and becomes available for future checks. Only reviving involves the interface-range precondition: an archived blueprint may have been built against a Node Runtime Interface version that the Integration Sidecar deployed today no longer supports (§3.6.1), so the Spawner checks the entry's version against `runtime_interface_range` (§2.3) before instantiating it. If it does not fit, it treats that blueprint as if it did not exist—it moves on to the next candidate, and if none is left, to the next source—instead of bringing up a node that will not start. With an entry vetoed or deleted by the operator (§3.13.8), or with a slot only the operator associates (§3.16.3), it does the same.

  **Everything this point provisions or requests is an intervention on a vacant niche, and the niche's same successive-candidates counter counts it** (§3.13.3, §3.13.5): bringing up another instance of an entry that already runs, reviving, importing or deriving are the same class of fact—someone tried to cover the vacancy and it was not enough—even though some reuse an entry and others build a new one. The predicate is over the result, not over the event: **it counts every intervention on a vacant niche that leaves it vacant**, without distinguishing what the Spawner did to try.

  **A request that comes back blocked does not consume the counter.** There was no intervention on the niche—nothing was provisioned—so the predicate above has nothing to apply to: nobody tried to cover the vacancy; a question was asked about it. It is the only exception, and it is deliberately narrow: it covers the case in which no artifact was delivered, not the case in which the delivered artifact was no good. **A request that comes back rejected does not consume it either**, by the stronger argument of the specification branch, below: there the vacancy ends, the task stops being re-announced, and there is no niche on which this counter keeps running. **Failing validation when provisioning does consume it**, on the other hand: if what was delivered has an egress slot the Spawner fails to associate (below) and it cannot provision it, there was an intervention and the niche stayed vacant—the same fact as a mute blueprint, further down.

  Two cases make that predicate explicit. A provisioned or revived blueprint that stays **mute**—its embedding falls outside the neighborhood of the task that originated the vacancy, and therefore it never proposes—counts just the same as if nothing had been attempted: there was an intervention and the niche is still vacant. Counting it only when the blueprint gets to compete would leave invisible exactly the case the counter exists to catch. And bringing up another instance of an entry that already runs counts when the vacancy persists after doing so: if the bottleneck is downstream of the node—a saturated egress class, for example—the instances stay busy *waiting* instead of relieving the queue, the vacancy crosses the re-announcement threshold again, and each intervention is still locally correct—vacant task, matching entry, free instance that will win—without any of them relieving the previous one. The cost axis sees the case but does not stop it: the duration drift fires `high_latency` (§3.12.3), and that review decides about the node, whose cause is outside, and not about how many instances run. The only signal that actually stops the niche's growth is this counter.

  On reaching the cap, the escalation is the same one that already exists for the improvement cycle (§3.13.5): `capped` and `hitl_contact_operator` with `niche_uncoverable`. **The reset, on the other hand, is this branch's own:** it clears when the niche stops being vacant, which the Spawner sees because it is already watching the re-announcements—not when a neighbor wins a score comparison, of which there is none here. **What is accepted is the state of the niche, not a node, and `accept_current` means something different depending on which state it is:** with instances running it is living with the queue; with none running it is the niche staying vacant, the `capped` mark being kept and the Spawner ceasing to intervene and to ask. The meaning §3.13.5 already fixes—the original node stays active and no candidates are created—is a third case, that of the improvement cycle, where there is a healthy node competing. **There is no global cap on these interventions:** each niche carries its own, and several vacant niches at once are independent escalations and not a shared quota—vacancy is real demand, and limiting it by quota would be rejecting demand. **The platform's capacity is not a quota:** a provisioning that does not fit is not rejected; it evicts or waits (§3.13.10).

  The egress precondition governs what is revived and what is derived alike, because it is not about the network but about what is outside it: **every egress slot of the blueprint has to be associated with a registered entry.** When reviving, they come from the entry's `egress_required` (§3.13.3), and when creating, from the derived specialty (§6); if any has no entry in the `egress_registry` (§2.3) that implements its interface, or has several and the network did not decide which (§3.16.3), the Spawner does not provision—it opens the query to the operator of §3.16.5, and meanwhile the niche stays vacant with its count running. A slot only the operator associates (§3.16.3) does not open that query, because what resolves it is a `node_provision` (§3.13.8): a derived blueprint that brings one fails validation and consumes the niche's counter (above), and the niche that is not covered in another way escalates through `niche_uncoverable`. The requirement traveling with the blueprint is what makes this precondition evaluable without instantiating it, just like the previous one. **With which floor what is revived comes back.** With one from its floor history (§3.13.3), and the search further up simulates with that same floor, so what is chosen because it would have proposed does propose.

If that floor no longer serves, the lifecycle corrects it, not the vacancy: a narrow one ends up widened, and a wide one, narrowed (§3.13.4).

Creation can take time; the Spawner does not need to signal anything special to "return" the task—it simply does not renew its own lease. When the lease expires, the task returns to `UNASSIGNED` through the already existing mechanism (§3.1) and the next re-announcement makes it available to the new node (if it is already active) exactly as to any other candidate—the same self-selection flow as always, with no special bootstrap.
* **Specification branch—badly framed task:** the Spawner publishes `task.failed` (`subject` = the vacant task) with `data.failure_reason` explaining the reason (see §3.8.1). **The Spawner never concludes this on its own.** It does not interpret text (§3.13.1), so it is in no position to judge whether what was left vacant describes a derivable specialty or not—and that is exactly why it should not be able to: a text written by an attacker must not be able to sway this decision of whoever signs capability credentials. This outcome is one of the three with which the derivation request of the missing-coverage branch comes back (above): the reason is written by whoever evaluated the text, and the Spawner carries it into `data.failure_reason` as it arrived, without rephrasing it. This introduces no new path: it enters the same reopen loop as any other `task.failed` (§3.3)—the requester that emitted that task decides, by its own criterion, whether to re-specify and reopen it, escalate, or give up. If the vacant task was the root request, that decision eventually reaches the User-Proxy and from there the human (§3.5), but as a consequence of the normal recursion of §3.9, not as a special case of this branch. **The rejection is about the lineage, never about the niche:** it does not stamp `capped`, does not mark the niche and does not condition the next vacancy on the same topic, which starts its own request from zero as if there had been none—otherwise a badly written request could permanently close off a region of the semantic space for everyone else. And **it does not consume the niche's successive-candidates counter** (above): there the vacancy ends instead of continuing to run, so the predicate that counts interventions on a niche that is still vacant has nothing to apply to.

#### 3.13.3. The Specialty Catalog

A store shared among all the partitioned Spawner instances of **all the networks of the same tenant**—with no boundary between them, because they all have the same owner (§2.4). Same role as the Context Store, but for specialties already built. It is the library of *blueprints*, and the other half of what the Spawner needs to decide: what can be built, against what is running, which belongs to the Active Node Registry (§3.11). An entry's fields are better grouped by **what they are for** than in a flat list, because they serve purposes that never mix:

* **To compete:** the triple of §3.7.3—the `embedding`, the **`capability_text`** it was derived from and the `model_id` under which it was generated—and the **floor history**. The `similarity_floor` is the similarity floor below which a node does not propose (§3.7.5), and each instance carries it in its credential and in its deployment record (§3.14.2, §3.11), not the entry. The vector is cache and the text is primary; the triple fixes where the blueprint competes and the floor, how far. The entry keeps one row per floor value with which it ran on this network and was archived: the floor, the `trigger_reason` of the archiving, the metrics snapshot of that moment, when, and the `model_id` of the space in which that floor makes sense. If the same floor is archived again, its row is replaced, so the table grows with the values the floor took and not with time. Active floors are not stored here: they are read from the Active Node Registry (§3.11), by the same rule that makes "archived" an absence and not a flag (further down). **When provisioning, the floor comes from that history**, from the row the decision strategy chooses (§3.13.4); a row whose `model_id` is not the current one is not chosen, and an entry with no eligible rows receives `task_similarity_floor` (§2.3). Another instance of an entry that already runs carries the floor of the instance it replicates. From there the lifecycle's exploration moves it (§3.13.4), never a one-off choice at derivation. And the **`attempt_window`**, the range of `current_attempts` in which the blueprint proposes and whether it competes in the final round (§3.5.2): whoever builds the blueprint declares it, it travels to the credential together with the floor, and if it is not declared the blueprint competes in every round.
* **To rebuild:** the `artifact_ref`—an immutable pointer to the deployable Logic Container—; the Node Runtime Interface version against which that artifact was built (§3.6.1); its `egress_required`, the egress slots without which its logic does not work, each with its interface (§3.16.3); in a channel, its `ingress_required`, the slots through which it receives messages from outside (§3.16.7); and its `memory_required`, the memory its Logic Container needs (§3.13.10). They are preconditions the Spawner evaluates **before instantiating anything**, and the `artifact_ref` and nothing else describes the node: that *nothing else* is the point, and it is developed below.
* **To manage the space it takes:** the `artifact_size`, the space the artifact's layers that are not part of the base image take up in the image registry—what deleting it frees—and the **deletion mark**, which the operator's request that deleted the image stamps (§3.13.8).
* **To admit the artifact:** the `scan_result` the Admission Scanner (§2.1) issued on it—`verdict` (`pass` | `fail`), the `ruleset_version` it was judged with and its `as_of`. It is composite for the same reason as an `egress_registry` entry (§2.3): **a verdict ages**, because the ruleset improves, and without the version it was issued under there is no way of knowing whether it still says anything. It is written once per artifact and never per network, because the digest of the `artifact_ref` is its natural key: two networks of the tenant that instantiate the same image do not scan it twice. The act of registration writes it, and that act does not register an artifact with `fail` (further down), so an entry is always born at `pass`. **When the ruleset advances, the Catalog re-analyzes the entries it stores**, and one may move to `fail`: it stays stored, because the Catalog does not delete, and no network brings it up. What the Spawner evaluates when provisioning is not the scan but its result and its freshness, and that is what keeps an entry stored long ago from being instantiated under a verdict that no longer holds.
* **To decide about the niche:** the **metrics snapshot**—the full signal vector of §3.12.2—which the Spawner writes at two moments: on the entry, when processing a `node_review` that derives (§6.3), and in the floor-history row, when archiving a node, together with the `trigger_reason` that caused the decommissioning.
* **For what it cost to obtain:** the **acquisition cost**—what that entry cost the tenant: the cost of deriving it if it was built (§6), or the price if it was imported already built, which is zero for a Shared Catalog entry with no rate (§3.13.6)—with its **`provenance`** (`derived` | `purchased` | `imported` | `seeded`) and its **`as_of`**. `imported` is an import with no rate, and `purchased` one with a price. It is kept per entry and not as a running average, because the dispersion across entries is information and not noise to average out (§3.12.2): if derivation costs turn out dispersed, that says the niches being derived are heterogeneous in difficulty. It carries `as_of` for the same reason as the `egress_registry` (§2.3): the cost axis is the one that ages fastest. And it carries `provenance` because a price and a derivation cost are not interchangeable as input to any calculation that estimates how much it costs to build—only `derived` entries go into it.

  **It is an immutable fact of the entry, not a signal.** It crosses no threshold and triggers nothing, so it does not take part in the signal aggregation invariant of §3.12.2—it is of the same class as the `artifact_ref` or the metrics snapshot. And **it is never compared against the cost of running** (`cost_own_p50`, §3.12.2): one is what it cost to operate the node, the other what it cost to obtain it, and after the fact the latter is a sunk cost—a node expensive to obtain and cheap to run is kept just the same. It is a legitimate input only **before** the fact, when deciding whether it pays to build or to acquire.

  **For the same reason, if the entry was derived under an egress constraint**—the niche needed an interface that no registered entry of the tenant implemented and the result was accepted anyway, incomplete in that dimension—**the entry declares it, with which interface was missing.** It takes the same shape: informative and nothing more, it gates no counter and enters no score; it lives in the entry and not in an event because an archived entry can be revived much later and the constraint is still part of how it was born.
* **To audit who built it:** the `author_id` of the act of registration—the Forge that derived it, the Shared Catalog it was imported from, or the deployment's seeding. It says who built the artifact, which is a fact about the artifact and the same for every network that sees it; `provenance` says how this entry reached this network, which is a fact about the relationship and differs between two networks of the same tenant that obtained the same artifact by different paths. The test further down sorts them, and that is why one carries a qualifier and the other does not. It is what gives a Forge a way to see what happened to what it built (§6.8).
* **To audit where it came from:** if the entry was built by derivation (§6), a pointer to the `TraceID` of that derivation. It is diagnostic and not contract—the trace has its own retention (§3.2) and the pointer can be left dangling without that invalidating anything in the entry—and it is what makes the cost above auditable: the number keeps a pointer to what it is made of. Next to the pointer, while the derivation is pending, the **handle** with which the Spawner checks it on each re-announcement round (§3.13.2)—it lives next to the niche's other counters and is resolved as soon as the request stops being pending.

It also stores the niche's **successive-candidates counter** and its `capped` mark (§3.13.5)—Spawner state about the niche, not about an instance—and next to that mark the `TaskID` of the operator query the niche has open (§3.15.7). For the same reason and in the same shape, it stores the **counter of successive reviews closed without replacement**, from which the backoff of the `no_action` period comes (§3.13.5): it is a separate counter and not a variant of the candidates one, because they count different facts—failing to cover a niche, versus finding no improvement for one already covered—and only failing to cover it escalates to a human. By that same criterion it stores the niche's **provider authorization request counter** (§3.16): it counts a third fact—that the operator denied an interface the niche asked for—and its outcome is different again, because exhausting it silences the requests instead of escalating. It stores a network-scope key for the irreparable vector space, which plays the same double role: it holds the open query and, being shared across partitions, chooses which instance asks when several detect the same failure at once. **Per egress interface** it stores a key of the same shape and with the same double role (§3.16.5), and of network scope for the same reason: what is decided about an interface holds for every niche that needs it; when what is missing is scope and not an entry, the key also carries the topic. Per interface and slot name it stores, in the same shape, the slot-association key, which carries the topic when any of its options is a `topics` entry (§3.16.5). Per ingress entry it stores the key that names the channels associated with it, which upholds their exclusivity (§3.16.7). It stores, **per audience**, the audience-without-a-channel key (§3.13.2): since when the audience has been without a channel. It backs the `audience_unreachable` `notice` (§3.15.7) and avoids broadcasting it again on each query that closes; it holds no query, and the topic's partition already determines who writes it. It stores, per Shared Catalog entry and price, the **denied-acquisition key**, which stamps that the operator denied that purchase or did not answer; and with network scope the **exhausted-credit key**, which backs the `catalog_credit_exhausted` `notice` and avoids broadcasting it on each rejected acquisition (§3.13.6). And it stores, per niche, a **write-if-absent provisioning key**: the write that arbitrates the only expensive case in which two instances can believe they own the same topic at once, while the ring's membership view converges (§3.19). One wins and the other does not provision; it is the same primitive—and the same "no need to designate an owner" argument—with which §3.15.7 chooses which instance opens a query to the operator. In the same way, **per operator request on the fleet**, it stores a key named by the request's `TaskID`, which stamps its outcome (§3.13.8). And **per `artifact_ref`**, the operator's veto mark (§3.13.8). Per entry it stores the **effective reservation** with which the network brings it up and the **reservation-raise key**, which holds the `memory_growth` query; and with network scope, the **capacity-pressure key**, which backs the `fleet_capacity_pressure` `notice` (§3.13.10). Per entry it stores the **presence mark** of each network that runs it, and with network scope the **storage-pressure key**, which backs the `artifact_storage_pressure` `notice` (further down). It is knowledge that persists beyond the lifecycle of any single instance, and it is also what makes the Spawner indifferent to its partition changing hands (§3.13.1). It is private by default with respect to other tenants, but a blueprint can be explicitly promoted to a Shared Catalog across multiple Zobik deployments under the requirements of §3.13.6. Besides node blueprints, the Catalog stores the **Topic Registry** (§3.7.6)—the same structure shared across Spawner partitions, with a different purpose: deduplicating topics instead of nodes. It also stores **bundle** entries—a set of blueprints instead of a single one, with its members and its `purpose_text`—under the requirements and the two closure rules of §3.13.6.

**Since the store holds rows from several networks, it needs a per-network qualifier—and it is not split into one store per network.** The cut does not run between two halves but between classes of field:

* **The blueprint carries no qualifier**—`capability_text`, `attempt_window`, `artifact_ref`, interface version, `egress_required`, `ingress_required`, `memory_required`, `topics_announced` (§3.13.6), `scan_result`, `author_id`, `artifact_size` and the deletion mark—because it is the same artifact for every network that sees it.
* **Everything that describes a network's relationship with an entry or a niche carries a network qualifier:** the competition triple (`embedding` + `model_id`, which are cache of the text), the floor history, the metrics snapshot, the counters of candidates and of successive reviews, that of provider authorization requests, the `capped` marks with their `TaskID`, the keys of irreparable vector space, egress interface, slot association, ingress entry, audience, denied acquisition, exhausted credit, write-if-absent provisioning, reservation raise, capacity pressure and storage pressure, the presence mark, the effective reservation, the per-operator-request key, the veto mark, the acquisition cost with its `provenance` and its egress-constraint declaration, the pointer to the derivation `TraceID`, the handle of a pending derivation, and the Topic Registry. **The test rules even over cost:** what it cost to build or buy is a fact about *this* network, not about the artifact—another network of the tenant that derives the same `capability_text` pays its own bill.

**The test for classifying any future field:** does it describe the artifact, or does it describe this network's relationship with a niche?

**That same cut separates two acts, with different owners.** A node appearing in a network is two facts, and each one writes one half of the entry:

* **The act of registration** writes the half without a qualifier. Whoever built or brought in the artifact executes it—a Forge when returning what it derived (§6.5), an import from the Shared Catalog (§3.13.6, §3.13.8), the deployment's seeding (§6.1)—and **it decides nothing about which nodes exist**: it makes available an artifact that may never be deployed. It needs nothing that only the deploying network knows, and that is why it can happen long before, in another network of the tenant, or be followed by no deployment at all. Registering is also what makes the image registry retain the image (§2.1). **The Catalog passes the image through the Admission Scanner before writing the entry** (§2.1), and the registration fails with its reason if the verdict is `fail`, if the `artifact_ref` no longer resolves or if the artifact does not fit in the storage capacity (further down). Registering again an `artifact_ref` with a deletion mark removes the mark, because registering requires the image to resolve: that is how an imported entry comes back, by bringing it in again (§3.13.8).
* **The act of provisioning** writes the qualified half. The Spawner of the deploying network executes it, and that is where it is decided which nodes exist (§3.13.1). It is the act that generates the embedding in the current space of *its* network, assigns it the `similarity_floor` from the floor history—or `task_similarity_floor`, if there is no eligible row—and signs the credential (§3.14.2).

**Preconditions are evaluated when provisioning and never when registering.** An entry may have been stored for a long time, and `runtime_interface_range`, the `egress_registry` and the `ingress_registry` belong to each network and move (§2.3): a compatibility verdict only holds for the network and the moment in which it was issued. Evaluating them when instantiating is what prevents bringing up an entry its network no longer admits—the path of §3.13.8 is that same one, with the operator naming an entry written long before. The exception is the entry with a price that the Spawner acquires on its own: the preconditions are also evaluated before paying for it (§3.13.6), and evaluated again when provisioning.

**A precondition the Spawner does not know how to evaluate counts as unmet.** The entry may have been registered by a piece of another version of the substrate, in another network of the tenant or in the Shared Catalog, and declare a precondition later than this Spawner's. The entry then serves as metadata and is not instantiated, just like one with the Node Runtime Interface version out of range (§3.13.6). A field that is not a precondition and that the Spawner does not know changes nothing it decides.

**An entry without any qualified row is a legitimate state:** no network has embedded it yet. The first one to look at it regenerates its triple through this section's convergence mechanism, and if it covers no niche it wins no auctions. Competition orders it with no need for a state field to maintain.

**The qualifier resolves a clash the unqualified version would have.** Further up, every neighborhood search discards the entries whose `model_id` is not the current one, and the discard triggers regeneration. With a single shared vector and two networks of the tenant with different `embedding_model` values—it is a per-network parameter, §2.3—the two would step on each other regenerating in a circle, indefinitely. With the triple qualified per network, each regenerates only its own rows, the cache is preserved and thrashing is impossible by construction.

**Reading rules:**

* **Each network reads its own rows by default**, and everything that follows in this section holds literally: each network sees exactly what it would see if the store were its alone.
* **Reading the rows of another network of the tenant is deliberate, and it already has its rule:** it is the same principle §3.13.6 fixes for the `shared_score`—"they are measurements over different populations"—applied one scope further down, between networks of the same tenant and not only between tenants. It serves to rank and decide before instantiating, never as a measurement of one's own.
* **The qualifier is attribution, not isolation.** It says whose the row is, not who can read it, and that is correct because there is no trust boundary to cross within a tenant. Crossing a tenant boundary is the Shared Catalog, which does have gates (§3.13.6).

**The Catalog does not describe how the node solves: it stores the pointer to what solves it.** The `artifact_ref` is content-addressed—typically the digest of a container image, never a mutable tag like `:latest`, because a tag can be repointed to other content and break the guarantee—and the Spawner passes it to the orchestrator to rebuild the **exact** Logic Container of that blueprint, whether it is a simple semantic node, a state graph with its own phases and tools (§3.6.1) or a deterministic node where there is no prompt and the specialty *is* code (§3.6.3). It is a single uniform field for any node, and without the network having to understand any of its parts.

**The only thing left outside the digest is the Integration Sidecar, and that is why its version goes alongside.** The Integration Sidecar is not part of this pointer—it is a generic process, versioned and deployed by whoever operates the substrate, not per blueprint (§3.6.1). Rebuilding a node is then rebuilding its Logic Container and **applying the current Integration Sidecar to it**, not the one that accompanied it when it was archived. That is correct—a revived node inherits integration improvements for free, which is the whole argument of §3.13.7—and it is what forces storing the Node Runtime Interface version next to the `artifact_ref`: it is the only datum of the entry that does not describe what is inside the container but **the shape of its edge**, which is precisely what the Integration Sidecar has to know before pairing with it.

**`capability_text` is the only text the Catalog stores: the description of *what* the blueprint knows how to do.** It is the only input of the embedding it competes with (§3.7.3) and the only thing the network reads about it. The *how* of solving internally lives inside the `artifact_ref` like any other part of its logic, invisible to the network (§2.2).

**Validity of the vector space: discard, repair, and only then abstain.** The decisions the Spawner makes by reading this Catalog are embedding comparisons—the neighborhood of the missing-coverage branch of §3.13.2 and that of §3.13.4—and §3.7.3 fixes that a comparison between different spaces is not valid. Hence an invariant over every read of the store: **the Catalog is never queried with a stale `model_id`, regardless of what is happening in the rest of the network.**

* **Every neighborhood search discards the entries whose `model_id` is not the current one.** It is the same check the Task Broker does in §3.7.5 step 4, applied here, and the datum is already stored: the triple of §3.7.3 carries a `model_id` per entry, both in the blueprints and in the Topic Registry.
* **The discard triggers regeneration**, from the source text and over all stale entries—active and archived blueprints, plus the Topic Registry. Regeneration is idempotent and crosses partitions without coordinating, because the vector is cache and the text is primary (§3.7.3): two instances that regenerate the same entry write the same value. It is a convergent invariant instead of a procedure that has to go right a single time.
* **Abstention is the fallback, and its predicate is per query.** When regeneration is not possible—the model does not respond, the entry has no valid source text—and the discard left the search without candidates, the Spawner **abstains** instead of treating the niche as uncovered: *if the discard removed at least one entry and none was left, the answer is not "there is no coverage" but "I don't know"*. If it discarded nothing, the answer is complete and it acts normally.

It reaches only the Local Catalog: the Shared Catalog has its own space, regenerates at both boundaries on its own and its vector never enters runtime (§3.13.6). The sweep this invariant triggers—when it runs, what happens if the model does not respond and what it costs—is one of the halves of the vector space change, and it lives with the others in §3.13.7.

**Changes of internal logic, not of external contract:** when the code of a Logic Container changes (a new version of a deterministic node's script, a rewritten prompt, a change of the model the node invokes, a migration to a more complex state graph), the Spawner does not treat it differently from "improving a node" (§3.13.4)—it provisions a candidate blueprint with the same external contract (same topic; its own `capability_text`, bounded to the original's neighborhood by the derivation bound of §3.13.4) but a new `artifact_ref`, which competes in parallel through the normal self-selection mechanism and only replaces the original when it wins the comparison of §3.13.4 (§3.13.5). Reverting is the same mechanism in reverse: reviving the archived entry that points to the previous `artifact_ref` (§3.13.2)—the Catalog never does a physical *delete*, so that old `artifact_ref` stays available as long as the operator does not delete its image (§3.13.8).

**It is an intended consequence of there being no editable fields.** The node's prompt and model live inside the `artifact_ref` and not as loose fields of the entry, so **any change of internal logic necessarily comes in through blue/green**: it is validated by competition against the node it aims to replace, instead of taking effect on the active node through a write with no evidence and no way back. And no new mechanism is needed to achieve it: the blue/green the network already uses also covers this case.

**The `similarity_floor` comes in that way too, and for the same reason.** A different floor changes which tasks the node competes for, so **it is provisioned as a candidate instance and the auction decides**, never as an edit of an active instance. In the usual case it does not travel alone: an artifact with better internal logic is a new entry, with its own floor. When it does travel alone, the candidate is another instance of the same entry with another floor: it keeps the `capability_text` and therefore the vector, so the derivation bound of §3.13.4 is satisfied from the start and the traffic split does not depend on the draw of §3.7.5: the wider floor admits it on tasks the original does not propose for.

**"Archiving" is two writes, not one, and they are ordered—because they do not weigh the same:**

1. **The Spawner asks the orchestrator to scale to zero / remove the deployment**, and confirms the decommissioning by reading the Active Node Registry (§3.11)—the same cross-check it already does in §3.13.4, not a new query. The node leaves the registry of active nodes, stops competing for tasks and **its credential is extinguished with it** (§3.11). On a `hitl_contact_*` channel, the decommissioning is a draining (§3.13.8).
2. **It persists in the Catalog the metrics snapshot** in the floor-history row that corresponds to the node's floor—the signal vector of §3.12.2, which the Spawner already has in hand because it arrived in the `node_review` it is closing (§3.12.3)—plus the `trigger_reason` that caused the decommissioning, and the snapshot is thus available for reviving later, whether or not an instance is running today.

**That order is what makes the dangerous divergence unreachable.** The other way around, a failure of the decommissioning would leave the Catalog recording as archived a node that is still running, competing and with a valid credential, and nothing would reconcile it: the Task Broker does not consult the Active Node Registry, nor does it need to (§3.11)—a node that is still deployed competes legitimately, and its credential tells the truth. The right answer to that state is never for the Task Broker to look at the registry, but for the node to stop running.

**Retrying converges with no new state.** "Archived" is not a flag anyone stamps: it is the absence in the Registry plus the snapshot in the Catalog. If the decommissioning fails—or if the Spawner instance dies between the two steps—nothing was written, the node is still active and the Network Monitor keeps observing it, so its next `node_review` reopens exactly the same decision and executes it again (§3.13.5). Both writes are idempotent: repeating them writes the same values. It is the same criterion with which the repair of the vector space closes further up—a convergent invariant instead of a procedure that has to go right a single time—and with the same economy: the trigger is a demand that already exists, not an in-memory retry that is lost with the process.

**The remaining half-failure is the cheap one, by construction.** If the decommissioning succeeds and the snapshot fails, what is lost is not coverage but knowing how the node was doing when it left: the blueprint is still available and at most it is revived later blind, after which the normal observation cycle archives it again. It is the same asymmetry §3.13.4 explicitly accepts—erring on this side costs a round; on the other, it costs a degraded node competing with a live credential.

**The snapshot stores the full plane and not the axis that triggered the decommissioning**, for the same reason the `node_review` payload carries it whole (§3.12.3): the `trigger_reason` says what lit up, and what to do with the entry later is decided by also looking at the axes that were healthy. It is also the last occasion to store it—the Network Monitor's aggregate lets go of a node when it leaves the roster (§3.12.1), so what is not written here remains nowhere.

> **The snapshot says how the node was doing on the day it was written, and it weighs according to its age.** Another window measured it, with other prices and another flow on the topic. It enters a comparison against live signals—when choosing what to provision on a vacancy (§3.13.2)—only with that age discounted, and one written more than `evidence_staleness` ago is *not evaluable*, just like an empty window (§3.12.1): it beats nothing evaluable. How it is discounted belongs to the decision strategy (§3.13.4). It is also read to decide about the entry itself: with which floor to revive it (§3.13.2) and whether to promote it (§3.13.6).

The cost axis is the one that ages fastest, because `cost` moves with the market (§3.12.2); `tokens_in` and `tokens_out` measure work done internally and keep describing the artifact as long as the `artifact_ref` is the same. That is why the discount can differ per axis. The snapshot belongs to the Local Catalog, qualified per network (further up): another network of the same tenant can read it under the deliberate reading rule; what crosses to another tenant is the `shared_score` of §3.13.6, calibrated separately.

All of this governs every path that archives: `low_usage`, `no_demand` and "a winning neighbor exists" (§3.13.4), the close of the blue/green (§3.13.4), the *winning neighbor* outcome of §3.13.5, `retire_niche` (§3.13.5), retirement at the operator's request (§3.13.8), and eviction and decommissioning for memory (§3.13.10). There is never a physical delete of the entry (§3.13.1). What is deleted is an artifact's image, at the operator's request, and the entry stays with its deletion mark (§3.13.8).

**The presence mark says which networks of the tenant run each entry.** The Active Node Registry is local to its network (§3.11), so no Spawner sees what runs on another network; the mark is the copy of that fact the shared Catalog exposes. It is a lease qualified per network, with the shape of the coverage lease of §3.15.7, and the following Spawner writes maintain it:

* **When provisioning, it writes it before bringing up the node.** A written mark and a node that never came up are corrected on the next renewal; a node brought up without a mark would be invisible to the other networks.
* **When renewing, it rewrites it for each entry its partition runs**, from its own Active Node Registry, and removes that of the entries it no longer runs. It renews within `catalog_presence_ttl` (§2.3).
* **When archiving, it removes it** once it confirms that no instance of that entry remains in its partition (step 1, above).

**If the Spawner stops renewing, the mark expires on its own**, and the network stops appearing as present without anyone having to detect it. It is the safe direction of the error: an expired mark with a node still running is the case deletion already contemplates (§3.13.8), and a live mark without a node only delays a deletion until it expires.

**No decision about its own network is made by the mark.** The Spawner decides about its network by reading its Active Node Registry, which is the source of truth (§3.11), and "archived" is still an absence in that registry and not a mark (further up). The mark is read by deletion (§3.13.8) and by `node_catalog_query`, which is how a network sees what the others run. It is a fact and not a measurement, so the deliberate reading rule further up does not reach it.

**Artifact storage has a capacity that the deployment of the tenant's pieces declares.** What is occupied is the sum of the `artifact_size` values of the entries without a deletion mark: it is what the operator can free, and what the Catalog counts without asking the registry. What does not fit is not registered (further up), and nothing is deleted to make room for it: the operator decides what is deleted (§3.13.8). Before reaching the limit, the Spawner warns: on each renewal it compares what is occupied against `artifact_storage_pressure_share` of the capacity (§2.3), and above it stamps its network's storage-pressure key and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` with `kind` = `artifact_storage_pressure` (§3.15.7). The key is deleted when what is occupied drops back below.

**`node_catalog_query`: the library, outward.** The Catalog is the store that outlives the instances, and everything decided about the library instead of about the fleet—promoting a blueprint to the Shared Catalog (§3.13.6)—is decided over what it stores. This is the query that exposes it: one more fixed-convention topic (§3.7.7), the same request/response pattern as `trace_query` (§3.10) and `node_query` (§3.12.3), with no selection payload. It returns one entry per blueprint with its `state`, its `topic`, its `artifact_ref`, its `capability_text`, its acquisition cost with `provenance` and `as_of`, the floors it runs with today and its floor history, with the snapshot of each row, its `artifact_size`, its deletion mark and the networks of the tenant with a current presence mark. It also returns what is occupied and the artifact storage capacity (further up), and each bundle with its `purpose_text` and its members, which is the list the Operator Channel expands to instantiate it (§3.17.1). The acquisition cost goes here and not in a separate event because it is exactly what gives the operator visibility into how much the network spent building its fleet without introducing any mechanism: whoever wants the total adds it up over this same listing. The history goes because it says with which radius an entry comes back when instantiated (§3.13.8). Size and presence go because they are what the operator looks at to decide what to delete (§3.13.8). The listing is heavy, so it goes by Claim-Check to the Context Store (§3.4) and the `task.completed` carries the reference.

**Any Spawner instance claims it, by auction without similarity.** It is the argument §3.7.6 already uses for `topic_catalog_query`, and it holds here literally because the store is the same: the Catalog is shared across partitions, so no instance is more competent than another and there is nothing to rank among interchangeable candidates. No deterministic routing or fan-out is needed, and the requester does not need to know `N`. Ranking by neighborhood, moreover, would make the Spawner compete against the very vector space it repairs, breaking the query right when the model is changing.

**The Spawner answers it because the Catalog is its own**, which is the same rule by which the Network Monitor answers the census of §3.12.3: each query is served by the component that writes the store it comes from. The Network Monitor does not read the Catalog on any path of the architecture, and exposing the library is no reason to give it that access—its memory covers only the nodes that run, and the Catalog is the one that lasts.

**It reports `state` by cross-checking with the Active Node Registry, and that cross-check introduces nothing new.** It is the same one the Spawner does when archiving—above, step 1—and when searching for neighbors already running (§3.13.4). And it is the only way to answer that field, because "archived" is precisely the predicate no store answers on its own (§3.11).

**It carries the snapshot and not the live signals.** The metrics snapshot can also exist on an active entry—the `node_review` that derived wrote it (§3.13.3)—but it is a point-in-time picture, not the live signals of §3.12.2, which belong to the Network Monitor and live partitioned: bringing them would force the fan-out §3.12.3 avoids, and for what is active the answer refers to the point form of `node_query`. The snapshot is a different object and is not mixed with those signals, which is the reading rule further up exercised in the query. It is included for reasons that do not reopen the previous point—it is **unreachable any other way** on an archived blueprint, because the point query has nothing to return (the Network Monitor's aggregate let go of the node when it stopped running, §3.12.1), and **it costs no extra call**, because it is already written next to the `artifact_ref` this query returns anyway.

**If nobody answers it, the operator cannot choose what to promote or what to delete, and nothing more.** A timeout visible to the requester, just like `trace_query` (§3.10): the network keeps working, including the revival of archived blueprints, which the Spawner decides by reading the Catalog on its own (§3.13.2, §3.13.4) and never through this query.

#### 3.13.4. Spawner decision: embedding neighborhood, not "the only node of the topic"

**The decision has two layers.** This section fixes what every decision on a `node_review` respects: against whom a node is compared, when a neighbor beats it, what is never replaced and what guarantees that the cycle ends. The concrete rule by which the decision is made within those limits is the Spawner's **decision strategy**: which objective metric it compares, with what margin and how it discounts the age of the evidence, how much it moves a node's floor when widening or narrowing it, when it declares a niche heterogeneous (§3.13.9), which candidate it chooses and which floor-history row it uses when provisioning (§3.13.3). The strategy belongs to the implementation, and several are equally valid. It reads the full vector that arrives in `node_review` (§3.12.3)—that is why the Network Monitor measures every signal and not just the ones a strategy uses, and changing strategy does not require measuring from scratch—it is deterministic like the whole Spawner (§3.13.1), and its parameters live in the Global Configuration (§2.3) with the current class.

**The strategy proposes and the evidence judges.** The strategy can generate candidates—one with the widened floor, those of a split (§3.13.9), a revived, imported or derived one—and none replaces anything without winning the comparison further down against measured signals, or without the usage axis archiving the surplus one. A wrong strategy costs rounds and never correctness: the candidate that does not win is archived, and archiving is reversible (§3.13.3). The exception is narrowing, which replaces without competing because its result is already measured (further down). A node's `similarity_floor` always moves through another instance of its entry, and never by editing an active instance (§3.13.3).

**The path**, in the order in which the Spawner evaluates it. Each step is developed by the block it names, and the outcomes with their deadlines are those of §3.13.5:

1. **Filters over identifiers:** if N is structural or serves a topic of the `hitl_contact_*` family, it closes with no action.
2. **Direct outcomes:** `no_demand` and `notice_provenance` archive N; `memory_exhausted` raises its reservation (§3.13.10).
3. **`low_usage`:** with a trial under way, *immature neighbor*. Otherwise, it is resolved by `win_rate`: below `high_win_rate`, or undefined, it archives; at or above, it provisions a candidate with the widened floor, unless N violates the constraint, in which case it is archived, or the widening would undo a current narrowing, in which case it closes `no_action`.
4. **`outer_gradient`:** it provisions another instance of N's entry with the narrowed floor and, when it is ready, archives N.
5. **The comparison rule:** `low_score` goes by disqualification; `high_cost`, `high_latency` and `routine`, by tie-break. An N that is not evaluable under tie-break closes `no_action`.
6. **Neighborhood:** the entries close to N with nodes running. If the vector-space discard emptied it, it abstains.
7. **Comparison:** with an immature pair, *immature neighbor*; if a neighbor beats N, it archives N.
8. **If nobody beats it:** a split, if the strategy declares the niche heterogeneous; under disqualification, a replacement candidate; under tie-break, an exploration candidate if no healthy neighbor covers the niche, and `no_action` if one does.

Where the path would provision a candidate or narrow N, it closes `no_action` if N has a slot only the operator associates. Where it would provision a candidate, it also does so if the partition already has `max_concurrent_exploration` candidates; narrowing adds no nodes to the fleet, so that cap does not reach it.

**The filters: a node the lifecycle does not touch.** The quality axes are derived from the stream (§3.12.2), so a degraded structural component is observed and scored like any other and can cross the threshold. The Spawner closes the resulting `node_review` with a `task.completed` with no action: that `node_id` corresponds to no blueprint it provisioned in its Catalog (§3.13.3), so there is nothing to archive or replace. The remedy is the operator's, through the deployment platform (§2.1, §3.14.1). A `hitl_contact_*` channel is closed the same way, by the predicate on the topic that vacancy already evaluates (§3.13.2): the Network Monitor emits no `node_review` on it (§3.12.3), and the filter on the Spawner's side keeps the guarantee from depending only on whoever emits. The operator brings up and removes the channels (§3.13.8).

**The direct outcomes: there is nothing to compare.** With `no_demand` the demand disappeared, and what is archived is the whole niche rather than a competitor: a topic without assignments has no `win_rate` to read and no trial to protect. With `notice_provenance` the Logic Container violated its contract (§3.7.7), which is not a mediocre result but a confirmed fact, and leaving it competing while a replacement matures is the risk not worth running. The Spawner **archives directly** (§3.13.3, scale to zero + snapshot in the Catalog), with no neighborhood analysis and no blue/green. The entry archived for `notice_provenance` stays out of what vacancy chooses (§3.13.2), and bringing it up again is an operator's `node_provision`; the other instances of the same entry are not touched in this act, because they fall through their own `notice` or a veto takes them down (§3.13.8).

That is safe because the recovery path is already written and no new one is needed: archiving is never a physical delete (§3.13.3), so if a task of that niche shows up later, the vacancy is resolved through the missing-coverage branch of §3.13.2 by reviving or choosing another entry. The cost of having been wrong is one vacancy round of latency, not a loss of capacity—a trade-off explicitly accepted in exchange for the network not accumulating resident nodes indefinitely.

**`low_usage`: the usage axis archives or widens, and never replaces.** The trigger asserts that N spends most of its topic's assignments free while other nodes serve them (§3.12.3). There is nothing to replace, because others already cover N's demand: provisioning a candidate to compete for those tasks would duplicate the waste the review set out to eliminate. What remains to read is why N is free, and the `win_rate` of the same payload says it (§3.12.2):

* **`win_rate` below `high_win_rate`:** N proposes and loses, so the other nodes cover its topic's tasks better. It is surplus, and it is archived.
* **Undefined `win_rate`:** N never proposed. It is the hyper-specific node that outlived its purpose, others already serve what its topic receives, and it is archived.
* **`win_rate` at or above `high_win_rate`:** N wins almost everything it proposes, but it proposes little. Its floor silences it, or it is a specialist of a small region, and the strategy provisions a candidate with the widened floor: another instance of the same entry with a lower floor (§3.13.3). If N was a specialist, the candidate proves it by losing. If N also violates the constraint—the usage trigger can travel instead of the quality one (§3.12.3)—it is not widened and it is archived: a node that is little used and performs badly has nothing worth keeping.

**The widened candidate replaces N with no rule of its own.** The two share a vector, so the draw of §3.7.5 splits the tasks that fall in both radii, and the candidate wins those that fall only in the wider one. N stays free in most of its topic's assignments, fires `low_usage` again with a `win_rate` close to one half—below `high_win_rate`, whose range guarantees it (§2.3)—and is archived. If the widening fails again and again, the limit is that same one: N ends up archived for a low `win_rate`.

**How many nodes a niche has is regulated by two signals read in opposite directions, with no declared cap.** If there are too many nodes, they spend most of the assignments free, fire `low_usage` and the usage axis archives the one that loses. If there are too few, none is free, none fires, and vacancy adds capacity (§3.13.2). The Spawner does not need to know how many instances a niche has or keep a record of which ones it archived.

**The exception is a trial under way.** A blue/green puts two nodes to compete for the same flow, so each one spends free assignments it would otherwise have won (§3.12.2): on a low-volume niche that fires `low_usage` without the niche having received less work, and archiving there would cancel the trial over a division the trial itself produced. On `low_usage` the Spawner first runs the neighborhood search below—with the discard and abstention that govern it (§3.13.3)—adds the other instances of N's entry that run with another floor, and checks the `n_observations` of whatever it finds (§3.12.3):

> If any of those nodes has `n_observations < min_observations`, or N has it and has at least one of them, the trigger **enters the tree of §3.13.5** instead of archiving, and closes as *immature neighbor*. The ceiling of the trial is still set by the maturation expiry of that same section.

**The predicate is about a pair and not about the node, because the split lowers usage on both sides.** `snoozed` silences the node under review while it lasts, but that period is short by design (§3.13.5): once it expires, its own `low_usage` arrives here just like the candidate's. Asking about the maturity of the pair covers either of the two without having to tell them apart, which is what allows going on without any persisted link between them (§3.13.5)—and the derivation bound further down is what guarantees that the candidate falls within the neighborhood where this question finds it. A lone node, with nobody to split with, is not in a trial: its low usage is its own, and it is read by `win_rate`.

**`outer_gradient`: narrowing replaces N without competing.** The trigger asserts that N performs worse at the edge of its radius than near the center (§3.12.3). The Spawner provisions another instance of N's entry with a higher floor (§3.13.3), and when it is ready it archives N with that `trigger_reason` in the row of its floor. It compares no one and opens no trial:

* **The result is already measured.** The narrow instance is the same artifact with the same vector, so in the region it keeps it performs what N already performed there. Widening competes because its new territory has no evidence; narrowing gives up territory, and what remains does have it.
* **What is given up is covered by the auction or by vacancy.** The edge tasks are won by the neighbor that used to lose against N for being farther away (§3.7.5). If there is none, they are left vacant, and the missing-coverage branch looks for an entry closer to them or derives one (§3.13.2). N's entry is not a candidate there: another instance of it carries the floor of the one running (§3.13.3), and with that floor it would not have proposed.
* **It consumes no counters.** It provisions neither a replacement candidate nor an exploration one, so it does not count toward `max_successive_candidates` or toward the exploration backoff (§3.13.5). Its limit is the evidence: it stops when the edge stops performing worse, or when the outer group does not gather `min_observations` (§3.12.2).

**Widening does not undo a current narrowing.** The path does not provision a widened candidate whose floor is equal to or lower than that of a floor-history row of the entry archived for `outer_gradient` within `evidence_staleness`, and it closes `no_action`. Without that rule, giving up the edge leaves N freer, the usage axis widens it back to the floor just abandoned and the cycle does not end. Once the evidence expires, widening becomes legitimate again, because the edge may have changed.

**A node with a slot only the operator associates (§3.16.3) is archived, but not replaced.** The usage axis, the direct outcomes and *winning neighbor* archive it like any other, because shrinking the fleet widens no egress. What it does not receive is a new instance—neither the widened candidate, nor those of a split, nor the replacement or exploration one, nor the narrow instance—because none could receive that slot. Where the path would provision one, the Spawner closes the review with `no_action` and the reason, even if the node violates the constraint: replacing it is an operator's `node_provision`.

**The other triggers share the tree, but not the rule by which the comparison is made at the end.** The dividing line is **what is wrong** with N, and the trigger says it: an evaluable node that violates the constraint never arrives as a cost trigger or as a routine one (§3.12.3), so the Spawner applies the rule based on the trigger's word:

* **Disqualification rule**—`low_score`: N **violates the constraint**. It is out of the competition, so **cost does not enter the comparison**: any neighbor that satisfies the constraint beats it, even if it turns out more expensive. It is the objective function of §3.12.2 applied literally—quality is a constraint, not a term that can be traded.
* **Tie-break by confidence rule**—`high_cost`, `high_latency` and `routine`: N **satisfies the constraint and the room for improvement is in cost**. Here the objective is compared, with the criterion further down.

The three tie-break triggers are resolved identically: they differ only in the priority with which the Network Monitor emitted them (§3.12.1). In all three cases N is healthy and the question is the same: whether something exists that does the same thing more cheaply. **An N that is not evaluable under tie-break closes `no_action`**: there is nothing to compare it against, and a candidate provisioned there is spending blind.

**Each node's objective is a single one, and the strategy chooses it with a predicate over the metrics and never over the kind of node.** The vector always carries cost and duration (§3.12.2), and the Spawner never infers where a number came from, which would mean looking inside the node (§2.2). Cost and duration are not combined into a single index (§3.12.2).

On receiving a `node_review`, the Spawner needs to know whether the node in question can be replaced without risk or whether it is, in practice, the only coverage of its semantic niche. Answering this by literal topic is too coarse—the same topic can host several hyper-specific nodes that overlap only partially (§3.7.1). Instead, the Spawner reuses the same computation it already does every day to assign tasks (cosine similarity, Layer 2, §3.7.2), applied now between capability embeddings instead of between a task embedding and a node:

1. It searches the Catalog for blueprints with embedding similarity ≥ `agent_similarity_floor` (Global Configuration, §2.3)—distinct from the floor with which a node proposes (§3.7.5), because it measures coverage redundancy, not fitness for a specific task. N's entry stays out: its other instances are the same artifact, and N is not compared against itself. The search is governed by §3.13.3: it discards the entries whose `model_id` is not the current one and triggers the repair with that discard.
2. It cross-checks those neighbors with its own network's Active Node Registry (§3.11): a similar blueprint with a good reputation covers nothing if no instance of it is running right now.

   **If the neighborhood search's discard emptied the result, the tree does not go on.** "There is no healthy neighbor" and "I could not look" lead to opposite outcomes—"there is no healthy neighbor" leads to provisioning a candidate, which is the expensive action—so the Spawner abstains and leaves the `node_review` for when the comparison becomes valid again (§3.13.3). The node under review stays active in the meantime, which is the safe state: a degraded node working a while longer costs less than a candidate provisioned against a neighborhood nobody could read.
3. **If there is at least one active neighbor that beats the node under review:** coverage is already duplicated in practice and the Spawner archives the node under review directly (§3.13.3)—no blue/green is needed; the neighbors already cover that semantic space. What it means for a neighbor to **beat** it is fixed here, and it holds the same regardless of how each one reached the network: a candidate from a trial, one from a split or one that was already there. It is also the comparison with which vacancy chooses what to provision (§3.13.2), so what vacancy chooses is not undone later by a review with the same data.
   * **Only an evaluable neighbor competes.** If the neighbor or N has `n_observations < min_observations`, or the metric the strategy compares is undefined for them (§3.12.2), no comparison is possible: the case is *immature neighbor* of §3.13.5.
   * **A neighbor that violates the constraint never wins.**
   * **Under disqualification**, it is enough for the neighbor to satisfy the constraint. N does not satisfy it, so any coverage that does replaces it.
   * **Under tie-break by confidence**, it is not enough for the neighbor to be healthy—N is too—: it has to beat it on the objective **by a difference the strategy can tell apart from measurement noise**. The two are not two numbers but two estimators with different variances, and the one with less history is the more likely to have been lucky; comparing raw points would bias the cycle toward swapping healthy nodes because of noise, which is the most expensive error here. Which objective metric is compared, with what margin and how the age of each piece of evidence weighs belong to the strategy; what is fixed here is that **a difference indistinguishable from noise replaces no one**. A tie archives neither of the two: both nodes coexist, and if there is surplus capacity the usage axis trims it (further up).
   * **Under either rule, the predicate incorporates the radius.** A neighbor only counts as duplicated coverage if its `similarity_floor` is **equal to or lower** than that of the node under review: a higher floor covers a subset of that space, not the same thing, and the lifecycle moves each node's floor on its own, so two neighbors do not share a radius by construction. If the only neighbor that wins has a narrower floor than N, the case does not archive here.
   * **The cost of having provisioned either of the two does not enter.** It is sunk the moment both nodes exist, and the only thing left to decide is which of the two histories is more credible. The one that loses stays in the Catalog, available to revive (§3.13.3).
4. **If no neighbor beats it:** the Spawner never edits N in place—re-provisioning without a safety net would mean that, if the new version turned out worse, there would be no way back. What it does depends on what is wrong and on what covers the niche:

   * **A split**, if the strategy declares the niche heterogeneous and the niche tolerates the cut (§3.13.9): it provisions several candidates instead of one.
   * **Under disqualification, a replacement candidate.** N does not satisfy the constraint and nobody covers it, so another node is needed **to cover the same niche**.
   * **Under tie-break, an exploration candidate only if no healthy active neighbor covers the niche**—the *no neighbor* case of §3.13.5—: N is, in practice, unique in its niche, and the question of whether something would make it cheaper can only be answered by trying. **If a healthy neighbor covers it and does not beat it, it closes `no_action`**: coverage is duplicated and there is no improvement to capture with today's information.

   **A candidate comes from the same sequence of sources as vacancy** (§3.13.2), with N as the reference instead of a task: an archived Local Catalog entry that, with the comparison further up and the age of its evidence discounted (§3.13.3), would beat N; if there is none, the query to the Shared Catalog with N's `capability_text` (§3.13.6); and failing that, a derivation (§6). The candidate competes in parallel through the normal self-selection mechanism (§3.7.5)—nothing special, the same path as any node. The original stays active in the meantime, and `max_concurrent_exploration` (§2.3) bounds how many candidates can coexist in a partition, so that a streak of routine reviews does not double the active fleet.

   **The candidate is not a copy of the node under review, and that is precisely where the paths differ.** What is kept is the **external contract**—the same topic, and with it the same audience and the same place on the Bus—not the vector. A revived or imported blueprint brings its own `capability_text`, and its vector is regenerated in the network's space (§3.7.3, §3.13.3). A derived one receives **its own `capability_text`** to cover that niche—predictably similar to that of the node under review, not necessarily the same. There is no "same embedding, different logic" path **among these**: by §3.7.3 the vector is a pure function of the text and the model, so assigning the candidate the vector of the node under review is exactly copying its text, and copying the text is asserting that there was nothing better to propose. (The widened candidate of `low_usage` does keep N's vector, but it is not one of these paths: it is another instance of the same entry, and it adjusts what a node that already has coverage competes against.)

   > **Derivation bound:** a candidate's `capability_text` has to produce an embedding with similarity ≥ `agent_similarity_floor` (§2.3) against that of the node under review. It is the same bound with which step 1 decides coverage, exercised now when creating instead of when comparing.

   **The bound is not tidiness; it is what upholds §3.13.5.** No link is persisted between the node under review and its candidate: the relationship is re-derived on each review by this same neighborhood search. A candidate whose text fell outside the floor would stop being its neighbor, and the next review would provision another candidate on a niche that already has two nodes. Inside the floor, on the other hand, the candidate is a neighbor by construction and the cycle closes with no bookkeeping.

   **How that text is derived is node-creation procedure (§6)**, not something this section resolves. What is fixed here is the predicate the result must satisfy, which is what the rest of the cycle needs in order to reason.

   **And the two embeddings differing is what gives the candidate traffic.** Inside the floor but not identical, the candidate is closer than the node under review to some subregion of the niche and wins those tasks through the normal mechanism of §3.7.5, with no dedicated rule. The exact-tie case is covered by the deterministic draw of step 4 of §3.7.5, which keeps a tie from always going to the same node and leaving the candidate at zero tasks forever. They are two paths to the same requirement, and neither asks the Task Broker to know that a replacement is under way.

A single mechanism thus covers two cases that look different ("improving a node" and "replacing for being the only one of the specialty"): both are the same blue/green pattern. "Improving a node" is not a Spawner action—it is what is observed from outside when the new blueprint, with better internal logic, ends up replacing the old one after proving better performance of its own. What changed internally (prompt, invoked model, graph structure) is indistinguishable from here, and there is no need for it to be otherwise: for the Spawner it is a different `artifact_ref` competing (§3.13.3).

**What does not change:** the Task Broker keeps selecting exclusively by real-time semantic similarity (§3.1, §3.7.5); the quality score never enters that auction. **The tie draw of step 4 of §3.7.5 is not a crack in this**: it is triggered by a property of the proposals—two similarities indistinguishable at the declared precision—and not by anyone's lifecycle state. The Task Broker does not know which of the tied ones is a candidate or that a replacement is under way; if the blue/green benefits, it is as an effect of a rule that would be just as necessary without it. The score is an input exclusively of the node lifecycle (Spawner + Network Monitor), never of which node wins a specific task. Coupling real-time assignment to historical performance would repeat the same error §3.3 avoids by reopening with no preference for the previous owner.

#### 3.13.5. Snooze and replacement convergence

When the Spawner decides on blue/green for a node N (§3.13.4, point 4), no explicit link remains between N and the new candidate—no data structure and no field relating them. It is deliberate: the network avoids that kind of rigid bookkeeping everywhere—§3.3 lets the reopen compete again instead of recording who had the task before—; here the same principle holds, reusing the embedding neighborhood search (§3.13.4) to re-derive the relationship each time it is needed, instead of persisting it.

This requires solving two problems that leaving N active while the candidate builds up history introduces: (a) the Network Monitor would keep emitting `node_review` on N repeatedly, because its score does not change just because a candidate is on the way; and (b) the Spawner needs, on each successive review of N, to be able to tell "there is not enough information yet" from "there is information now and it is time to act".

**A uniform procedure, with no special branch for "replacement under way":** each time the Spawner processes a `node_review` on any node N—whether it is the first review, a later one on the same N, or one on the candidate that at some point replaced it and now also performs badly—it always runs the same procedure of §3.13.4 (neighborhood search, cross-check with the registry of active nodes, score query to the Network Monitor), classifying the result into the following outcomes:

* **`no_action`—there is no opportunity to pursue:** N satisfies the constraint and a healthy neighbor covers its niche without beating it, or N is not evaluable—there is no direction to move it in with today's information. Nothing is created or archived. It is the natural outcome of a routine review on a healthy node and also that of a false positive, and **existing cheaply is its point**: it is what makes the policy of triggering generously of §3.12.1 tolerable. It applies under the tie-break by confidence rule (§3.13.4), and under any rule when the instance that was due cannot be provisioned: N has a slot only the operator associates, the partition already has `max_concurrent_exploration` candidates, or the widening would undo a current narrowing (§3.13.4).
* **Direct archiving**—`no_demand`, `notice_provenance`, or `low_usage` with `win_rate` below `high_win_rate`, undefined, or on an N that violates the constraint (§3.13.4) → archives N.
* **Winning neighbor**—there is an active neighbor that beats N (§3.13.4) → archives N.
* **Reservation raise**—`memory_exhausted` → replaces N with another instance of its entry with a larger reservation, asks before raising it or removes the entry, according to the caps of §3.13.10.
* **Narrowing**—`outer_gradient` (§3.13.4) → provisions another instance of N's entry with the narrowed floor and archives N when it is ready.
* **Immature neighbor**—the trial on the niche is still open: there is a pair N belongs to with either of the two below `min_observations`, still building up history → **snooze**: it creates nothing new, there is already a candidate on the way; it only postpones N's next review. The condition looking at the pair is what gives an outcome to the `low_usage` that §3.13.4 makes an exception of, whether N is the node that originated the trial or the candidate.
* **Candidate**—another node needs to be tried: a replacement one under disqualification, an exploration one under tie-break when no healthy active neighbor covers the niche—the *no neighbor* case—, or the widened one of `low_usage` (§3.13.4) → provisions it and applies snooze to N.
* **Split**—the strategy declares the niche heterogeneous (§3.13.4) and the niche tolerates the cut (§3.13.9). It is not a new `trigger_reason` (it is decided on the same payload of §3.12.3) but one more outcome of this tree: instead of a single candidate, the Spawner provisions up to `m`. Each of those candidates, and N itself while active, then enters the same procedure of this section like any other node—there is no special retirement rule. The flow precondition and what is validated before provisioning live in §3.13.9.

*Candidate* covers both "first review of N" and "the previous candidate failed", without telling them apart explicitly—it is the same outcome. If the new candidate also performs badly, the Network Monitor detects it completely independently (it competes and is observed like any other node, §3.12.1) and at some point fires its own `node_review` on *it*; at that moment the same procedure runs again, now with N as one of the candidate's possible neighbors. The cycle converges without any node needing to know "who replaces whom"—the relationship is re-derived on each review, never persisted.

**The *immature neighbor* outcome needs a deadline, because "still maturing" may never end.** On its own, *immature neighbor* postpones with no exit condition: as long as the candidate does not reach `min_observations`, each review of N snoozes again and the trial stays open. And waiting is not enough, because the limit is not one of patience but arithmetic: **`n_observations` counts the node's current observations and not its history**—the window is a count, but `evidence_staleness` trims the ones that aged (§3.12.1). If the flow of tasks the candidate wins is lower than `min_observations / evidence_staleness`, old observations expire faster than new ones come in and the counter stalls at a fixed point below the minimum. It is not slow maturation: it is unreachable maturation, and provisioning a candidate makes it worse by construction, because it splits between two nodes the same flow with which one used to mature.

Without a deadline, the damage is twofold and permanent: the candidate holds a `max_concurrent_exploration` slot forever, and N stays `snoozed` indefinitely at the short cadence of `node_review_snooze`—without the growing backoff spacing it out, because that backoff only lengthens the `no_action` period. It is the same non-convergence the cycle seeks to avoid, coming in from the other side.

> **Maturation expiry:** if a candidate does not reach `min_observations` within `max_candidate_maturation` assignments granted on its topic (§2.3) since it was provisioned, the Spawner **archives the candidate** (§3.13.3) and the trial is declared expired. The node that originated the trial stays active.

  **The deadline is counted in assignments of the topic and not in time** (§3.12.1), because what this expiry judges is whether the candidate converted the opportunities it had. A trial on a niche that cools down simply does not advance until there is work again; if the niche does not come back, the candidate leaves anyway, but through `no_demand` (§3.12.3), which is the real reason.

* **Being wrong is cheap, for the usual reason:** archiving is never a physical delete. An expired candidate stays in the Catalog with its triple intact, available to revive if the niche gains volume—and if that happens, the way back is the one already written (missing-coverage branch of §3.13.2, or a candidate from here).
* **No new counter is needed: the existing ones absorb the outcome,** and they do so differently depending on what class of candidate it was, which is the distinction this section already drew. A replacement candidate consumes `max_successive_candidates` on expiring, just like one that competed and lost; if the niche reaches the cap, it escalates as `niche_uncoverable`, and that is the right escalation—a degraded node in a niche with so little traffic that no replacement can be evaluated **is** a condition a human has to decide, and no automatic machinery will resolve it better. An exploration one feeds the exponential backoff and never escalates: exploration on that niche becomes rare on its own, which is exactly what is appropriate when the niche does not provide the flow to explore it. The widened one consumes neither: its limit is the usage axis (§3.13.4). Nor does narrowing, which opens no trial (§3.13.4).
* **It is the only closure of a trial that does not mature**, and that is why the deadline carries so much weight. As long as the candidate stays below `min_observations` the usage axis does not reach it—the preamble of §3.13.4 makes an exception of its `low_usage` exactly for that reason—so both the candidate that wins **no** task and the one that wins some, does not fire `low_usage` and still never gathers enough evidence leave through here. What separates them is the deadline and not the outcome: the former uses up `max_candidate_maturation` without having moved its counter.

**Snooze mechanism:**

* The Spawner communicates it in the `task.completed` with which it closes the `node_review` (`data.action = "snoozed"` or `"no_action"` as the case may be, plus `data.snooze_until`), addressed back to the Network Monitor—which is the requester of that task (§3.9.1).
