* The Network Monitor respects `snooze_until` for that specific node before emitting `node_review` on it again. The rest of its work—observing and evaluating all the other nodes, including any new candidate—goes on unchanged.
* **What each outcome silences, which is the only thing the Network Monitor needs to interpret:**
  * `snoozed` **silences every trigger on N**, even an evidence one and even if its score is still below `score_floor`. That is correct: there is already a candidate on the way, and a new review would only re-derive the same conclusion at the cost of a cycle.
  * `no_action` **silences only routine triggers**. If after having closed with no action the node degrades or its cost drifts, that is new information and the review is emitted anyway—what the deadline spaces out is looking again *blind*, not reacting to an observed fact.
  * `capped` (further down) silences everything, **with no expiry**, until the human decides.
* Length of the deadline: `node_review_snooze` for `snoozed`; for `no_action`, the growing period of the next block. Both are Global Configuration parameters (§2.3).

**Different deadlines, because the outcomes mean different things.** `snoozed` says *"there is a candidate maturing"* and asks to come back soon, to see whether it has matured: that is `node_review_snooze`. `no_action` says *"there is nothing to do with today's information"*, and coming back soon would be pure waste: that is **`no_action_snooze`**, deliberately longer. Conflating them into a single deadline would make routine reviews repeat at the cadence of a replacement under way, which is the flow §3.12.1 bounds.

**And the `no_action` deadline grows with each fruitless review.** A node having been reviewed several times without anything surpassing it **is information about that node**—evidence that it has little margin left—and the policy uses it instead of discarding it. The shape is the same as task re-announcement (§3.1): **exponential backoff**, doubling `no_action_snooze` on each successive review under tie-break that does not archive N—a close with `no_action` or an exploration candidate—up to the ceiling `max_exploration_backoff` (§2.3). Also counting the exploration that did not archive N is what spaces out a routine review whose candidate failed to surpass it.

* **The ceiling is not a calibration detail: it is what keeps the backoff from becoming an accumulator.** Without it, an old node would become asymptotically unexplorable—and that would be wrong precisely for the reason that motivates exploring: the frontier moves for exogenous causes (§3.12.1), so *"nobody surpassed it in fifty attempts over three years"* cannot mean *never again*, if the new model came out yesterday. With a ceiling, the exploration frequency drops but never reaches zero.
* **The counter lives in the Specialty Catalog, on the Spawner's side** (§3.13.3), next to the successive-candidates one. The argument is the same as there: whoever closes a review without replacement is the Spawner, so writer and reader are the same component and there is no need to carry the value over the Bus. It is cleared when N is archived, which is the sign that the niche actually moved.
* **The Network Monitor does not need to know this counter exists.** It receives an ever more distant `snooze_until` and that is enough: **the growing deadline *is* the deprioritization**, expressed in the only field the two already exchanged. No field is added between the two components.
* **The backoff inherits from `no_action` what it silences**, according to the bullet further up: it spaces out blind exploration and never the reaction to an observed fact. If the model's price goes up again next month, the corresponding `high_cost` is emitted however backed off the node is.

**Retry limit:** nothing in the procedure above, on its own, prevents the cycle "create candidate → snooze → candidate fails → create another candidate" from repeating indefinitely if a specialty turns out to be consistently hard to cover well. A cap is needed, and it follows the same shape as `current_attempts` (§3.3): a counter of successive candidates on the same semantic niche, checked against `max_successive_candidates` (Global Configuration, §2.3) before provisioning a replacement candidate.

* **Where the counter lives: in the Specialty Catalog**, next to the niche's blueprint (§3.13.3), not in the payload of any event. Whoever creates candidates is the Spawner, so whoever can count them is the Spawner: writer and reader are the same component and there is no need to carry the value over the Bus. The Network Monitor does not need to know this counter exists—and could not maintain it, because it does not create candidates. It is incremented when a replacement candidate is provisioned and cleared when *winning neighbor* archives N, which is the sign that the niche ended up covered. It is the same field that the interventions on a vacant niche of §3.13.2 count—bringing up another instance, reviving, importing or deriving without the vacancy clearing—with that branch's own reset instead of this one; that is where the full predicate is, and why it counts the result and not the event.
* **Only a replacement candidate consumes it, and they are two counters and not one.** This is the interaction to watch between routine exploration and the coverage cap: a perfectly healthy node, explored several times with candidates that failed to surpass it, would keep incrementing this counter up to the cap; the niche would be marked `capped` and the Spawner **would open a `hitl_contact_operator` for it with `kind = "niche_uncoverable"`**—that is, the network interrupting a person to warn them that it cannot cover a niche that is covered without any problem. Failing to explore and failing to cover a niche are different facts, and only failing to cover it is a condition a human has to decide. Exploration candidates—under tie-break, whether the trigger is `routine`, `high_cost` or `high_latency`—therefore feed the backoff further up, which is their natural limit and escalates to no one, and **never `max_successive_candidates`**; replacement ones count here. A healthy node whose cost went up is not a niche the network fails to cover.
* **On reaching the cap, the Spawner stops creating candidates** for that niche and the original node stays active despite its score. It is a case of a "niche the network fails to cover well": it is not a failure of a specific task, so it does not enter the reopen loop of §3.3—it is a condition of the network that a human has to decide. The Spawner escalates it itself: it opens a `hitl_contact_operator` with `data.kind = "niche_uncoverable"`, stores its `TaskID` next to the `capped` mark and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` (§3.15.7).
* **And it suspends surveillance of the niche.** It closes that `node_review` with `data.action = "capped"` instead of `"snoozed"`: with no expiry deadline, the Network Monitor stops emitting `node_review` on N until the human decides. Without this, the Network Monitor would keep firing reviews—its score did not improve—and each one would spend a full cycle only to end in "I do nothing". The ways out the human can choose are the closed enumeration of §3.15.7, and each one falls entirely within the Spawner's domain: with `retry` it clears the counter and the `capped` mark and normal surveillance resumes; with `accept_current` the mark stays as it is—the original node stays active and no candidates are created—stamped with the decision so that nobody asks again; with `retire_niche` it archives the node (§3.13.3) and clears both, leaving the blueprint available to revive if the niche has demand again (§3.13.2, missing-coverage branch).

#### 3.13.6. Shared Catalog across networks: publication and governance

The Catalog (§3.13.3) is **private by default with respect to other tenants**—tenant scope, already shared among its networks as the rest of §3.13 describes—and "shared across tenants" is an opt-in extension on that base, not an ambiguous property of the component. The private case is not a special case of this section: it is the default behavior of everything already defined in §3.13.3–§3.13.5; what follows applies only to the network that decides, explicitly, to participate in a Shared Catalog.

**The catalog levels:**

* **Local Catalog** (§3.13.3): the source of truth of a tenant's networks for their Spawners. Nothing already defined changes.
* **Shared Catalog:** a federated service, external to any individual network, that aggregates blueprints explicitly **promoted** from the Local Catalogs of different Zobik deployments. No network queries or writes the Shared Catalog by default—it is an additional integration that a network operator turns on, similar in spirit to deploying an emergent role the network did not come with (§2.1). **The network reaches it through egress:** the link is an `egress_registry` entry (§3.16.2) whose operations are the acts of this section—query, acquire, promote, report, notify a scan rejection—and turning on the integration is registering that entry. Unlike the Local Catalog, **it declares its own vector space**, independent of that of any participating network (§2.3): see "Canonical vector space" further down.

**Promotion: local → shared, never the reverse implicitly.** A blueprint does not reach the Shared Catalog just by existing in a local one: **the operator of the origin network promotes it, as an explicit action, and the Spawner executes it.**

**Why the operator and not the Spawner.** It is not a governance choice but a limit of what the network can know. Since the node's internal logic lives entirely inside the `artifact_ref` (§3.13.3), there is no per-field granularity to offer: promoting the artifact is promoting the complete know-how, and that artifact was derived from the tasks of a concrete use case. No component of the network can guarantee that it does not carry anything private from that use case, because none reads inside the node (§3.13.3) or interprets the text of a task (§3.13.1), so the Spawner is in no position to assess that risk. Only the owner of the use case is. It is exactly the shape §3.14.2 gives to the credential of an audience channel: **the Spawner's signature is not enough; it takes an operator proof that the Spawner can carry but not fabricate.**

**The trigger is that action and nothing else.** There is no periodic sweep of the Catalog looking for candidates, it is not evaluated when processing a `node_review`, and a blueprint that comfortably clears the floors below is not promoted on its own. The operator queries `node_catalog_query` (§3.13.3), chooses, and their channel emits `node_catalog_publish` with `data.kind = publish` and the signed proof (§3.17.1); the Spawner that owns `data.target_topic` selects itself, just as for `node_review`, except on a bundle (further down). The complete walkthrough is in §5.5.

**Any Local Catalog entry, active or archived.** The Shared Catalog is the federated equivalent of the Local Catalog, not of the Active Node Registry, which §3.11 declares local by nature and meaningless outside the network that keeps it. Making promotion conditional on the blueprint running would couple the export path precisely to the store that does not cross the boundary. That is why what the operator looks at to choose is `node_catalog_query` and not the fleet census (§3.12.3): the query that spans what is promotable is the one that reads the promotable store. The floors below are evaluated just the same on an archived entry, because archiving persists the snapshot next to the blueprint (§3.13.3), and they filter themselves: a blueprint archived for `low_score` does not pass them. The ones that do pass them are those archived by the usage axis (§3.12.3)—good quality, and here they were surplus or their demand went away—and they are the best candidates in the whole system: here they are no longer needed; on another network they may be. The `trigger_reason` of the archiving says so directly, without having to read it from the number.

The minimum requirements promotion is subject to—the Spawner verifies the local reputation floor before pushing; the Shared Catalog, on receipt, checks security and publisher reputation:

1. **A local reputation floor of its own, stricter than the one for internal use:** `min_observations_publish` / `score_floor_publish` (Global Configuration, §2.3), distinct from the thresholds of §3.12.1—a blueprint must have shown quality *within* its origin network, with more observations than are enough simply to keep it active, before it is a candidate to leave it.

   **It is an admissibility precondition, not a decision criterion.** There is no moment when the floor "chooses" what to promote: the operator is who promotes, and what the floor does is bound what they can promote. A blueprint that does not qualify is not promoted even if the operator asks for it, and the Spawner returns the rejection with its reason; one that qualifies is not promoted until someone asks for it. Both sides of that asymmetry matter—a blueprint that does not qualify not being promoted is what makes the publisher's reputation mean something; one that qualifies waiting for someone to ask for it is what keeps the decision where it belongs.

2. **Automatic security check at publication time:** the Shared Catalog scans the artifact before accepting the promotion—static analysis of the content of the `artifact_ref`, which is the same object whether the node is deterministic or semantic (§3.13.3): code and scripts in one case, prompts and model configuration in the other, all inside the same image. It looks for embedded secrets, credentials and injection patterns. It is a deterministic check with zero token cost, the same criterion that governs everything that can be resolved without an LLM (§3.6, opening principle).

   **It is an Admission Scanner (§2.1) of the catalog's own, not that of any participating tenant**, because a Shared Catalog is federated across tenants. That is why **the `scan_result` that travels with a promoted entry does not count as a verdict in the importing tenant**—it was issued by a scanner that tenant does not control, under a ruleset it did not choose—and the import goes through the tenant's Admission Scanner like any other registration (§3.13.3). Within a tenant the verdict is reused by digest, because there the scanner is a single one (§2.1).
3. **Publisher reputation, separate from that of the blueprint:** the Shared Catalog keeps, per origin network, a signal of its own—what fraction of its promoted blueprints ended up marked as malicious or low quality by other networks (see "Revocation" further down). A network with a bad publication record can lose the ability to promote without additional review, without that affecting the reputation of the blueprints it already promoted correctly. This separation is what answers "who is responsible?": there is no central curator approving each blueprint; there is an identifiable origin network whose own reputation is at stake.
4. **`capability_text` mandatory; the vector, never:** the blueprint's capability text (§3.13.3) is a condition of promotion, because it is the only thing that allows rebuilding the embedding in a space different from that of the origin network (§3.7.3). The **local vector is not promoted**—the catalog discards it even if the publisher attaches it, and recomputes it itself in its canonical vector space. Promoting a vector is promoting one network's vector space to another that does not share it.
5. **The `artifact_ref` is optional, and its absence is what separates two levels of promotion.** It is the direct consequence of promoting the artifact being promoting the complete know-how, the same fact by which the operator decides the promotion: there is no per-field granularity, so the only choice available is to attach it or not. The publisher makes it per blueprint and not as a global catalog policy:

   * **Full promotion** (`capability_text` + `attempt_window` + `artifact_ref` + Node Runtime Interface version + `memory_required`): the importing network can **rebuild the node**, whether deterministic or semantic. In exchange, it exposes its internal logic to every participant of the catalog.
   * **Metadata-only promotion** (`capability_text` + reputation): it serves for **coverage decisions** (§3.13.4)—knowing that the niche can be covered and with what quality it was covered elsewhere—and nothing else. The importing network knows what can be built, not how.

   The tension admits no single default: **there is no middle ground** in which something instantiable is promoted without promoting the know-how that makes it work, and that holds equally for a deterministic node and for a semantic one (§3.13.3). Making `capability_text` mandatory reopens nothing: the capability text describes *what* the node knows how to do, not *how* it solves it, so requiring it does not force publishing internal logic. A publisher who still considers their capability text sensitive can rewrite it when promoting—with the explicit trade-off that the `shared_score` aggregated further down becomes attributed to a description different from the one that was measured locally.

   **The interface version travels with the artifact and the importing network validates it.** A promoted `artifact_ref` was built against another deployment's substrate, so compatibility is not transitive: the importing Spawner checks that version against its own `runtime_interface_range` (§2.3) before instantiating, exactly as when reviving an archived blueprint of its own (§3.13.2, missing-coverage branch). A blueprint that does not fit the local range is still useful as metadata; it is simply not instantiated.

   **The blueprint's `egress_required` travels too, and it is resolved against the importing network's `egress_registry`.** A `target`, a registry key or a handle only mean something in the deployment that declared them (§3.16), so what crosses the boundary is the slots with their interfaces, which fix the shape of each call and not its destination (§3.16.3). The importing Spawner associates them against its own registry (§2.3) before instantiating, and if any is not associated the outcome is the same as that of an interface version out of range: it serves as metadata and is not instantiated, until the operator registers or chooses the entry through the paths of §3.16.5. The requirement being explicit is what avoids the silent failure mode—an imported node that starts and fails every task because it cannot resolve a credential nobody gave it.

**`topics_announced` travels too, in the same shape as `egress_required`.** It is the list of domain topics the blueprint announces—a declaration about the entry, never an inspection of the artifact (§3.13.3)—and each topic can carry the **fixed texts** with which the blueprint announces it when it does not write them on each announcement (§3.7.4). The list is what allows verifying closure without the blueprint ever having run on the destination network: that every topic it announces is claimed by another blueprint already active, or is itself covered by the importing network; the fixed texts also allow verifying that that claimant wins the announcement (further down). **It is verified on import and on seeding, and never enforced at runtime**: an Integration Sidecar that rejected an announcement on an undeclared topic would break the topic minting of §3.7.6, where a node proposes new topics without asking permission, and one that rejected a text different from the declared one would take away from the Logic Container the decision of what to announce (§3.7.4). An incomplete declaration is indistinguishable from any other vacancy: the uncovered topic is simply left vacant and the Spawner handles it like any other (§3.13.2).

**A Shared Catalog entry can declare a price**, which the network pays when acquiring it (further down). The acquisition cost of §3.13.3 records it as `purchased`, so that a price is not confused with a derivation cost when estimating how much it costs to build.

**Canonical vector space: the catalog has its own.** Each network chooses its own as a Global Configuration parameter (§2.3), so two federated networks do not share a space except by coincidence. The Shared Catalog therefore declares **its own `model_id`**, and on accepting a promotion **regenerates the embedding from the `capability_text`** under that space. All the catalog's blueprints thus end up in a single space, comparable among themselves, whatever network published them. Consequences that bound the reach of that vector:

* **The canonical embedding never enters runtime.** It is not signed, does not travel in any credential (§3.14.2), and is never compared against a `task_embedding` or against the embedding of an active node. It is a catalog index and nothing more: it exists to rank and deduplicate blueprints *within* the Shared Catalog.
* **Importing is regenerating again, locally.** The importing network's Spawner generates the embedding from the `capability_text` under the model of *its* Global Configuration, and signs it at provisioning time—the same act it already performs when creating any node (§3.7.3, §3.14.2), with no new authority and no special path by provenance. If its `model_id` matches the canonical one, reusing the catalog's vector is a valid optimization, not a separate case. There are therefore **two regenerations**: one when promoting, in the catalog's space; another when provisioning, in the network's.

**Querying the catalog is querying by text, not by vector.** It follows from the above: a network that looks for candidates on a vacancy (missing-coverage branch, §3.13.2) cannot send a vector of its own against an index in another space. It sends the **text**—that of the vacant request, or the `capability_text` of the niche it wants to cover—; the catalog embeds it in its canonical space, ranks and returns the candidates with their `capability_text` and their `shared_score`. The decision to instantiate is still the Spawner's, and local regeneration only happens on the chosen blueprint; a candidate whose `artifact_ref` the network vetoed stays out of the choice (§3.13.8). The import that does not wait for a vacancy is requested by the operator, through `node_catalog_import` (§3.13.8). The *local* neighborhood check of §3.13.2 and §3.13.4 does not change at all: it always operates on the Local Catalog, where all vectors share the network's model—not by construction (an assumption that a runtime model change breaks) but because §3.13.3 restores it by convergence each time it stops being true.

**The query for a candidate is a closed step of the Spawner**, and the missing-coverage branch (§3.13.2) and the replacement and exploration candidates (§3.13.4) use it. It receives a text and a topic—the text of the vacant task, or the `capability_text` of the node under review—and returns a suitable candidate, already registered in the Local Catalog, or none. Whoever uses it sees nothing else, so what happens inside is fixed here and changed here:

1. It queries the catalog by text, as the previous paragraph fixes.
2. It discards metadata-only candidates, those whose `artifact_ref` the network vetoed (§3.13.8) and those with a denied acquisition on this network (further down).
3. It orders the remaining ones putting first those without a price, and within each group by `shared_score`, reading along with it its dispersion and `n_networks` (further down).
4. It takes the first one that, with the embedding regenerated in the network's space, passes the filter of whoever queries—on a vacancy, that with `task_similarity_floor` it would have proposed for the task; on a node, the derivation bound against it (§3.13.4)—and, if it has a price, also meets the provisioning preconditions (§3.13.2): its Node Runtime Interface version falls within `runtime_interface_range`, and each slot of its `egress_required` is associated without asking (§3.16.3). It acquires it (further down) and executes on it the act of registration of an import (§3.13.3): the tenant's Admission Scanner analyzes it, and the entry ends up with `provenance` = `imported`, or `purchased` with the price paid as acquisition cost. If the acquisition is not executed or the registration fails, it moves on to the next candidate.

A registered candidate is one more Local Catalog entry, and whoever queried provisions it, with the same preconditions as any other.

**And the catalog itself can change model.** The same problem, one level up—with the difference that here it is tractable: since the catalog stores each blueprint's `capability_text`, regenerating its entire space is an operation **local to a service**, with no coordination with the participating networks, which do not read that vector for anything at runtime. No network finds out, and no deployed instance is affected.

**The shared score is a selection criterion, not a runtime score.** The signals of §3.12.2 do not measure the blueprint in isolation: they measure the interaction between the blueprint and the context of the network that hosts it. `reopen_rate` depends on which requester evaluates the result and with what expectation; the cost axis, on the difficulty of the tasks that topic receives on that network, on the prices that network pays and on what coverage the neighbors around it have. A blueprint with a high reputation in the catalog can perform badly—or turn out expensive—on a concrete network without any of the measurements being wrong: they are measurements over different populations. Hence the rule that governs both levels:

> **The shared score is read only before the node exists. Once instantiated, the network is governed exclusively by its local score.**

Its legitimate uses are therefore prior to provisioning: choosing which blueprint to instantiate on a vacancy (missing-coverage branch, §3.13.2) and choosing the candidate for a node under review (§3.13.4). For both it is enough for it to **rank** candidates; it does not need to be calibrated on the same scale as the local score, and it is never mixed with it. An imported blueprint, once running, is for the Network Monitor a freshly provisioned node like any other: its cold start is already resolved generically by `min_observations` (§3.12.1), the snooze of §3.13.5 and the incomplete opportunity window that leaves its usage undefined, with no need for special treatment by provenance.

**Aggregation: a volume-weighted mean, plus the dispersion.** A blueprint's shared score is computed as the average weighted by the `n_observations` of each network that reports it:

`shared_score = Σ(n_i · score_i) / Σ(n_i)`

Weighting by `n_i` keeps a network with marginal traffic from moving the mean as much as one that exercised the blueprint at large scale, with no need for a manual per-network weighting parameter. But more volume is not more similarity to the querying network, so the mean alone hides precisely what matters to decide an import: the catalog publishes next to it **`n_networks`** (how many distinct networks report) and a **dispersion measure** among the `score_i`. Two blueprints with a mean of 0.85 are opposite cases if one sustains it evenly across twelve networks and the other averages 0.95 on three with 0.4 on two: the second is context-sensitive, and the Spawner evaluating it can read that dispersion as a risk of landing on the bad side. It is information the catalog already has by construction and that collapsing to a scalar throws away.

**Acquiring is an operation of the catalog's egress entry.** Its fields are the candidate, its price and the `purpose` of whoever requests it: `vacancy`, `replacement` or `exploration` from the query above, and `operator_request` from a `node_catalog_import` (§3.13.8). An entry with no rate is acquired at price zero. How much the network spends without asking, how much with the operator's approval and above how much it does not buy are the `rules` of that entry (§3.16.2) on the price and the `purpose`: `allow`, the `operator` audience and `deny`. The question is the `gate_approval` of §3.16.4, and while it is answered the Spawner holds the task it is querying for by renewing its lease. What the operator needs to decide and the gate does not carry—the candidate's `shared_score` with its dispersion and `n_networks`, and what the network's `derived` entries cost (§3.13.3)—the Operator Channel fetches by pull, with the rule of §3.15.4: its absence degrades the presentation of the question and never its delivery.

**Whether buying or deriving pays off is decided by the `allow` threshold the operator sets.** The sequence of sources of §3.13.2 puts the Shared Catalog before the Forge, so an entry with a price within that threshold is acquired before requesting a derivation.

**With a price, the provisioning preconditions are evaluated before paying**, because paying for an artifact the network cannot bring up is spending with no node. What remains for after payment is the analysis by the tenant's Admission Scanner, which needs the image: a `fail` verdict makes the registration fail and loses the price, and the same `rules` bound that loss.

**A Shared Catalog artifact that the Admission Scanner rejects is reported to the catalog.** The Spawner that executed the registration does it, with the operation of the catalog's entry that carries the digest, the `ruleset_version` and the rules the artifact broke. It is a fact the tenant observed and not an assertion about the publisher, so it carries no operator proof and is not a `report` (further down). What the catalog does with it is its own governance, like what it does with what is already published (further down).

**An acquisition the operator denied is not asked again.** When the gate comes back with `deny` or without an answer, the Spawner stamps the denied-acquisition key, per Shared Catalog entry and price (§3.13.3), and from then on the query discards that candidate. A different price is a different question, and an operator who changes their mind brings in the entry through `node_catalog_import` (§3.13.8), which does not read that key. A `deny` from the rules is not stamped: it is evaluated on each acquisition, so an edit of the rules governs from the next one.

**Without credit, the search goes on and the operator finds out once.** On a rejection for insufficient credit, the Spawner moves on to the next candidate, stamps the exhausted-credit key (§3.13.3) and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` with `kind` = `catalog_credit_exhausted` (§3.15.7). The key is deleted with the next priced acquisition that executes.

**What the network requires from the Shared Catalog in order to acquire.** The catalog is a separate service, and the above depends on these properties of it:

* **The account belongs to the tenant, and the catalog charges against a credit the tenant loads.** The network authenticates with that account's credential, behind the entry's `credential_handle` (§3.16.6). The balance is the accumulated cap on what the network spends on acquiring, which §3.16.2 leaves to the destination system.
* **What decides an acquisition can be read without paying:** the price, the `capability_text`, the `shared_score` with its dispersion and `n_networks`, the Node Runtime Interface version, the `egress_required`, `topics_announced` and the digest of the `artifact_ref`. Payment enables downloading the image.
* **The acquisition carries the accepted price**, and the catalog charges that price or rejects.
* **Acquiring is idempotent per account and `artifact_ref`, and payment is one-time per artifact:** acquiring again what the account already paid for does not charge. That is what makes repeated acquisition through an expired lease or by two Spawner instances harmless, with no arbitration in the network. Another version is another digest and another acquisition.
* **Rejections are distinguishable:** insufficient credit, price different from the accepted one, revoked entry.

**The bundle: the unit of promotion of an entire fleet.** Besides a single blueprint, an operator can promote a **bundle**: the set of all the active nodes of their network, never a chosen subset—membership and activity are the same fact, so there is no selection to make and no criterion to fix about which ones go in.

A bundle carries a **`purpose_text`**, the description of what that entire fleet is for. It is not a `capability_text`: a bundle never competes (further down), so it has no `similarity_floor` and no signed embedding, and its vector, if it exists, is a catalog index just like the rest of the canonical vector space further up—it never enters runtime. The operator writes it, by the same argument by which they decide to promote: only the owner of the use case can say what their fleet is for.

**Closure, at different moments:**

* **On publishing**, it is strict: every domain topic a member announces is claimed by another member. The publishing Spawner computes it by observation, cross-checking against its own Active Node Registry (§3.11)—the same cross-check it already does when archiving, when searching for active neighbors and when answering `node_catalog_query` (§3.13.3).
* **On importing**, it is relative: a member of the bundle claims it, or it is already covered on the destination network. It is computed against the `topics_announced` of each member (further up) together with the importing network's local Topic Registry and Active Node Registry.
* **On seeding**, it is strict as on publishing and computed over the declarations as on importing, because the seed bundle comes in through deployment and none of its members has run yet (§6.1). The deployment computes it before the first `node_provision`.

**A topic with fixed texts is also checked by similarity.** When a member's `topics_announced` brings the fixed texts of a topic (further up), closure requires that the cosine between each of them and the `capability_text` of whoever claims the topic exceed the floor that claimant competes with—that of its active instance when there is one, and otherwise `task_similarity_floor` (§2.3)—under the `embedding_model` of the network that computes it. A topic without a fixed text is checked only by name, because a text the emitter writes on each announcement does not exist before the blueprint runs.

Both are only about domain topics: fixed-convention ones (§3.7.7) exist in every network by construction and are exempt, because otherwise no bundle would ever close.

**Any Spawner instance claims the publishing or bringing in of a bundle.** The request carries no `data.target_topic`, because a bundle has one topic per member, and it needs no owning partition because neither of the two acts touches the fleet: publishing reads the Catalog and the Active Node Registry and pushes the set, and bringing in is the act of registration, which does not decide which nodes exist (§3.13.3). Both stores are shared across partitions, so the argument by which any instance answers `node_catalog_query` holds (§3.13.3). Instantiating a bundle does touch the fleet, and that is why the network does not receive it as a request: the Operator Channel expands it into one `node_provision` per member (§3.17.1), and each one is claimed by the partition of its topic.

**A bundle requires full promotion of all its members.** The choice between full and metadata-only promotion (further up) is per single blueprint; a bundle of members without an artifact is not instantiable, which is the only thing the object exists for.

**It is a unit of packaging and never a runtime object.** The sequence of a flow lives in no object: it is distributed among the blueprints, in which subtasks each one announces and on which topics, and a decomposition is only expressible over a set of blueprints. The bundle names that set and allows verifying its closure before instantiating it, but it dissolves in the act: on import, its members become independent Catalog entries (§3.13.3) and nothing on the Bus knows the bundle existed.

**It is an immutable snapshot.** If the publishing network later archives one of its members, the already published bundle is not updated on its own—promoting a new fleet configuration is publishing a new bundle, consistent with the Catalog never doing a physical *delete* (§3.13.3).

**Revocation and unpublishing: a single machine with two initiators.** Marking a shared blueprint as not importable is the same operation whether whoever hosts it or whoever published it asks for it, so it is a single mechanism on the same topic, and what separates the initiators is the `data.kind`:

* **`report`**—the importing network asserts that someone else's blueprint is malicious or low quality. **Who detects it and who reports it are different:** the Network Monitor measures (§3.12.1) and the Spawner acts locally by archiving, but reporting outward is not an action on the network's own fleet—it is a public assertion about a third party's artifact, which consumes the publisher reputation of whoever issues it. None of the Spawner's decisions covers it, and "a network" is not an actor that can sign anything. The operator issues it, through the same path and with a proof of the same shape as promotion.
* **`unpublish`**—the operator who published withdraws their own blueprint.

The asymmetry between the two is one of reach, not of mechanism: `report` also adjusts the original publisher's reputation; `unpublish` does not. And "low quality" could in principle be derived from the local score, but **"malicious" is not derivable from any measurement**—the signals of §3.12.2 measure performance, not intent—so the operator's gate covers both cases instead of splitting the path into an automatic one and a manual one.

**No central authority over others' instances.** In either of its forms, the Shared Catalog marks the blueprint as revoked: no new network can promote an update on it or import it for the first time, and if it came through `report` the original publisher's reputation is adjusted. About instances already deployed on networks that imported it before, the protocol guarantees nothing, and does not even guarantee knowing they exist: the Shared Catalog keeps the record of which networks imported a blueprint, but that record is not a control channel—there is no event that reaches them and no way to verify what they run today. Each Spawner keeps being governed by the *local* score observed on its own active instances (§3.12.1). An operator who unpublishes cuts the flow going forward and nothing more.

**What happens with what is already published is the catalog's governance, not the network's.** Whether an unpublished blueprint nobody imported is really deleted, how long the import record lasts: these are policies of the federated service, external to any individual network, and they change no guarantee of the protocol. What the protocol does fix is the **publisher→blueprint link of the reputation record: it is permanent, and no catalog policy reaches it.** On it the publisher's reputation rests its whole answer to "who is responsible?"—there is an identifiable origin network whose reputation is compromised by what it published, and it stays so after unpublishing it.

#### 3.13.7. Vector space migration: changing the embedding model at runtime

Changing `embedding_model` (§2.3) redefines the space in which everything is compared. §3.7.3 makes it *executable* by making every embedding a recomputable derivative of a stored text, and §3.13.3 fixes the invariant with which the Catalog is read while the space is mixed. This section gathers the rest: what moves on its own, what has to be repaired, what happens to what is already running, who does it and how the operator knows it is finished.

**The change splits into asymmetric halves, and only one is a procedure.**

| Half | What moves it | Cost |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Generation**—the `task_embedding` of each `task.announced` | the Integration Sidecar reads the current parameter in its own cycle (§3.6.1) | instantaneous and atomic for the whole network; zero restarts |
| **Comparison**—the vector of each entry of the Catalog and of the Topic Registry | the Spawner's sweep, triggered on demand when reading the store (§3.13.3) | convergent and with no coordination; it runs even with the network paused |
| **Signature**—the capability embedding inside the credential | re-provisioning: it is a signed, static artifact (§3.14.2) | one restart per node; **it is the whole procedure** |

Generation needs no coordination of any kind: the Integration Sidecar is a generic process deployed by whoever operates the substrate, not per blueprint (§3.13.3), so there is no Logic Container to touch and no node to re-provision for the network to start announcing in the new space. Comparison converges on its own, through the sweep described right below. Signature is the one that costs, and it is the only population that requires a procedure.

**Re-provisioning carries the `similarity_floor` along without touching it** (§3.13.3). A cosine does not mean the same thing in two spaces, so the inherited floor ends up miscalibrated, and **what reframes it is the node lifecycle and not this procedure**, through different paths depending on which way it shifted. One that ended up wide shows up as a degraded score and blue/green corrects it against new evidence (§3.13.4). One that ended up narrow shows up as `low_usage` with a high `win_rate` and is widened (§3.13.4). Archived entries do not carry the old floor: their history rows belong to a `model_id` that is no longer the current one, so they come back with `task_similarity_floor` (§3.13.3) and are recalibrated through the same paths. It is the same convergent invariant with which §3.13.3 repairs the Catalog—what is enforceable is that the result is correct in the end, not that the migration gets it right in one go.

**What stays out**, so that the scope is bounded in writing:

* **The Shared Catalog** declares its own space and regenerates at both boundaries on its own; its vector never enters runtime (§3.13.6).
* **Structural nodes**, whose credential the Spawner does not sign (§3.14.2). They are not left behind because they do not depend on the vector space: their admission does not go through similarity (§3.7.5, step 4).

**The Catalog sweep: the discard is the evidence and the trigger.** The day the model changes, the Catalog is temporarily populated by entries from two spaces, and the neighborhood searches of §3.13.2 and §3.13.4 would return plausible numbers that decide archiving or duplicating. The invariant of §3.13.3 closes it on demand: a discard for a stale `model_id` is not just a filter, it is evidence that the Catalog is half-regenerated, and the Spawner responds by regenerating **all** stale entries—active and archived blueprints, plus the Topic Registry—from their source text. The total scope is not overzealousness: the neighborhood is searched by similarity and not by topic, so partial coverage does not give a precise answer. And since the trigger is demand, a regeneration left incomplete—by a crash, by an instance that died halfway—resumes on its own the next time someone queries an affected niche, instead of remaining as silent corruption waiting for someone to notice it.

**It does not need to know whether another one is already running.** Asking would require a flag shared among instances, and there is no need: §3.7.3 fixes that the vector is a pure function of `{source_text, model_id}`, so two Spawners regenerating the same entry write the same value. The property declared is not "it starts only if there was no other" but that **the result is the same whether it runs once, twice, or N times in parallel**—stronger, and with no coordinator and no new state. The only thing duplicated is the spending on model calls; dampening it with a claim per entry is an implementation optimization, outside the contract, just like the internal organization of the Network Monitor's aggregate (§3.12.1).

**Regenerating entries of another partition is legitimate.** The Spawner is partitioned by `topic` and the Catalog is shared, so a total sweep writes entries that another instance "owns". It does not contradict §3.13.1: the partition governs *which specialties exist*—creating, archiving, provisioning—not who recomputes a cache over data already written. The vector is cache and the text is primary; recomputing it decides nothing about the niche.

**While the repair runs, the decision in progress does not wait.** The Spawner is the only authority that generates embeddings (§3.13.1) and the `capability_text` is in the same entry it just discarded, so it regenerates on the spot the few entries blocking its comparison and answers with a complete neighborhood, instead of deferring. It can afford it because its path is not latency-critical: §3.13.2 already says that creation "can take time". The side effect is a demand-driven priority order—what actually gets in the way is repaired first.

**The abstention predicate is per query, not against the state of the network.** Tying it to `network_admission = frozen` (§3.18) would fail in both directions—that pause covers several causes and only one is the model change, and an early unpause would give free rein with entries still stale—besides adding a second reader of admission in a lifecycle decision, when the only enforcement point is the Task Broker.

**And the abstention is recorded, not just exercised.** On abstaining because the inline regeneration was not possible, the Spawner stamps the Catalog's irreparable-vector-space key (§3.13.3) with the pending entries, the `model_id` and since when. It serves several purposes at once, and that is why it is a single write: it holds the operator query the Spawner opens at that moment (`vector_space_unrepairable`, §3.15.7), chooses which instance opens it when several stumble on the same thing, and **gives the corresponding `notice` the authoritative state the invariant of §3.8.2 requires of it**—without it, the broadcast condition would be internal behavior no component can verify on its own. It is cleared when a later regeneration leaves the space consistent, which is also the moment the condition stops being true.

**Abstaining is the cheap error**, by the asymmetry §3.13.4 already explicitly accepts in another context: being wrong by not acting costs latency and never coverage. Creating in doubt produces the expensive error—a duplicated blueprint that persists and that later has to be detected with the same neighborhood machinery that was broken when it was created. And it costs no new mechanism: §3.13.2 already solves returning a task simply by not renewing the lease, and the count of vacancy rounds is derived by observation, so it keeps growing while the Spawner abstains—when the comparison becomes valid again, it acts immediately without having lost the signal.

**On the cost, which is not a decision of the network.** A total sweep is as many model calls as the Catalog has entries, triggered at the moment a query needs them and not at the one the operator chose—even with the network paused for an unrelated cause. There is nothing here the network should moderate on its own: **whoever changes the embedding model has already made the decision to pay for the regeneration**, because it is the same act (§2.3, §3.7.3). Executing it is fulfilling that decision, not extending it. It is moreover bounded by construction—once the triple is written the mismatch disappears, so the work is not repeated—and it is the cheaper of the two alternatives: a mixed vector space does not save the spending; it trades it for coverage decisions made on meaningless numbers.

**Nobody has to wait for the sweep to unpause.** The Catalog's vector **is not a credential**—it is minted when provisioning—so reviving a blueprint with an old vector does not make it unable to compete: the Spawner regenerates from `capability_text` in the act of provisioning, just as when importing (§3.13.6). That vector only needs to be up to date for neighborhood comparisons to be valid, and the sweep answers for that. What decides the unpause is the signature.

**Fleet migration trigger: passive detection, with no event.** The Spawner compares the current parameter against the `model_id` of its active fleet's credentials (§3.13.1). Nobody notifies it and there is nothing to subscribe to—the mismatch is self-evident and is not lost if the process dies.

**The step, which introduces no mechanism.** For each active node of its partition that is not a `hitl_contact_*` channel, the Spawner takes the `capability_text` from the Catalog entry (§3.13.3), generates the embedding under the current model, signs the credential and **re-provisions the same `artifact_ref`** against the orchestrator—the same path it already uses to provision and revive—stamping the new `model_id` and its `signer_id` as deployment metadata (§3.11). "Re-issuing credentials in bulk" needs no new channel because the credential is delivered to the Integration Sidecar at provisioning time (§3.14.2): **re-signing *is* re-provisioning**. It is not blue/green and does not go through §3.13.4—the external contract does not change, there is no candidate to compete and no performance to compare; it is the same node with its credential up to date. What it costs is the restart. **Channels stay out, like structural nodes:** their admission does not depend on the vector space (§3.7.5, step 4; §3.14.2), so the census shows them with the `model_id` of their provisioning and the unpause does not have to wait for them.

**No bookkeeping, and resumable by construction.** The Spawner is already partitioned by `topic`, so each instance migrates its fleet with no coordinator and no fan-out. And since each deployment carries its `model_id`, **the registry is the progress mark**: the operation is idempotent (re-signing an already migrated node writes the same value, by the same purity that makes the sweep convergent), resumable and crash-proof. There is no new state to maintain and no process to resume.

**If the embedding model does not respond.** Generating an embedding is the same operation in everything the Spawner executes here—regenerating a Catalog entry and signing a credential—so the failure has a single treatment and there is no new case. A model that is down would reactivate the trigger on every following query, turning the mechanism into a cycle of failed calls precisely under the condition in which something is already broken; that is why retries go with backoff (`embedding_regeneration_backoff`, §2.3), in the same spirit as the Task Broker's re-announcement (§3.1). If the failure persists beyond it, the Spawner escalates it itself: it opens a `hitl_contact_operator` with `data.kind = vector_space_unrepairable` and broadcasts a `notice` on `network_risk` (§3.15.7). The topic fits it literally—"something cannot be guaranteed and a human decision is needed"—: the network cannot repair the space in which it compares, it abstains meanwhile, and that stops progressing silently. The state in the meantime is the conservative one by construction—unmigrated nodes keep running with their old credential, and the network stays paused because unpausing is an operator decision that nothing automates.

**Why migration requires `frozen`: because of the active fleet, not the Catalog.** The Catalog repairs itself, paused or not, so it is not what needs the pause. The other fronts require it:

* **The restart per node.** Migrating the fleet is restarting everything that runs, in an order nobody coordinates.
* **Vacancy is immediate and total, not gradual.** At the instant of the `config_change`, every new `task.announced` is born in the new space (the Integration Sidecar reads the current value) while the whole fleet is still signed in the old one. There is no intermediate regime in which the network keeps working halfway: the Task Broker discards every proposal until a node is re-provisioned. Admitting work during that would be admitting it to be left vacant.
* **Budgets and noise.** The tasks left in flight at the moment of the change fall into the stale-space branch of §3.13.2, and each `task.failed` of that branch consumes `trace_budget_reopens`, which is monotonic (§3.15.1), and fires `trace_risk` precisely when the operator is the central piece of the procedure. Under `network_admission = frozen` the Task Broker does not award tasks of emergent topics (§3.18), so the Spawner never becomes assignee and that branch emits nothing during the window: the reopens all occur after the unpause, ordered and bounded, instead of exhausting budgets while the operator is busy migrating.

That population of stale tasks is moreover a **finite queue that drains itself**: since `embedding_model` belongs to the current class and the Integration Sidecar reads it on every emission (§3.6.1), every `task.announced` after the change is already born in the new space, so once those in flight are exhausted the branch stops firing without anyone turning it off.

That is why the schema co-constraint of §2.3 makes invalid the snapshot that edits `embedding_model` without leaving the network in `frozen`: the worst scenario is not discouraged; the Config Store rejects it (§3.14.4).

**What is NOT suspended during the window, and it is the precision easiest to lose.** What the pause stops is **the branches that consume neighborhood**, not the Spawner. Closing a human query no channel claims (§3.13.2) runs *before any branch*, does not depend on similarity and its topic is exempt from the pause (§3.18). Turning it off during the migration would leave those queries with no one to close them and their requesters waiting, exactly when the operator is the one driving the procedure. **The Spawner is not suspended during the window**; what is suspended is the decisions that need a valid vector space to be made.

**The operator's sequence**, entirely over paths that already exist:

1. A `config_change` (§3.14.4) that sets `network_admission = frozen` and the new `embedding_model` **in the same snapshot**. The co-constraint makes it mandatory, and the per-snapshot atomicity of §2.3 guarantees that nobody observes a mix: there is no window between pausing and changing.
2. Query the **census** form of `node_query` (§3.12.3) as many times as they want. It is exempt from the pause, generates and compares no embeddings, and returns the roster with the `model_id` of the credential of each running node—which is the progress indicator, and not the `model_id` the Catalog carries per entry, which measures the sweep and converges on its own. The migration is finished when the whole roster is under the new model.
3. A `config_change` back to `open`.

**The limit worth knowing.** Nothing forces the pause to last as long as the migration does: the operator can unpause in step 3 before the roster is complete, and the network would admit work again with part of the fleet in the old space—massive vacancy and a storm of stale-space failures (§3.13.2). There is no structural defense against that, and adding one would require the network to judge whether its own migration is finished, which is the kind of decision the sweep's per-query predicate already refuses to tie to `network_admission`. What there is, is an operational affordance: the census says exactly how much is left, and §3.18 does not unpause on its own by timeout. Resuming can be a verified decision; nothing in the network forces it to be.

#### 3.13.8. Operator requests on the fleet

**The operator acts on the fleet through requests the Spawner executes:** instantiating a Catalog entry (`node_provision`), creating one (`node_create`), bringing one in from the Shared Catalog (`node_catalog_import`), and removing a node, vetoing an artifact or deleting its image (`node_retire`). These are the paths that start in a person instead of in something the network observed on its own—a vacancy that crossed its round threshold (§3.13.2), a `node_review` that asked for replacement (§3.13.4)—and what makes actionable the library that `node_catalog_query` lets the operator read (§3.13.3): the operator shapes the network without waiting for each thing to show up on its own.

**What they share.** The Operator Channel emits `task.announced` on the request's topic (§3.7.7) with `data.target_topic` and the operator proof (§3.14.2)—the same shape with which it emits `node_catalog_publish` (§3.13.6). The Spawner that owns `data.target_topic` selects itself and the Task Broker assigns without similarity (§3.7.5, step 4). Bringing in a bundle carries no `data.target_topic`, and any instance claims it (§3.13.6). The channel carries and derives nothing (§3.17.1).

* **Validation is layered, all of it over data that comes in the event or in what the request names**, and any layer failing is a `task.failed` with its `failure_reason` and its `failure_code` (§3.3) that the operator sees through their channel—the same division with which §3.14.4 treats a configuration edit. Every request is rejected if the proof does not verify (§3.14.2), and the one that carries `data.target_topic`, if it is not the `topic` of the entry or node it names: a mismatch there sends the request to a partition that does not own the niche. No layer is an inspection of the artifact: all of them are predicates over identifiers.
* **Each request carries its own duplicate check**, the one §3.6.4 requires of every non-repeatable external action: a write-if-absent in the Catalog (§3.13.3), named by the request's `TaskID`. The first one wins and stamps the outcome; a duplicated delivery or a re-announcement after an expired lease (§3.1) reads the stamped outcome and returns it instead of executing again. Naming it by the request and not by the niche is what lets two different requests on the same entry through.
* **They remain available with admission paused**, like the rest of the table of §3.7.7, where the criterion is.

**Instantiating: `node_provision`.** The request names a Catalog entry—active or archived—its `topic` and its `artifact_ref`, and carries the egress slot association the person chooses (§3.16.3) and, for a channel, that of its ingress slots, with the sharing mark when the entry already has another channel (§3.16.7). The `capability_text` and the `similarity_floor` the node competes with come from the entry—the floor, from its history (§3.13.3)—so the request carries no magnitude chosen by the person. A blueprint archived for `no_demand` whose demand the operator knows is coming back is brought up when the operator decides, instead of when a vacancy rediscovers it.

* **Validation.** Besides the common ones, the Spawner rejects if the entry does not exist, if the request's `artifact_ref` is not the entry's—two entries that differ only in the floor share it (§3.13.2), so naming the entry does not pin it down—if its `artifact_ref` is vetoed—with the veto as the reason—if its Node Runtime Interface version falls outside `runtime_interface_range` (§2.3), or if any slot of its `egress_required` is not associated: it has no entry that reaches it, it has several and neither the request nor a stamped network decision (§3.16.5) chooses one, only the operator associates it and the request does not name it (§3.16.3), or the request associates it with an entry that does not implement it or that is `topics` and does not include the request's `topic`. The list of a `topics` entry is written only by the registry, so a request does not extend it. It also rejects if any slot of its `ingress_required` is not associated by the request with an entry that implements its interface, or if the entry already has another channel and the request does not meet the conditions for sharing it (§3.16.7). The association the request brings governs for that node and is not stamped as a network decision. When it names a `provision` entry, the proof carries it in the clear next to the `artifact_ref`, and the Spawner stamps it in the grant (§3.16.3).
* **It is the only path through which a `hitl_contact_*` channel is brought up**, apart from the two of the deployment. The request's proof carries in the clear the `audience`—which comes from the `topic`—and the `artifact_ref`; the Spawner embeds it in the channel's credential, and the Task Broker verifies it before admitting it (§3.14.2). The person deploys a channel they have already seen, because creating and bringing in end in the registration. The request does not require an `egress_registry` rule to name the audience (§3.5.3): a channel brought up before its rule waits without receiving queries, and in the reverse order the queries of that audience close with no answer until the channel exists (§3.13.2). A `hitl_contact_operator` channel brought up this way answers queries and does not sign acts (§3.14.2).
* **A channel's request can grant it an entry topic**, `entry_topic`: the right to open traces on that topic (§3.5.1). The proof carries it in the clear next to the `audience` and the `artifact_ref`, and that audience is the one the traces the channel opens inherit (§3.5.3): the population the entry reaches, whether a person or a non-human emitter is behind the channel. It is granted to any audience except `operator`. Whoever opens a trace is its owner in round 1 of the origin audience (§3.5.2), and in `operator` that round is also that of the structural queries about the trace, whose precedence stays with the deployment's channel. The Spawner rejects the request if it grants `entry_topic` to `operator`, if the topic belongs to the `hitl_contact_*` family or is a fixed-convention one with a structural claimant (§3.7.7), or if the blueprint declares no ingress slot: a channel opens traces only from the messages it admits (§3.16.7).
* **The preconditions are those of reviving and the outcome is different.** On a vacancy, a blueprint out of range is treated as if it did not exist and the search goes on, and an unassociated slot opens the operator query of §3.16.5 with the niche vacant meanwhile (§3.13.2). A request names an entry, so there is nowhere left to keep searching, and whoever would decide that query is the person waiting for the outcome: the request closes with the reason and the query of §3.16.5 is not opened.
* **No restriction by `state` and no neighborhood check.** Instantiating an entry that already has running instances is a decision about the fleet, and a redundancy check would be a new Spawner decision (§3.13.1). What validates surplus coverage is competition (§3.13.3): if the niche is already served, the instantiated node loses the auctions and the lifecycle archives it through the usage axis (§3.13.4). The topic exists because the entry exists, so there is no minting to decide either (§3.7.6), and the node's cold start is covered by the generic treatment of any recent provisioning (§3.12.1, §3.13.5).
* **The embedding is generated on the spot, as in every provisioning.** The request names the entry, so there is no neighborhood search for the invariant of §3.13.3 to gate: the Spawner generates the embedding from the `capability_text` under the current `embedding_model` and signs it when provisioning (§3.14.2), whatever `model_id` the entry has stored—the same act with which it imports a blueprint (§3.13.6) and with which it revives an archived one while the Catalog converges (§3.13.7). If generation keeps failing beyond `embedding_regeneration_backoff` (§2.3), the request closes with `task.failed` and that reason: the path that escalates a persistent model failure runs on its own and does not need the requester held (§3.13.7).
* **Without room, it evicts like vacancy, and without a free node to evict it closes with the reason** (§3.13.10). An entry whose reservation does not fit even with the fleet empty is rejected with that reason.
* **It names a single entry.** Instantiating a bundle is as many `node_provision` requests as there are members, which the Operator Channel emits when expanding it (§3.17.1), and each one is validated, provisioned and closed on its own.
* **The close identifies the node.** The `task.completed` carries `data.node_id`, the same identifier with which `node_review` and `node_query` name a specific node (§3.12.3). With it the operator verifies that the node actually runs, through the point form of `node_query` against the Active Node Registry, which is the store that answers what is deployed (§3.11).

**Creating: `node_create`.** The request carries in `target_topic` the topic the node will claim, a text by the operator describing what they want, and optionally the `artifact_ref` of an existing entry as a base. The Spawner carries it to the Forge without interpreting it (§3.13.1), through the same egress (§6.2) as a vacancy request and with the input of §6.3 that corresponds to it. Here there are no re-announcements to pace the poll (§6.4): the Spawner holds the request under its lease (§3.1) while the derivation runs, and stores the handle in the request's key, so if the lease expires the instance that picks it up finds the derivation in progress instead of requesting another. The operator names `data.target_topic` because the Spawner cannot choose it: choosing a topic from a text is interpreting it.

* **Creating ends in the act of registration, never in a provisioning.** What is delivered is a Catalog entry the Forge registered (§6.5), and the `task.completed` returns its reference. Deploying it is a separate `node_provision`, so the operator sees the artifact before it runs. With a base, the base is an input to the derivation and not a node under review: the result does not enter the tree of §3.13.5 and is not bounded by the derivation bound of §3.13.4, and if it is deployed it competes with the base's node like any pair of neighbors (§3.13.4).
* **The other outcomes of the poll.** A blocked request relays its question to the operator (§6.4), who is the one who asked; a rejected one closes `task.failed` with the reason as it arrived. A network without a Forge rejects the request: it has no one to address it to (§6.1).
* **A new topic is registered on close.** If `data.target_topic` is not in the Topic Registry, the Spawner registers it with the operator's text as source text (§3.7.6), with no neighborhood check: the operator named the topic, not a node. If the entry is never deployed, the registration is left orphaned and is harmless, by the same argument as §6.3. A topic of the `hitl_contact_*` family is not registered (§3.5.3), and the request validates only the shape of its name: its audience may not yet have a rule naming it (instantiating, above). The same holds when bringing in.

**Bringing in: `node_catalog_import`.** The request names a Shared Catalog entry and its `topic`, or a bundle (§3.13.6), and the Spawner executes the act of registration of an import (§3.13.3, §3.13.6): the tenant's Admission Scanner analyzes the image—a `fail` verdict closes the request with that reason and is reported to the Shared Catalog—the entry ends up with `provenance` = `imported` or `purchased` and its acquisition cost, and a bundle verifies its relative closure. The acquisition is the operation of §3.13.6 with `purpose` = `operator_request`, under the same `rules`; if it is not executed, the request closes with the reason the rule or the catalog's rejection names. It is rejected if the entry is revoked in the Shared Catalog, if it was promoted without an `artifact_ref`—there is nothing to deploy—or if its `artifact_ref` is vetoed on the network. The interface range and the egress slots are not evaluated here but when provisioning (§3.13.3), and the `task.completed` returns the reference to the local entry or bundle: deploying it is `node_provision`, one per member if it is a bundle (§3.17.1).

**Removing and vetoing: `node_retire`.** One topic with a `data.kind`:

* **`remove`**—names a `node_id` and its `topic`. The Spawner archives it with the write order of §3.13.3 and `trigger_reason` = `operator_removal`, a value the Network Monitor does not emit (§3.12.3). It requests the metrics snapshot through the point form of `node_query` (§3.12.3); if it does not arrive, it archives without it, which is the cheap half-failure of §3.13.3. It is rejected if the `node_id` does not correspond to a blueprint it provisioned: a structural node is taken down through the deployment platform (§3.13.4). Archiving is reversible, so the network can bring the entry up again if a vacancy calls for it (§3.13.2); what prevents that is the veto. A `hitl_contact_*` channel is the exception: on that family the network brings up nothing on its own, and it comes back only through another `node_provision`.

  **Decommissioning a channel is a draining, because a channel holds roots no one else can close** (§3.5.4). The Spawner does not remove it: it provisions it again in drain mode, on the same entry and with what its Integration Sidecar stored (§3.14.1), whether it is running or down. Starting that way, the channel resumes its roots and opens no traces: a message that would open one it rejects before the emitter with `listener`, or leaves unconsumed at the origin with `source` (§3.16.7). It proposes only in round 1 of the traces it opened, and closes each root through the usual delivery. When it has none left, or when `channel_drain_timeout` (§2.3) expires, it closes those that remain with `task.failed` and a reason that names the decommissioning, and terminates; the other audiences' queries it had assigned go back to their audience through the lease (§3.1). The Spawner confirms the decommissioning in the Active Node Registry (§3.13.3) and holds the request under its lease meanwhile, as `node_create` holds a derivation. **The draining being marked in the provisioning and not in the stop signal is what separates a decommissioning from a restart:** a channel that restarts resumes its roots and keeps opening traces.

  The deployment's channels are not decommissioned through this request (§2.1). When shutting the network down, the deployment first drains the channels that open traces and stops the Operator Channel last, since it is the only one through which the network is still operated.
* **`veto`**—names an `artifact_ref` and its `topic`. The Spawner stamps the mark in the network's qualified row (§3.13.3) and archives with `operator_removal` every node of the network that runs that `artifact_ref`; a channel, by draining it (`remove`, above). The mark is a provisioning precondition (§3.13.1), so the vetoed entry stays out of reviving on vacancy (§3.13.2) and in blue/green (§3.13.4), of import from the Shared Catalog (§3.13.6) and of `node_provision`. It is local to the network, and it reaches one digest and only that one: a similar artifact the Forge derives later is another `artifact_ref`.
* **`unveto`**—names the `artifact_ref` and its `topic`, and deletes the mark. It brings up nothing: the entry becomes available again for what the network decides on its own and for a `node_provision`.
* **`purge`**—names an `artifact_ref` and its `topic`, and deletes its image from the image registry. It is the only thing that frees the space an artifact takes up (§3.13.3), and it reaches all the networks of the tenant, because the registry is a single one. **It is executed only on an artifact no network runs:**

  1. The Spawner stamps the deletion mark in the unqualified half of the entry, and from then on no Spawner of the tenant instantiates it (§3.13.1).
  2. It reads the presence mark of every network (§3.13.3). If any is current, it removes the deletion mark and rejects the request naming those networks.
  3. If there is none, it deletes the image.

  Provisioning writes its presence mark before reading the deletion one, and deletion writes its mark before reading the presence one. With both writes before both reads, any interleaving lets at least one see the other: a simultaneous provisioning and deletion never both go through.

  **An expired presence mark with the node running**—the Spawner of that partition stopped renewing—lets the deletion through. The node keeps running as long as its platform keeps the image it already pulled. When the Spawner renews again, it archives with `operator_removal` every instance of its partition whose entry has a deletion mark.

  **Before stamping anything, it is rejected on a seeded artifact**, the one some network has with `provenance` = `seeded`: without it, the deployment that brought it loses a piece that does not come back through any path of the network, and in a Forge it breaks the closure of its bundle (§6.1). What does come back is what was imported: bringing it in again registers it again and removes the mark (§3.13.3), without paying again (§3.13.6). What was derived comes back through a `node_create` that takes it as a base, which produces another `artifact_ref`.

  **The entry keeps everything it stores**: its acquisition cost, its floor history with its snapshots and its `author_id` remain valid, for the accounting of fleet spending and for the Forge that built it (§6.8). With the mark, vacancy and blue/green treat it as nonexistent (§3.13.2, §3.13.4), a `node_provision` is rejected with the deletion as the reason—also that of a member of a bundle being expanded—and its promotion to the Shared Catalog can only be metadata-only (§3.13.6).

The `task.completed` of `remove` and `veto` carries the archived `node_id` values. Removing or vetoing a node is independent of deploying what replaces it: without that request, the node already running and the new one compete like any pair of neighbors (§3.13.4).

#### 3.13.9. Splitting a heterogeneous niche

**Whether a niche is heterogeneous is decided by the strategy (§3.13.4)**, on the cost or duration dispersion (§3.12.2) and the rest of the vector that arrives in `node_review` (§3.12.3): high dispersion has several causes, and only one calls for splitting the niche. Splitting is an outcome of §3.13.5. This section fixes what governs every split, whatever the criterion that triggers it.

**The flow precondition.** Splitting divides among several nodes the flow with which a single one used to mature, so there are niches that do not tolerate the cut even if the cut is semantically correct. The Spawner does not trigger this outcome on a niche whose observed flow is not enough for **all the resulting candidates** to exceed the minimum flow the ratio `min_observations / evidence_staleness` fixes (§2.3, §3.12.1) during the trial. With the node under review still active and taking its share while the trial lasts, the practical threshold is on the order of (`m`+1) times that minimum and not `m`—triple for a cut in two, which is the small case. `usage_share` and `n_observations` already arrive in the payload (§3.12.3), so the precondition introduces no counter or parameter. If the niche is replicated (§3.12.2), the flow each candidate would see is `usage_share / k`, with `k` the active nodes of the niche.

**What is provisioned is `m` candidates that enter the normal procedure of §3.13.5, with no special retirement rule.** How to derive where to cut—and therefore into how many parts—is node-creation procedure (§6); what this section fixes is the contract on the result and its lifecycle. The node under review stays active meanwhile—not because the network treats it as privileged, but because nothing takes it out of competition until someone beats it, the same rule that governs any node. Each new candidate, like the node under review, is evaluated from then on by the tree of §3.13.5 like any other: the one that does not gather evidence expires through `max_candidate_maturation` and is archived without affecting the others (§3.13.5); the one that loses against a neighbor is archived by the normal rule; and the niche converges to the `m`+1 candidates that actually win tasks, without the network ever needing to know which of them is "the original".

**Validation is atomic, even though provisioning is not.** Before provisioning any of the `m` candidates, all `m` are evaluated against `runtime_interface_range` and the `egress_registry`, and if one fails none is provisioned. Provisioning itself cannot be atomic, and there is no need for it to be: if the orchestrator fails halfway, what remains is normal candidates competing, which the lifecycle treats like any other (further down). Two closures close the result before provisioning:

* **Topic closure, relative**—the same one §3.13.6 fixes when importing a bundle: a topic a candidate announces is claimed by another candidate, or is already covered on the destination network. Nothing guarantees that the candidates do not announce subtasks to one another.
* **Coverage closure, geometric**—candidates whose centroids shift toward the extremes of the niche can leave in the middle tasks that the node under review used to cover and that now match none of them. The derivation bound of §3.13.4 applied to each candidate bounds that gap but does not eliminate it: each one being at `≥ agent_similarity_floor` from the original node does not imply that the union covers it. The risk, however, is bounded by §3.13.4: a node's width is not fixed by its text but by its `similarity_floor`, which is not derived—every candidate is born with `task_similarity_floor`, the network's default value (§2.3). What the split moves is the **centroids**, and several balls of the same radius with shifted centers cover more than one, never less. The gap only appears when the `similarity_floor` of the node under review went down through a widening (§3.13.4) and the new candidates are born with the default floor, which is narrower—if the node under review is at the default value, the union covers its region by construction, and both numbers are available before splitting.

**No human approval is needed to execute the split.** It is one more proposal generator, not a judge (§3.13.4): if the cut comes out along the wrong axis, it self-corrects through measurement—the candidates stay dispersed or do not beat whoever is already covering the niche, they expire through `max_candidate_maturation` and the niche converges on its own, exactly like any blue/green candidate that loses.

**The brake.** The recursion—splitting what is already split—is bounded by the flow precondition itself, which runs out on its own: a niche's flow is a finite budget that splitting spends, so after a few successive cuts it is no longer enough for all the candidates to exceed the minimum, and the precondition stops holding. The limit is set by the observed demand, not by a declared quota.

#### 3.13.10. Fleet capacity

**The fleet runs on a finite capacity the platform declares (§3.14.1), and the network uses it as a cache of the Catalog:** what does not fit waits, or room is made for it. It is something other than the quota §3.13.2 rules out. A quota rejects real demand; capacity only decides what runs now, and what leaves comes back through vacancy, because archiving is reversible (§3.13.3).

**Each node runs within a memory reservation**, which is at once what the platform sets aside for it and the limit it imposes on it (§3.14.1). With both things in a single number, the sum of the fleet's reservations is a real bound and not an estimate.

* **`memory_required` is declared by whoever builds the blueprint**, in the unqualified half of the entry (§3.13.3), because it belongs to the artifact. An entry that does not declare it takes `default_memory_required` (§2.3).
* **It is not verified when registering or importing.** Declaring too little only harms the node itself, which dies by its limit and goes through the raise further down.
